## INTRODUCTION {#cuaderno.5.0}

Don’t let your life be barren. Be useful. Make yourself felt. Shine forth with the torch of your faith and your love.1 Apostolic concern is inseparable from the fight to achieve sanctity. With the vocation to Opus Dei, the zeal for apostolate is also a zeal for proselytism, that is, the desire that others should receive the same vocation. When one has something good, when a soul is happy, when it feels this interior joy and desires this happiness, it tries to pass on this good and this happiness to others.2 In the Work, apostolate and proselytism are interwoven. Our Father used to repeat that we have the imperative duty to do proselytism; the duty to transmit this divine gift, and to try to get other souls to serve Our Lord in Opus Dei.3 If we were not to do apostolate, if we were not to do proselytism, we would neither please God, nor would we be able to sanctify ourselves. Our Lord has called us to be saints and to sanctify.4 Our Founder has taught us that proselytism has to be based on a life of prayer and sacrifice. It is carried out in the midst of the world, through one’s professional work and in one’s own surroundings. It is open to all souls. Its source is in one’s relationships of friendship and confidence, and it calls for the exercise of all the Christian virtues. Our vocation leads us constantly to renew our proselytistic zeal.

1 The Way, 1 2 From our Father, Crónica, February 1964, p.7 3 From our Father, Crónica, February 1964, p.7 4 From our Father, Crónica, May 1963, p.6 8 The Apostolic Mission

This book gathers together a series of editorials from the internal publications. Reading it will help us to nurture this desire for souls. Let us always keep in mind those words of our Father: No son of God in his Opus Dei can remain at peace if he does not feel continually, like the beating of his heart, a hunger for proselytism.5 This is what we ask Our Lady, Mother of God and our Mother. Regina Apostolorum, ora pro nobis!

5 From our Father, Crónica, March 1956, p.36

## THE APOSTOLIC MISSION {#cuaderno.5.1}

As thou didst send me into the world, so I have sent them into the world.1 Apostle means precisely this: one sent. Applied to Christians it means an envoy of Christ. An apostle of Our Lord, then, acts in his name. Everything that one does can then be thought of as done by him who sends us: He who receives you receives me, and he who receives me receives him who sent me.2 With respect to Jesus, we have to be what Jesus was to the Father, because our apostolic mission – the mission of Christians – is a continuation of his mission. My food, Our Lord says, is to do the will of him who sent me.3 And this has to be our food too, to do the will of Jesus who has called us – ut eatis,4 that you go – to send us to all peoples.

An imperative command of Christ Bear in mind, son, that you are not just a soul who has joined other souls in order to do a good thing. That is a lot, but it’s still little. You are the Apostle who is carrying out an imperative command from Christ.5

1 Jn 17:18 2 Mt 10:40 3 Jn 4:34 4 Jn 15:16 5 The Way, 942 10 The apostolic mission

That command consists in making Our Lord known wherever we have been sent: making him known through example, with our words, with our prayer, with the cross, as Christ made the Father known. No one knows the Father except the Son and anyone to whom the Son chooses to reveal him.6 We, apostles of Jesus, are the heralds of that revelation which is needed for souls to be saved. For how are they to believe in him of whom they have never heard? And how are they to hear without 7 a preacher? And how can men preach unless they are sent? Whatever may have been the way in which God chose to call us, each one’s vocation can be summed up in those words of Scripture: I heard the voice of the Lord saying, “Whom shall I send, and who will go for us?” Then I said, “Here am I! Send me”. And he said, “Go, and 8 say to this people...” In order to be apostles of Jesus, the doctrine of Our Lord has to become the flesh of our flesh, the light of our intellect, the eloquence of our word. Thus we shall be able to say with respect to Jesus what he said about his relationship with the Father: The word which you hear is not mine but the Father’s who sent me.9 Our example and our word have to manifest Christ: Your apostolate must be the overflow of your life ‘within’.10 We need interior life and formation to be able to say with Christ: I have given them the words which thou gavest me, and they have received them and known in truth that I came from thee; and they have believed that thou didst send me.11 Our mission is a participation in and a continuation of the mission

6 Mt 11:27 7 Rom 10:13-15 8 Is 6:8-9 9 Jn 14:24 10 The Way, 961 11 Jn 17:8

The apostolic mission 11

of Jesus, carried out in a specific place, among certain men, at a certain time. Through us Christ wants to make himself present for some specific souls, in a particular situation, in that place where he himself has sent us to make his name known.

In our own environment But... where have we been sent to? To the very place we were already. Our vocation hasn’t taken us out of our place, our environment, our work, our social relations. Our vocation has left us where it found us. What amazes you seems natural to me: that God has sought you out in the practice of your profession! That is how He sought the first, Peter and Andrew, James and John, beside their nets, and Matthew, sitting in the custom-house. And – wonder of wonders! – Paul, in his eagerness to destroy the seed of the Christians.12 It was through social relations and with their work that the Twelve began their work. Matthew brings his friends – publicans and sinners – together round Our Lord, inviting them to the feast with which he was celebrating his calling as an Apostle. And John and Andrew speak with their brothers and friends and professional colleagues, and so Peter comes to Jesus, and so do James and Philip and Nathanael... Paul, who did manual work, made use of it to practise the mission he had received. Arriving at Corinth, he meets Aquila and Priscilla, and because he was of the same trade he stayed with them, and they worked, for by trade they were tentmakers.13 And one of the first Christian communities flourishes in that house: Aquila and Priscilla, together with the church in their house, send you hearty greetings in the Lord.14

12 The Way, 799 13 Acts 18:3 14 1 Cor 16:19 12 The apostolic mission

Thus the first of the faithful found their vocation as Christians – as saints and apostles – in the practice of their human professions. And in the practice of those professions they went to carry out the new mission they had received. “We do not stop going to the forum, the market, the baths, the stores, the offices, the inns, and your fairs. We do not cease our relationships. We live together with you in this world. We sail with you. We go to the army. We till the land and we trade with its fruits. We sell the produce of our work and toil to the people.”15 And they took advantage of those duties to instil the fire of Christ in their colleagues and in their surroundings. Our vocation to sanctity and to the apostolate came to meet us too – as with the first Christians – in our work, when we were beginning our professional training, or after years of practising our profession. And right there is where we have been sent because our apostolate “is to be done not only ‘in the world’ but also, as originating ‘from the world’ and, consequently through those professions, occupations, and jobs and in the places and circumstances corresponding to this secular condition.”16

A new way of being Our calling has only changed the way we are present in our surroundings. “When someone is sent it means that in some way he is the agent of one who sent him... It also implies some reference to the place where he was sent, whether because he had never been there before or because he begins to be there in a different way.”17 So if a state nominates as its ambassador to another government a person who is already in that other country, from that moment on that person acquires a new relationship with respect to the nation that has sent him, and also

15 Tertullian, Apologia 42, 1-3 16 Pius XII, Motu proprio Primo feliciter, 12 March 1948 17 St Thomas, S. Th., I, q.43, a.1, c The apostolic mission 13

with respect to the country where he already was. From the moment of his appointment he is the representative of his government, its envoy, and he must live and speak as such. He stays where he was, but in a different way, identifying himself with the character and the interests of the state he represents. Therefore, the fact that we have been sent to the environment of our professional work implicitly brings with it a new attitude, a different way of being there, which is that of being envoys of Jesus Christ as his apostles. And so we cannot behave in our work as we did before. Now we have to act as apostles, showing Christ, revealing him to the people with whom we work. Our vocation respects our professional work and leaves it intact. But it absorbs our profession, giving it another meaning and a higher purpose and does not just give us an apostolic task for our free time. It is a new way, an apostolic way, of working and of dealing with our professional colleagues. If we did not do this we would fail radically in the mission we have received. Now that you have given yourself to him, ask him for a new life, a ‘seal’, to guarantee that your mission as a man of God is authentic.18 Let it be like our credentials. All this is done in a natural way for naturalness is a fundamental characteristic of our work. To be a missionary, with a mission, and not to call yourself a missionary.19 But naturalness is not cowardice, nor human respect nor a lack of interest in souls. Normally we do not have any reason to show our vocation, but a person in Opus Dei ought to be like a burning coal who spreads fire wherever he is, or who at least raises the spiritual temperature of those around him, leading them to live an intense Christian life.20

18 The Way, 909 19 The Way, 848 20 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1930 14 The apostolic mission

Sanctifying one’s work That new way of being in our work environment, that seal which has to give genuineness to the mission we have, in the first place demands supernatural outlook since the light of faith is what teaches us that professional work acquires its full meaning and a more complete significance when it is wholly directed to the salvation of souls.21 For us then, as a consequence of the mission received, our professional work is wholly and always a means of sanctification and apostolate.22 It couldn’t be any other way as our professional work fills the greater number of hours of our day. But, besides, it is of the essence of our particular vocation that our profession is promoted to being an instrument of the supernatural action of God in souls and in society. Work itself has to have an apostolic dimension, doing tasks well, finishing them, with supernatural discernment, so that each one may convert his own job into a fruitful instrument of apostolate.23 The formation that the Work gives to its members has this purpose: it gives them a supernatural outlook in the fulfilment of their apostolic mission, since they know that in practising their professional, social, economic duties, they ought to obtain, above all, their own sanctity and that of those who work and live with them.24 This is the programme our Founder gives us when he says that a particular characteristic of the spirituality of Opus Dei is that we each have to sanctify our job, our ordinary work; we have to sanctify ourselves in our job; and we have to sanctify others with our job,25 with the supernatural ambition of putting Christ at the top of all human activities.26

21 From our Father 22 From our Father 23 From our Father 24 From our Father 25 Alone with God, 55 26 From our Father, Letter 19 March 1954

The apostolic mission 15

Sanctifying work means making work itself holy. Both the thing that is done and the intention with which we do it must be directed to God. There are jobs, the internal ones for example, which are directed by their very nature to extending the Kingdom of God without our having to do anything more than do them. But the same doesn’t happen in our ordinary professional work, so we must seek its sanctification and direct it to the building up the kingdom of God. In some cases it will be easy to see how this is possible, such as in philosophy, literature, sociology, politics. In others it will be more difficult, but it is always possible. And doing it is the personal task of each one, on his own responsibility, the responsibility that he has before God who has sent him there. Each one has to sanctify himself in his work. In that ordinary job he has to find a source of personal sanctity. It has to be filled with love for God. It has to be prayer. It has to be lived with a spirit of mortification and penance, with financial responsibility. Each one has to find the way of putting all the virtues into practice when he carries out his profession: faith, hope and charity; justice, temperance, fortitude, prudence. And in a very special way, zeal for souls: because, for us, sanctity and apostolate are two aspects of the same end, and therefore are inseparable. Our professional work has to be so directed to the supernatural purpose of our mission in the world that our Father tells us the professional vocation that is not subordinated to the divine vocation is like a tree that gives leaves but no fruit. If your profession, if the fulfilment of the human things we have around us does not help us win vocations, bring souls closer to God, it is because we are going very wrong, very wrong. What would you say of a family that didn’t want 27 to have children? We have been sent by Jesus Christ into the world without taking us out of it, but with a new way of being in it, precisely in order to make

27 From our Father, Get-together, 1 June 1961 16 The apostolic mission

human things divine. The members of Opus Dei are not forbidden to exercise any honest profession. On the contrary, they ought to elevate and sanctify all the professions, converting them into instruments of sanctity for themselves and others, and into an occasion of apostolate.28 We want to work everywhere in order to bring all things to God. And that is also why with a right intention the members of the Work seek in an honest manner, with personal effort, the greatest possible prestige among their colleagues in study or at work, whether this be intellectual or not, since this prestige will permit them to carry out an effective apostolate through their own profession.29 That prestige gives us a higher place among those who surround us and consequently it gives us authority to influence others by what we do and say. And this is precisely our ‘bait’ as a ‘fisher of men’.30 A man, professionally well prepared, commands respect in his environment at work. He sets the tone; he attracts the esteem of others. His actions and his words find a special resonance: he is placed on a pedestal. If he is an apostolic soul, he will use this pedestal to place Christ aloft in order to extend the light and warmth of God over those who surround him. If he is not, if he does not act as an envoy there, as one who represents Our Lord and has the mission of leading souls to Him, he takes possession of the pedestal for himself and uses it to swell his vanity. He becomes his own envoy, the spokesman of his own personal excellence. He is the herald of his talents, the ambassador of his own ego. Without apostolic vibration, prestige is the ruin of humility. Hence the subordination of our work, in all its aspects, and from all points of view, to our vocation. And that subordination is so essential

28 From our Father, Letter 15 October 1948 29 From our Father 30 The Way, 372

The apostolic mission 17

that when a work is not useful for our apostolic mission, it is not good for anything. That is why our Father told us: It is true that our professional vocation is a part of our divine vocation, but only in so far as it is a means for our apostolate and for our sanctification: to serve God, to make us saints, and to sanctify others. If at some moment our professional vocation places obstacles, then it spoils everything because it has ceased to be a means. Because if it is not the baited hook to catch fish, I am not interested in it and it is not a part of the divine vocation, for it will no longer be a professional vocation but a diabolical one. In so far as it is a means to sanctify us and to sanctify others, our professional vocation is a part of our divine vocation. I become very sad when I see one of my sons and he only tells me his professional accomplishments, and that he has obtained this and the other. If all his activity does not help him fish souls, then it is very bad.31

The first target of the apostolate: our colleagues at work God has given us all the means to make Him present in the environment of our profession, to carry out effectively the mission we have received. For nor do men light a lamp and put it under a bushel, but on a stand, and it gives light to all in the house.32 The light is the life of Christ in our life, his doctrine in our intellect, his strength in our words. The lamp-stand is precisely our work, our professional prestige, the position we hold. We cannot bury the light we have received, thus impeding it from giving light to all in the house. Be men and women of the world, but do not be worldly men and women.33 And we would be worldly if the things of the world did not have a supernatural end for us; if we did not put them at the service of

31 From our Father, Get-together, 1 June 1961 32 Mt 5:15 33 The Way, 939 18 The apostolic mission

our mission; if we did not feel like envoys of God in the world; if all our activities were not primarily and essentially directed to an apostolic purpose. The specific apostolate of the members of Opus Dei is to sanctify their professional work and direct it towards the salvation of souls, principally the souls of their professional colleagues.34 This is our specific goal, which means that he who does not use his profession for that purpose would completely devalue the mission he has received, even though he may try to compensate for this with other apostolic activities after finishing his day’s work. We should, therefore, have a definite zeal for the souls around us, a desire to lead them to God. Do as Peter and John – our Father advises us

– When you go to the prayer, keep those friends and acquaintances very much in mind, and then with your example, tell them: respice in 35 They have to be able to see the life of Christ in our nos! look at us! life. They have to know God through us. To prayer, mortification, and example we must add the words, apostolate of friendship and confidence, apostolic words, full of enthusiasm, that give doctrine and persuade and enkindle. ‘Nonne cor nostrum ardens erat in nobis, dum loqueretur in via? Did our hearts not burn within us as he talked to us on the road?’ If you are an apostle, these words of the disciples of Emmaus should rise spontaneously to the lips of your professional companions when they meet you along the ways of their lives.36 Those companions have the right to expect it of us, because we have been sent to their side for that reason. And although they do not know it now, they will know some day that God had given us this specific mission for nothing is covered that will not be revealed.37 If

34 From our Father 35 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934 36 The Way, 917 37 Mt 10:26

The apostolic mission 19

there comes a moment in which the circumstances of work or the demands of our vocation lead us to some other place, perhaps those souls with whom we have lived for a time will no longer have anyone able to lead them to God, no one who has that mission and the means to fulfil it. This is so fundamental that it obliges us throughout our life and in whatever place we may be. You might ask me: Father, what about when I’m eighty? The same then: in dealing with your brothers, with your professional colleagues, with your friends.38 We must awaken the light and the warmth of an authentic Christian life in everyone. Do not let any soul who comes close to us be lost with respect to the apostolate and, as far as possible, to a vocation.39 These words of the priestly prayer of Jesus, a short time before finishing his mission on earth, ought to be our words too at the end of the day. I have manifested thy name to the men whom thou gavest me 40 to these particular men to whom Our Lord has sent out of the world, us. Now they know that everything that thou hast given me is from thee; for I have given them the words which thou gavest me, and they have received them and know in truth that I came from thee; and they have believed that thou didst send me.41 The faithful fulfilment of our mission provides our best credentials. Sanctify them in the truth; thy word is truth. As thou didst send me into the world, so I have sent them into the world. And for their sake I consecrate myself, that they also may be consecrated in truth.42 From our apostolate come new apostles, from our dedication, new dedication, that in turn will produce other new vocations in geometric progression.

38 From our Father, Get-together, 1 June 1961 39 From our Father 40 Jn 17:6 41 Jn 17:7-8 42 Jn 17:17-19 20 The apostolic mission

What a lot of spiritual calories you need! And what a tremendous responsibility if you let yourself grow cold! And – I don’t even want to 43 think of it – what a terrible crime if you were to give bad example! Our Lord finished his priestly prayer in this way: Father, I desire that they also, whom thou hast given me, may be with me where I am, to behold my glory which thou hast given me in thy love for me before the foundation of the world. O righteous Father, the world has not known thee, but I have known thee; and these know that thou hast sent me. I made known to them thy name, and I will make it known, that the love with which thou hast loved me may be in them, and I in them.44

Re-appraising professional work We have been sent by Jesus Christ, just as Jesus Christ, the Word, the Son of God, was sent by the Father. Our mission, a continuation of Christ’s mission, is a divine one in so far as it is God who has sent us to sanctify specially those who are our companions at work, in our own environment, wherever we are. This is an essential aspect of our way. Thus we should ask ourselves frequently: since I have been here, in this place and in this work, among these people, what change has there been in those around me? How am I influencing their lives? If after a reasonable time there were no change, it would be a clear symptom of salt that has lost its taste. When we do not set those around us on fire with the love of God it is because we ourselves have died out. We are dead. Inside we are a corpse.45 It is true that on occasions God doesn’t want us to see the fruits of our apostolate and that others harvest those fruits. But normally if there is an abundant sowing there will also be an abundant harvest. So, every

43 The Way, 944 44 Jn 17:24-26 45 From our Father The apostolic mission 21

sound tree bears good fruit, but the bad tree bears evil fruit. A sound tree cannot bear evil fruit, nor can a bad tree bear good fruit. Every tree that does not bear good fruit is cut down and thrown into the fire. Thus you will know them by their fruits.46 Periodically, in the light of these words, we need a thorough reappraisal on the way we do our work, on our intention, on the responsibility with which we fulfil the mission we have received. This enables us to begin with new vigour, so as to control our environment always and really lead the souls that surround us, our work and the environment itself, to God. Let it never be that these souls, work and environment separate us from God. And all this, so as to fulfil our mission effectively, to give reason to our presence in the particular part of the world where we are, where Jesus Christ has sent us. Non vos me elegistis; sed ego elegi vos et posui vos, ut eatis et fructum afferatis, et fructus vester maneat.47 You did not choose me, but I chose you, and appointed you that you should go and bear fruit, and that your fruit should abide. May our Holy Mother Mary, Regina Apostolorum, always help us to hear, in the midst of our professional work, the imperative voice of the One who sends us: ‘Proclaim the Good News... I shall be with you...’ It is Jesus who has said this... and he has said it to you.48

46 Mt 7:17-20 47 Jn 15:16 48 The Way, 904

## TO ALL ROADS {#cuaderno.5.2}

Some wandered in desert wastes, finding no way to a city to dwell in; hungry and thirsty, their souls fainted within them. Then they cried to the Lord in their trouble, and he delivered them from their distress; he led them by a straight way, till they reached a city to dwell in.1 Sacred Scripture shows us God’s continuous Providence in many ways. Nothing is hidden from his sight; he arranges everything with a Father’s affection in order that men find the path that leads to him. Our Lord’s heart is a heart of mercy that feels compassionate towards men and draws near to them. Our dedication to the service of souls is a manifestation of Our Lord’s mercy, not only towards us, but towards the whole of humanity.2 One day he passed by our side, as had happened before to John, Andrew and Matthew ..., and we followed him along the path of the Work. From then on our life acquired a new dimension. God’s grace gave us an apostolic soul, sending us to announce to other men, our equals, the wonders of the God who seeks us with such great predilection. Work and everyday affairs for us were turned into living prayer, naturalness, apostolic vibration, presence of God, with the hope of bringing this light to another heart, another mind.3

1 Ps 106:4-7 2 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1930 3 From our Father, Get-together, 3 January 1969, in Crónica, 1969, p.160

To all roads 23

Apostolic zeal is born of interior life The concern to help souls is a need, a direct consequence of our Christian life and an interior impulse. For us apostolate is not something unnatural, but rather something that gets inside us and is present in our breathing, in our professional work, in our sports, in our sight, in the depths of our heart. In accordance with our vocation, an apostolic impulse informs all that we do, just as the soul is in the 4 entire body! Apostolic zeal is active in this way because it stems from an intimate union with Our Lord towards which our contemplative vocation necessarily leads us. The effort to identify ourselves with Jesus Christ makes us come out of ourselves and become conscious of the spiritual needs of those around us; and it makes us feel the urgency to give ourselves with a constant apostolic desire. An apostolic impulse arises from being close to Christ, from trying to follow him and imitate him. We must really be other Christs and feel ourselves co-redeemers with the Good Shepherd who is so divine that he went about doing good (Acts 10:38). In this way our soul will have a superabundance of interior life, which will be reflected in peace and joy, to be given to others as well. It will attract people spiritually and perform real miracles.5 Only in this way will what our Father told us become a reality in us: all my children are Christ passing by in the world. In the four corners of the earth there are colleagues and friends of yours who are discovering Christ in your brothers and in you. And they in turn will bring Christ to other hearts, to other minds. You are Christ passing by along the middle of the street, but you must walk where he walked.6

4 From our Father, Get-together, 11 October 1970, in Crónica, 1970, pp.1174-75 5 From our Father 6 From our Father, Get-together, 3 January 1969, in Crónica, 1969, p.160 24 The apostolic mission

Effectiveness lies in faithfully following the path opened up by the footsteps of Christ. This demands renouncing everything personal; it is a way of joyful sacrifice, of daily dedication, of putting all one’s effort into fulfilling God’s will. St Peter reminds us, for to this you have been called, because Christ also suffered for you, leaving you an example, that you should follow in his steps.7 And our Father, reminding us of the need for abnegation in apostolic work, has repeated, the best preparation for proselytism is for you and me to be saints. Thus proselytism is done almost without our noticing it: almost without need of words, the warmth that you carry in your soul, that flame, the light of God that lights up your heart, will attract others. Have you seen what happens to iron when put into the fire? It becomes red-hot. It looks like a jewel, but it is only iron. This is the way we light up when we are faithful to our vocation. Our life is like a burning coal: we burn, we open up the way, we give off heat and light – the light of Christ.8 Then Our Lord works through our actions, and we give others the best of what we have: love of God, a rich treasure that we scatter to the winds, a divine joy, a calling that is a source of happiness for whoever listens attentively. As the Holy Spirit says, he who finds me finds life.9

Like the beating of our heart Each day in the Mass when I extend my hands over the Offering, over the bread and wine that will be converted, by virtue of the words of the Consecration, into the Body and Blood of Christ, I place all of you there, my daughters and sons: in electorum tuorum iubeas grege numerari, for Our Lord to count us among his chosen ones; not considering ourselves a select few but feeling like leaven and

7 1 Pet 2:21 8 From our Father 9 Prov 8:34

To all roads 25

enkindling others. We are the same as other men and women around us; but God’s choice of us has given us a new light and made us red-hot. My children, this is wonderful! We have won the supernatural lottery. When I think of this divine predilection, I almost feel ashamed; and sometimes (I am not afraid to have you know) I feel like crying, and the tears burn my eyes because I have failed to correspond to God’s grace as I should have. In considering these things we end up making the resolution always to act and to think out of Love. In the midst of our personal aberrations there comes an ever renewed and uncontainable desire to have others share in our happiness. Do you see, my children? Proselytism flows naturally; it is like the beating of the heart; it is a kind of hunger to spread this craziness of love of God to many other souls.10 We must have a holy daring to want others to come close to Christ and to want for them – the more of them, the better – the grace of a vocation to the Work, which Our Lord generously grants when we generously ask him to grant it to many souls. Goodness tends by its very nature to spread, my children. And if I enjoy this good, this love of my Father God, I must necessarily feel the effective desire to have others share in it. This is why I have so often told you that those people who have followed Christ, but do not try to win souls, give me the impression of having failed. Can you imagine the heart of a son of God that has died out, that is without warmth, without supernatural vibration and without the operative effectiveness that enkindles and burns with its presence alone? St Augustine says that whoever saves one soul has his own soul predestined. Think what will come of drawing other souls to the way of God! It will be our glory; we will earn a great, great little corner of Heaven. Furthermore, these vocations that Our Lord has willed to

10 From our Father 26 The apostolic mission

bring through our hands will act as the nut and locking-nut for us to be loyal to our way.11 The Love which brought us to the Work moves us never to forget the commitment that we made to Our Lord when he made us his apostles. It could not be otherwise; our correspondence must be complete. At Christ’s side, love is necessarily apostolic and it overflows into its surroundings. Otherwise it is not love, but an illusion, a dying fire, that neither sets ablaze what it touches nor gives off any heat.12 Our Father always taught that apostolic zeal is an exact, adequate and necessary manifestation of interior life. When we love Our Lord we feel the burden of souls. I never lack interest in anyone, my daughters and sons: I want to lead them all to God. I suffer for souls! At times I do not understand how my heart and my head can bear it. This is our spirit: to feel the lament of so many dry hearts who seem to tell us, hominem non habeo (John 5:7), I do not have anyone to give me a hand and bring me closer to the light and warmth of Christ. It is not possible to separate Christ’s being as God-Man from his role as Redeemer. The Word became flesh in order to save men and make them one with him. This is the reason for his coming into the world. We are other Christs, called to co-redeem, and neither can we split our life as children of God in his Work by separating it from our apostolic zeal.13

Instruments of God God positively makes use of each one of us to continue drawing souls closer to himself. And in these times when God’s enemy seems to win so many victories among men, dragging them after the stupid desires

11 From our Father 12 The Way, 412 13 From our Father, Letter 6 May 1945

To all roads 27

of their hearts,14 Our Lord pours himself out with a special Providence, giving us his strength to move, help and influence everybody around us: our relatives, colleagues at work, friends and acquaintances ... We count on the grace of our God. We want to make our own his constant concern for souls. God only expects us to use the means within our reach in order to bless this apostolic work abundantly. He has taught us this in a special way in a passage of the Gospel which is moving in its eloquence: the parable of those invited to the wedding. The kingdom of God may be likened to a king who gave a wedding banquet for his son. He dispatched his servants to summon the invited guests to the wedding, but they refused to come (Matt 22:2-3). That great King has come and invited many people to the banquet, our Father has commented. Here on earth all souls are called to the wedding of the great King, and they do not want to go; they reject the invitation and the hall is left empty. My children, I also understand these refusals. More than once I have spoken to you about those early moments of being alone, those years in which I practically repeated Our Lord’s words, Ecce prandium meum paravi, tauri mei et altilia occisa sunt, et omnia parata: venite ad nuptias (Matt 22:4). My banquet is ready. My oxen and my fat calves are killed and everything is ready: God has prepared for us a fullness of Love, without betrayal, without weariness, with all goodness and all beauty. Some ignored the invitation and went their way, one to his farm, to his egoisms, to his comfort, another to his business (Matt 22:5): to their profession, which they do not know how to make a divine way on earth.15

14 Cf Jer 9:14 15 From our Father, Meditation The King’s banquet, 13 October 1963, in While he spoke to us on the way, pp. 204-205 28 The apostolic mission

Let us ask God to have mercy on all men, to return again and again, with the zeal of this good King, to search for them and to make them fall in love. But the things of God always go ahead, even though we may be unwilling. Then he said to his servants: the banquet is ready, but those who were invited were unfit to come. That is why you must go out into the by-roads and invite to the wedding anyone you come upon (Matt 22:8-9). Does that not move you, my children? God calls everyone. You and I belong to this crowd, to this group that he wanted to search out in all the crossroads of the world. And we have come like these men of the parable: the lame, the blind, the deaf.16 The servants went out into the by-roads and rounded up everyone they met, bad as well as good. This filled the wedding hall with banqueters.17 It is also our obligation to act as these servants did; ours is a duty of proselytism so that everyone may vibrate and not remain distant from the divine paths. Wherever there are persons capable of serving God, there we must be present to lead them to Christ. We must set this invitation of the Great King within their reach. Everything is ready, come to the banquet. It is our duty to call many souls to dedicate themselves to work in the service of God. The spirit of our Work is a universal spirit, open to all. I have very often repeated that our place is there: in the midst of the world, at the crossroads of human paths. No closing doors and windows! The more people we are in contact with, the better. It is good for us members of Opus Dei to deal with a lot of people because we are interested in all souls. Around 1934 I wrote, We are an intravenous injection in the bloodstream of society. Our Lord has chosen us as messengers of his

16 From our Father, Meditation, 17 October 1965 17 Mt 22:10

To all roads 29

doctrine of salvation; so, my children must be in the most diverse places; and there they ought to be eminently apostolic, with the absolute certainty that, in working for him, there is no action of ours, no matter how small it may seem, that is not effective. In his Providence, God wants to make men share in his Redemption, and with a particular vocation he has called us in a special way to be coredeemers. My children, with this light of your life, of your work, of your perseverance, you will draw many souls to God, to the warmth of this Work, our most beautiful Mother.18 Proselytism is something so co-natural to the spirit of the Work that we began to be apostolically concerned about our friends and acquaintances even before asking for the admission. Perhaps we were moved by that invitation that our Father included in a point of The Way: Through the world still echoes that divine cry: ‘I have come to bring fire to the earth, and how I wish it were blazing already!’ And you see: it has nearly all died out ... 19 Will you not help to spread the blaze? These words are filled with the vibration and force of a divine commitment which we can never forget. On the contrary, having said yes to God, and placed our entire life in his service, we have lit an unquenchable fire, a flame fed daily by the love of God that tends to spread like the swift heralding of those servants of the parable. Ite ad exitus viarum, et quocumque inveneritis, vocate ad nuptias (Matt 22:9). Go out to all the roads! Go out! I have repeated this so often to you: it is not our way to stay cooped up at home, but to go out to all roads, seeking souls where they are, and then bring them to God, wounded with love, with understanding, and with dedication, or at

18 From our Father 19 The Way, 801 30 The apostolic mission

least with a desire for dedication.20 We have to spread out like a fan. We must open up like a hand, and each have a group of souls, easy ones and hard ones, dangling from each finger ... and pull! And each one should not be one, but ten.21 Dedication for dedication. Whoever has received the calling spreads the invitation. And once the silence is broken, the first voice is carried in ever wider circles which covers the great diversity of human paths. My children, I sometimes think that when you begin to work with souls, they are like cherries. You pull on one, and you get two. You take another one, and you get four or five more. People of every condition, of any personal, professional or social situation, because we are interested in everyone. Certainly those that God sends to his Work are a selection, but they are not to be enclosed but rather spread around, because we must do good to all mankind.22

Go out in search of souls As a fire raging in a forest, as a flame setting the mountains ablaze.23 Proselytism, the desire to bring many souls to Opus Dei, is shown in each one of us with the gentle and constant impetus of an unquenchable fire. This overwhelming zeal, which never ceases when we live our dedication faithfully, successfully finds the occasion to show this urgency in a constant apostolate in the ordinary situations of each day. It is the logical counterpoint, the adequate response, to the urgency that Our Lord shows in the parable of the wedding feast: exi in vias et saepes, this time it is St Luke who tells us, et compelle intrare, ut impleatur domus mea (Luke 14:23). Go out to the highways and

20 From our Father 21 From our Father, Crónica, June 1964, p.12 22 From our Father 23 Ps 82:15

To all roads 31

by-ways and push those whom you find to come and fill my house. Force them to come in; push them in; bring them to me. All this is the meaning of that compelle intrare of the Gospel. It is perfectly compatible with the most delicate respect for the freedom of souls, and it is absolutely contrary to passivity, to laziness or human respect. My children, you must draw closer to your friends and acquaintances, and call them in the name of God: compelle intrare! If no one had called you, probably you would not be here now serving God in the Work. You must tell many people that Christ also seeks them as he sought the first Twelve, as he sought out the Samaritan woman, and Zaccheus and the paralytic. Each one in his own place of work, wherever he is, should feel the need to reach many people so that the banquet hall be filled. I ask all my children to make a very intimate examination in their personal prayer to see what they have done until now to bring souls to Opus Dei. This examination will finish with a very firm resolution: to do whatever is in their power to obtain many vocations, because we need them. Thank God, we do not lack vocations, even in the midst of the painful circumstances that presently affect so many Christians and so many environments. But precisely because of this we need more hands; the Church needs them; God wants them in his service. You must all feel a concern for this sowing. We cannot stay indoors. God did not want his Work to be a residence or a corporate work. He has brought it forth to sanctify souls. We must necessarily go out to seek them, like the servants in the parable, without waiting for them to come to us. To this end we need to deal with Jesus Christ in the Bread and in the Word, in the Host and in prayer. We must be in love, and just a little crazy. Many years ago now, they said that I was crazy. And a 32 The apostolic mission

priest friend of mine told me, they say you are crazy. And I answered him, pray that I be crazier still. It was true, my children. I was crazy, as you are too, with the same craziness; crazy with love for God.24 This love keeps us burning with a constant concern for others, which leads us to be their loyal friends, speaking to them and getting them to know Christ who is eager to enter their lives. We must move, break this crust of comfort that at times holds us back. We cannot be passive. We must get into the lives of others, as Christ got into your life and mine. If God had not acted this way, what would have become of me? Jesus Christ did not ask my permission to use me as an instrument. With divine mastery he came and stood in the midst of my soul: You do this and this for me. And I went to obey like a little donkey. He is the King of all creatures and we are his messengers.25

Effective desires Ineffective desires, half-loves, a tensile force that is lost, without an aim, are arrows that strike the air.26 But the yearning for souls present in Christ’s whole life, in his example and in his word, demands deeds from us: a specific correspondence, a generous answer to the compelle intrare. People become frightened. They are surprised at our eagerness to bring other souls to God to serve him. We know that it is Our Lord’s desire and a coherent manifestation of our love. I remember now what I said to my sons many years ago, and I repeat it to you, that they ought to be imprudent in the apostolate, not

24 From our Father 25 From our Father 26 Ps 90:6

To all roads 33

careful and cautious. Only old people should be prudent; and even though your brothers may become angry when they hear me say this, I am the only old man in Opus Dei. You must feel a great desire for proselytism, and get rid of any kind of fear. You must kill yourselves doing proselytism, because there lies our effectiveness.27 The deeds that God expects of us are example and word: the example of a Christian life, and at the same time, the word, the doctrine of Christ; for example alone would be of little value. We have a tongue to speak with, even imprudently. The more imprudent you are the better! Always, but especially now, I think of the time of St Paul and I remember his advice: argue, obsecra, increpa ... oportune, importune (cf 2 Tim 4:2). And St Paul, who has known and savoured the intense joy of being with God, goes out to preach sure of himself, and he is an apostle every minute, even in prison.28 There are no obstacles or difficulties that can diminish this interest. If the occasion to speak, to make God known, seemingly does not arise, any circumstance can create the opportunity. Our daily actions, and also this imprudence our Father speaks to us about, will always find the way to bring it about, so that apostolate be a constant concern in our lives. God must dwell in every soul around us. And we have received the divine command to carry out this task. All my children, therefore, must have a zeal for souls that leads them to transform their whole life into prayer and into effective desires to set others on fire with Love of God.29

27 From our Father 28 From our Father, Get-together, 25 August 1968, in Crónica, 1968, p.991 29 From our Father

## Bearing fruit {#cuaderno.5.3}


God spoke to us many times in Sacred Scripture about the need to bear fruit. I am the true vine, and my Father is the vinedresser. Every branch of mine that bears no fruit he takes away, and every branch that does bear fruit he prunes that it may bear more fruit.1 God wanted to single us out with a special gift, with the grace of a vocation to Opus Dei, which is a choice vine, wholly of pure seed,2 and he expects sweet and attractive fruit from us. Who plants a vineyard without eating any 3 of its fruit? Who tends a flock without getting some of the milk?

False excuses The Gospel tells us that on a certain occasion, Jesus, as he was leaving Bethany was hungry. And seeing in the distance a fig tree in leaf, he went to see if he could find anything on it. When he came to it he found nothing but leaves, for it was not the season for figs. And he said to it, “May no one ever eat fruit from you again”. And his disciples heard it.4 Our Lord also teaches this same lesson with the parable of the barren fig tree5 as if to insist that he expects fruit from us. And our

1 Jn 15:1-2 2 Jer 2:21 3 1 Cor 9:7 4 Mk 11:12-14 5 Cf Lk 13:6-7

Bearing fruit 35

Father would comment: It was not the season, but Our Lord came to eat the fruit. Christ Our Lord knew that it was not the time for figs. And yet, when he sees that the tree is barren, even though it has that appearance of fruitfulness and those leaves, he says, May no one ever eat fruit from you again (Mark 11:14). The fact is, my sons, that there is no excuse for anyone who does not make good use of his time. Our Lord’s words are very strong: Never again will you bear fruit. No one will eat fruit from you. Imagine how the disciples felt on hearing the Wisdom of God speaking in this way! But ... can this be possible? He curses that creature because it has no fruit! And yet, the reason is clear: there are no excuses for ceasing to bear fruit: it’s that I have not received the necessary formation; it’s that I was ill; it’s that I have not got the right qualities ... My sons, we have to be the tree that bears fruit. There are others who have nothing to offer when people go to them; they have only leaves. We have to bear fruit, fruit that will satisfy the hunger of souls; because we have all the supernatural means and sufficient doctrine; and because, if we want to, we shall, with the grace of God and in spite of our wretchedness, be in a position to lead a life capable of illuminating and drawing others along. This is the life of Christ within us.6 On another occasion Jesus said: By this my Father is glorified, that you bear much fruit, and so prove to be my disciples.7 But it is not a question of just any fruit, but rather fruits of supernatural life. Our Father wrote in the Instruction for the Work of St Raphael: If you do not make the young fellows men of prayer, you have wasted your time.8 Any result, other than the spiritual improvement of the people we are

6 From our Father, Meditation, 9 January 1956 7 Jn 15:8 8 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 36 The apostolic mission

dealing with, no matter how worthy and brilliant it may seem, would be useless if it did not help to bring souls closer to God. For what will it 9 profit a man, if he gains the whole world, and forfeits his life? Woe to the man who adorns himself with the leaves of a false apostolate, the man who displays the foliage of an apparently fruitful life, without bearing fruit. He seems to make good use of his time but is really a barren tree.10 Fruits of supernatural life are the unmistakable sign that we are following the Master, that we are united to him. “In order to realise what a good thing it is to obtain, along with our salvation, the salvation of others, listen to the prophet who speaks on behalf of God telling us: If you utter what is precious, and not what is worthless, you shall be as my mouth.11 What does this mean? It means that whoever leads another from error to the truth or from vice to virtue, is, within the limits of human efforts, imitating God. For he himself, being God as he was, took our flesh precisely for that reason, and he became man for the salvation of all mankind.”12

Showing our spirit of proselytism with deeds It is necessary for the Work of God to spread everywhere, declaring the reign of Christ forever, et fui tecum in omnibus ubicumque ambulasti, firmans regnum tuum in aeternum.13 The Church needs workers who will work in God’s vineyard. None of my children, our Father used to say, can rest satisfied if he does not win four or five vocations each year who are faithful, and who, living our spirit of divine filiation, can address Our Heavenly Father as Father.14

9 Mt 16:26 10 From our Father, Meditation, 9 January 1956 11 Jer 15:19 12 St John Chrysostom, In Genesim homiliae 1, 3, 4 13 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934 14 From our Father, Noticias April 1960, p.23

Bearing fruit 37

We must do proselytism and there is no excuse for not doing it. Whoever does proselytism gets vocations. Whoever does little proselytism, gets few vocations. Whoever does a lot of proselytism gets a lot of vocations. 15 If there are no vocations, love for God is lacking. Is that clear? Our Lord has told us so: each tree is known by its own fruit. For figs are not gathered from thorns, nor are grapes picked from a bramble bush.16 The grace of God will not be lacking; nor will the means, nor will the people who can come closer to the Work. God has sown the seed of our personal vocation on a very fertile hill. He fenced it in and cleared it of stones ... What more was there to do for my vineyard, that I have not done for it? When I looked for it to yield grapes, why did it yield wild grapes? And now I will tell you what I will do to my vineyard. I will remove its hedge, and it shall be devoured; I will break down its wall, and it shall be trampled down. I mean to make wasteland of it; no more pruning and digging, only briars and thorns will grow there.17 If at some time we see that there has not been any fruit, or that the fruit has been miserably small, let us say: Let it alone, sir, this year also, till I dig about it and put on manure. And if it bears fruit next year, well and good; but if not, you can cut it down.18 And it must be a firm, resolute decision that is expressed in deeds. Is my spirit of 19 God asks us for deeds: the reality proselytism borne out by my deeds? of our prayer, the reality of our generous dedication, the reality of our proselytism in action; for we understand the apostolate of action as the delectable fruit of prayer and sacrifice.20

15 From our Father, Crónica, March 1966, p.11 16 Lk 6:44 17 Is 5:1-6 18 Lk 13:8-9 19 From our Father, Examination from Brief Circle 20 From our Father, Instruction, 19 March 1934 38 The apostolic mission

We must pray, we must go to God in humble petition, to obtain from him what is a very special grace, a supernatural and unmerited grace. Then, Dominus dabit benignitatem et terra nostra dabit fructum suum (Ps 84:13). That blessing of God is the origin of every good fruit, of the climate we need so that in our lives we can become saints and beget children for him.21 And our petition must be sincere, it must be in keeping with an authentic unity of life, and be accompanied therefore by appropriate deeds, so that what we ask God may be fulfilled. I want your prayer to be that of children of God, not the prayer of the hypocrites who have to hear Jesus tell them: Not every man who says to me, ‘Lord, Lord’, shall enter the kingdom of heaven (Matt 7:21). Our prayer, our cry: ‘Lord, Lord’, goes together with our efficacious desire to fufil the Will of God.22 Prayer and action, then, must go hand in hand. If apostolic work were not imbued with prayer, it would be turned into a merely human activity. Our Father used to say: I measure the effectiveness of undertakings by the degree of sanctity attained by those who carry them out. Corporate activities are always a means, never an end. The purpose for which the sons and daughters of God in his Opus Dei work is, on the one hand, personal sanctification, and on the other, fostering sanctity in the world. Universities, university residences, home management schools ... Are these the ends? No; just as the spade and the hoe are not an end for the farmer, but the means for tilling the land.23 He would tell us the same thing about individual apostolate: It would make me very sad if a son of mine spoke to me only about his professional achievements. He can, and at times, he should speak about these things; but what I want to hear from you is an account of

21 From our Father, Meditation, 3 December 1961 22 From our Father, Meditation The prayer of the children of God, 4 April 1955, in In Dialogue with the Lord, p. 30 23 From our Father, Get-together, 1 August 1962

Bearing fruit 39

the fruits of your apostolate, of the vocations you have won, of the souls you have brought closer to God. If not, I would not believe in the sincerity of your desires to be an apostle.24 Since we are involved in lay and secular affairs, each one of us must make his profession an instrument for social progress and an instrument for his own sanctification and that of others.25 If this supernatural dimension were missing, work would not be even humanly complete. It could not satisfy the spiritual aspirations of those who are at our side. So we must turn our own profession into an instrument for apostolate. We must seek vocations. If this concern were missing, there would be reason to think that there is no true life of prayer either. For prayer leads us to seek what God wants us to do, so that we may put it into practice: it results in deeds. Every activity in its turn, if it is to be effective, must be based on prayer, on keeping in mind the apostolates any apostolic target no matter how small it may seem and we must realise that it is always a supernatural reality which depends on God more than on us. The two things have their root in the one same charity, in the one same reaching out of the will towards others, for the sake of God, which must be like a continual goad that keeps us from falling asleep and that impels us to pray and to work untiringly for the love of souls.

The fruitfulness of sacrifice Dominus dabit benignitatem (Ps 84:13). Our God expects fruit. If we do not give it, we deprive him of it. But he does not want fruit that is stunted and puny as a result of our not having known how to give ourselves.26 Here then is another premise for our proselytism: unless a grain of wheat falls into the earth and dies, it remains alone;

24 From our Father, Letter 15 October 1948 25 From our Father, Letter 2 October 1939 26 From our Father, Meditation, 3 December 1961 40 The apostolic mission

but if it dies, it bears much fruit.27 Our desire to win souls must follow the same steps that Christ followed. “He became man for the salvation of all mankind. But why should we merely say that he took our flesh, when in reality he took everything human, accepting the cross in order to free us from the curse that chained us to sin? Hence, St Paul exclaims: Christ redeemed us from the curse of the law, having become a curse for us.28 If then, he, being God of ineffable essence, was subject to all this for us and for our salvation, what should we not do for the others who are of the same flesh and blood as ourselves in order to free them from the clutches of the devil and teach them the way of 29 virtue?” Apostolic activity demands abnegation, a spirit of sacrifice. We see it in Our Lord’s life. He had nowhere to lay his head,30 and he travelled continuously in search of souls in spite of his weariness.31 He was so busy attending to the people that at times he did not even have time to eat.32 The first manifestation of generosity that we have to live in proselytism is the spirit of sacrifice that the carrying out of the apostolate brings with it, demanding time, dedication and the renouncing of personal projects – whether big or small – for the sake of other people. Your unity of life ought to include a spontaneous magnanimity, renewed each day, that must be clearly seen and has to be shown in all things, so that, as faithful soldiers of Jesus Christ in the world, you offer yourselves as holocausts, truly saying, in the uprightness of my heart, I have freely offered all these things (1 Chron 29:17). This has to be your preparation for the continuous apostolate that Jesus

27 Jn 12:24 28 Gal 3:13 29 St John Chrysostom, In Genesim homiliae 1, 3, 4 30 Cf Lk 9:58 31 Cf Jn 4:6 32 Cf Mk 3:20; Jn 4:34

Bearing fruit 41

asks of us, as continuous as the beating of our hearts.33 The farmer, in order to obtain fruit, must first toil.34 Effort, and the disposition to live many supernatural and human virtues, are necessary in our apostolic activity, which must be vigorous, industrious, practical, varied, dynamic, enterprising and gratuitous.35 This takes time and constant application. Be patient, therefore brethren, until the coming of the Lord. Behold, the farmer waits for the precious fruit of the earth, being patient over it until it receives the early and the late rain. You too must wait patiently and take courage.36 St Paul says, Now I rejoice in sufferings for your sake, and in my flesh I complete what is lacking in Christ’s afflictions for the sake his body, that is the Church.37 Our Lord has wanted to associate us with his redeeming work, calling for our personal sacrifice, in addition to our word and our example, in order to apply his infinite merits to souls. Thus a member of the Work who proposes to win a new vocation, does extraordinary mortifications and he asks others to do likewise, convinced that of himself he can do nothing if he doesn’t obtain abundant grace from Heaven with prayer and sacrifices.38 But in order for that sacrifice to be acceptable in the eyes of God it must be based on true generosity in the service of souls: to love one’s neighbour as oneself is much more than all whole burnt offerings and sacrifices.39 For it is not possible to set our consciences at ease with a few more or less showy mortifications – What to me is the multitude of your sacrifices? Says the Lord. I have had enough of burnt offerings of rams40

33 From our Father, Letter 16 July 1933 34 2 Tim 2:6 35 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950 36 Jas 5:7-8 37 Col 1:24 38 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934 39 Mk 12:33 40 Is 1:11 42 The apostolic mission

– if there was not really in our lives that other sacrifice that is less perceptible because it is more interior, and that does not consist in giving up our possessions, but in the radical surrender of ourselves: I have become all things to all men, to bring everybody salvation.41 It is on the foundation of that charity that we must place the humble offering of our mortification and that of our brothers, in the certainty that it will never be fruitless. If God does not give his grace to one person, he will give it to another. The sacrifice of a righteous man is acceptable, and the memory of it will not be forgotten.42

Specific objectives When hunger for proselytism is continually reflected in prayer and in the determination to develop the apostolate with whatever sacrifice may be necessary, there will also be a spirit of winning new apostles in our action. The field will be ploughed and, as well as being generously sown, it will receive all the care that the farmer puts into it. And then, with God’s blessing, the harvest will come.43 If this action, this work of tilling the land, were lacking, it would mean that there was no authentic prayer, no true mortification, no interior life, no love for the Work. These souls, even if they were in the Work, would be dead, rotten. Iam foetet (John 11:39). Proselytism is a manifestation of the spiritual life that you have.44 In your hearts and in your deeds you have to be totally convinced that abundant St Raphael and St Gabriel apostolates are absolutely essential for the vitality, development and effectiveness of our Work.45 This need will lead us to practical decisions, so that the desire that

41 1 Cor 9:22 42 Sir 35:7 43 From our Father, Crónica, July 1962, p.12 44 From our Father, Crónica, 1968, p.606 45 Alone with God, 268

Bearing fruit 43

moves us will be expressed in specific and effective actions. We need to foresee and anticipate the little happenings of each day. Thus, each morning, when we go out early to work, whatever that work may be, among the other things that occupy our thoughts there will be certain targets for proselytism, that will be precisely determined. Those targets may seem to be small, but they are not really so, because they demand true renunciation, forgetting about ourselves in order to think about the others, overcoming laziness, and making good use of our time. They demand a spirit of sacrifice, so that they come to be a reality lived day after day, in the morning just as well as in the afternoon, when it is a feast day just as well as when it is an ordinary working day, in the winter just as well as in the summer. They demand constancy for renewing that daily plan, for seeing in the prayer how we can make the most of everything we find ourselves involved in. In that way, there will also be a true work of winning new apostles: vocations. That is why our Father wanted us to examine ourselves each day on the work of winning new apostles, so that we could see the specific deeds in which our own zeal had been shown. For proselytism is made up, as our whole life is, of little things; and if these were missing, it would not be real. We must say in the Confidence: I have done this, I have thought about that, I have prayed so much, I have mortified myself, I have prepared this visit. And if your brother doesn’t ask you, you should tell him anyway.46 If we take pains to live this spirit of examination, there will be resolutions, there will be plans; and as a consequence, there will be the reality of winning new apostles. We will lead a coherent life as contemplatives in the midst of human concerns, for the need to act will urge us to bring our work of winning new apostles to the prayer, so that we then put into effect our specific resolutions. In this way, we will live our whole day with supernatural

46 From our Father, Crónica, May 1963, p.11 44 The apostolic mission

outlook: our work, our conversations, our friendships. We will feel the need of having a spirit of sacrifice in order to fulfil those resolutions, and of having recourse to God and offering mortifications. All this produces a circle in which prayer leads us to action, action to prayer, and both prayer and action to our having a spirit of mortification, working with the right human and supernatural intention in this contemplative life of ours, which is so active. Jesus Christ, Our Lord, is saying to us now: Lift up your eyes, and see how the fields are already white for harvest (John 4:35). All human things, the whole world, is now the divine field for our operatio Dei; and, as the spiritual fruit of the work of my children, the Lord’s granaries are filling up with good wheat: the bread of a most sweet offering, food for souls, grain for the new sowing.47
##
47 From our Father, Letter 28 March 1955

## THE BASIS AND ROOT OF EFFECTIVENESS {#cuaderno.5.4}

The life of a Christian begins with Baptism. By Baptism we receive the gift of sanctifying grace which makes us share in God’s nature, be capable of acting in a divine way and ultimately merit to see God face to face in the glory of Heaven without the mediation of any created thing. That state of grace present in the soul after Baptism and in the penitent after receiving absolution “is nothing other than a certain beginning of glory in us.”1 It is a seed, a grafting on of divine life, which, like natural life, demands a progressive growth and the exercise of certain vital functions. This development needs and depends upon our union with Christ, the giver of supernatural life. As Our Lord told the Samaritan woman, Whoever drinks of the water that I shall give him will never thirst; the water that I shall give him will become in him a spring of water welling up to eternal life.2 Furthermore, as St Thomas says, “Whoever drinks of the living water of grace that our Saviour gives will never more desire anything. He will only long to receive it in greater abundance.”3 He will want to grow in supernatural life, to identify himself with Christ in such a way

1 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.24, a.3 ad 2 2 Jn 4:13-14 3 St Thomas, Super Evangelium S. Ioannis lectura 4, 3 46 The apostolic mission

that he can say with St Paul, Vivit autem in me Christus,4 Christ lives in me. This divinising of the soul, this living on a higher, supernatural level, is not due to man’s merit. It exceeds absolutely his natural strength and merits. Interior life begins with the infused virtues and the gifts of the Holy Spirit which we receive gratuitously through the mercy of God. Only with their help can we bear supernatural fruit, and perform deeds worthy of eternal life.

Growth in charity, growth in apostolate Among all the virtues, St Paul says, above all put on charity which binds everything together in perfect harmony.5 For charity in a certain sense includes all of them. It gives them life, makes them meritorious, and directs the acts of all the other virtues. Hence, to grow in supernatural life is to grow in charity, in love of God. This growth also depends on us and requires our co-operation. As St Thomas explains, “Although charity is a divine gift, we must on our part be disposed to receive it. Hence we should recognise that two things are especially necessary to acquire charity and two more to grow the charity already possessed. To acquire charity it is necessary, in the first place, to listen attentively to the word of God ..., and then to meditate continually on the divine riches, for as the Psalm says, And in my meditation a fire shall flame up.6 The other two things that increase the charity already possessed are, in the first place, detaching the heart from earthly things ..., and then a firm patience in adversity.”7 With prayer and mortification we prepare ourselves to receive grace and to develop the divine life in us. The fruit of this supernatural

4 Gal 2:20 5 Col 3:14 6 Ps 39:3 7 St Thomas, In duo praec. caritatis et in decem legis praec. expositio V, 1155-1159.

The basis and root of effectiveness 47

vitality will be deeds, acts of love, as “he died for all, that those who love might live no longer for themselves but for him who for their sake died and was raised.”8 We can all remember how the desire to do apostolate, to live in the Work dedicated to the service of souls, sprung in our heart from our interior life, from our personal relationship with Jesus Christ. Apostolate follows on from interior life as a natural consequence because the reason for loving God and loving our neighbour is the same, namely, God himself. Because of charity, what we want and seek for all souls is that God may be in them, that they may be like God and love Him. “The apostolate is lived in the faith, hope and charity which is poured out by the Holy Spirit into the hearts of all the members of the Church. And the precept of charity, which is Our Lord’s greatest commandment, urges all Christians to work for the glory of God through the coming of his Kingdom and for the communication of eternal life to all men, that they may know the only one true God and Jesus Christ whom he has sent.9”10 Apostolate accompanies interior life as an effect follows its cause, as a consequence its principle, for it is something which follows necessarily, for “there is no sacrifice more pleasing to God than zeal for the salvation of souls.”11 As a consequence, this zeal becomes an unmistakable sign that there is authentic love for God, true interior life. St John says, We have passed out of death into life, because we love the brethren.12 Our Father also teaches us, Our spirit has to be life-blood, sap for the whole plant, so that the tree give divine fruits.13

8 2 Cor 5:15 9 Cf Jn 17:3 10 Second Vatican Council, Decree Apostolicam actuositatem, 3 11 St Gregory the Great, In Ezechielem homiliae, 12 12 1 Jn 3:14 13 From our Father, Letter 6 May 1945 48 The apostolic mission

The origin of zeal for souls and its cause is in interior life. Our Father once told us, I want to remind my children once more that the basis of all our work lies in an intense interior life, in our being truly and effectively contemplative souls. Hence our first duty is not only to foster and sustain, but also constantly to improve our interior life and that of the others. This is an especially grave obligation for those in the Work who have functions of government and formation. Keep very much in mind that without interior life there can be no true proselytism or fruitful deeds. It would make our work uncertain and even false.14 Our contemplative vocation demands above all else that we foster and grow in interior life. The consequence of this will be a fruitful apostolic activity because “teaching and preaching proceed from the fullness of contemplation.”15

Symptoms of lukewarmness St Ambrose says that “sometimes there is a great deal of interior activity but very little action. This occurs when one who may know all the principles of medicine proposes to cure a soul without putting them into practice. This lack of apostolic action implies a lack of interior life.”16 For when there is true interior life, zeal for souls does not remain as an ineffective desire. It consists in a firm, energetic act of the will that overcomes the obstacles and difficulties that may appear. Apostolic zeal arises even from a little love for God. But in order to carry on through all the difficulties that apostolic activity brings with it, a strong and vigorous love for God is needed, together with a spirit of sacrifice, daring, constancy, and the practice of all the virtues. Moreover, there may also be difficulties of a practical kind, due to the

14 From our Father, 11 February 1967 15 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.188, a.6 16 St Ambrose, Expositio Evangelii sec. Lucam 1, 9

The basis and root of effectiveness 49

lack of time or suitable means, such as lacking proper instruments for the apostolate, or having too much work to do. In order to face up to this scarcity of material means or of human talents without losing heart when results are slow in coming, a strong love of God is required, and a solid, vigorous interior life. When this interior life is lacking, the apostolate begins to lose its attraction and, as a result it begins to be abandoned. The apostolic impulse no longer comes spontaneously from within. It is only external circumstances which make us remember the apostolate and, in a certain way, force us to do it. The indications we receive no longer appear as a practical aid to facilitate and guide our action. The one who receives them looks upon them more as an external stimulus, lacking effectiveness, foreign to his tastes and desires, and reminding him of some bothersome duties of conscience. In these circumstances, if he does not react in time by working to enliven his own interior life, the situation tends to get worse. For if he no longer finds enjoyment in apostolate, or meaning in the sacrifice it entails, he tends to neglect prayer, refusing to face, in the presence of God, the need to get out of this situation. He also loses the spirit of examination of conscience which would demand a specific resolution. Then, in thinking about the apostolate and the practices of piety, he only becomes sad and begins to neglect his spiritual duties even more. A vicious circle is thus set off, for if the heart, which was made for happiness, finds only dissatisfaction in the spiritual life, it will turn aside to other things, seeking a compensation for what it no longer finds in interior life. It will become centred on some ordinary activity, perhaps in the practice of one’s profession or in seeking little satisfactions, even though licit in themselves, but leading away from the true path. And from this attitude of slaking the heart’s thirst in the natural life will come a greater repugnance for the things of the spirit, which become 50 The apostolic mission

even more difficult and more disagreeable. Little by little, the heart may find itself tempted, no longer by the attraction of natural life, but by things which are even lower. Then there begin to appear the great difficulties which have but a partially objective basis. Agreed, writes our Father, there is a lot of pressure from outside and that excuses you in part. But there is also complicity within – take a good look – and there I see no excuse.17

The way to rectify When there begin to appear these symptoms of lukewarmness such as avoiding the examination of conscience and personal dialogue with God, when a person cannot do without compensations, when the apostolate becomes something forced and is felt more as an obligation than as a need; when these symptoms appear in a more or less advanced stage, the moment has come to put into practice more than ever what our Founder has taught us: First, prayer; then, atonement; in the third place, very much ‘in the third place’, action.18 Without interior life, with only will-power, apostolic activity cannot be carried on for long. For it would call for a gigantic effort, which would be sterile in any case. Alone, we cannot do anything worthwhile because we will have cut off the path to a relationship with God. Sine me nihil potestis facere (John 15:5), without me you can do nothing. But united to Our Lord, we can do all things: omnia possum in eo qui me confortat (Phil 4:13). We can do all things in him who will strengthen us, even though we make mistakes and commit errors, as long as we struggle not to. A person I know – I never really got to know him well – once dreamt that he was flying in an aeroplane at a great altitude, and not inside, but on the wings. And he suffered terribly. Our Lord made

17 The Way, 700 18 The Way, 82

The basis and root of effectiveness 51

him understand that this is how souls without interior life move at the heights of the apostolate, with the constant danger of falling down, with suffering and insecurity.19 When zeal and vibrant apostolic desire are lacking in the work of souls, it is not a case of trying to sustain the apostolate by dint of will-power, in a violent way. It is necessary to go to God who is the source of grace because only in him will we find the supernatural drive necessary for the supernatural work of apostolate. I am the vine, you are the branches. He who abides in me, and I in him, he it is that bears much fruit, for apart from me you can do nothing.20 It is necessary to use the supernatural means of prayer and mortification and thus we will transform this bad “complicity” of the soul into a cooperation with the action of grace. Prayer is needed, therefore, not only to recognise that without God we can do nothing, but also because in prayer “we identify our will with the will of God.”21 Christ, with his Passion imminent, gave us an example of this kind of prayer to identify his human will, which resisted the Passion and the Cross, with the divine will: Father, he repeated, let not my will, but thine be done ... And being in an agony he prayed more earnestly; and his sweat became like great drops of blood falling upon the ground.22 In this way we too should pray in order to make our own the will of God, whenever obstacles impede the apostolic tasks Our Lord asks of us. We cannot content ourselves with carrying out the demands of the apostolate as if they were a burdensome obligation. As St Thomas says, “Prayer ought to continue as long as needed to provoke the fervour of interior desire.”23 If not, the tasks end up being abandoned. Without prayer it is impossible to

19 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931 20 Jn 15:5 21 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.83, a.5 ad 2 22 Lk 22:42-44 23 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.83, a.15 52 The apostolic mission

persevere in the apostolate.24 Moreover, with prayer there must be mortification, which will not consist in imposing great penances on ourselves as if pain were a talisman to produce apostolic effectiveness or an exorbitant price that God demands for it. It is a case of putting to death the “old man”, cutting the ties that bind the soul to all that is not God, that exhaust its energies and leave it blind and without the capacity to taste how pleasing it is to fulfil the divine will. We have to mortify our own pleasures in order to be pleased with what pleases God. We have to let go of our own will and die to our self-gratification in order to find satisfaction, pleasure and joy in fulfilling the will of God. This is a satisfaction that is often imperceptible to the senses, but it fills the soul. Unless a grain of wheat falls into the earth and dies, it remains alone; but if it dies, it bears much fruit.25 In this way, once the “complicity” of the soul ceases, the storm of temptations abates. When we place our happiness in following God, there is ever more delight in our spiritual life even though it is demanding. Obedience and apostolic work are no longer an external and unpleasant obligation, but rather the channel which carries intimate desires for apostolate, the fruit of interior life. The process is then reversed: it becomes the path which draws an ever greater apostolic effectiveness from our interior life.
