The foundation of all apostolate Interior life is what sustains apostolic work. If there is no interior life, apostolate diminishes and ends up disappearing. St Ambrose says that “it sometimes happens that there is exuberant activity and a weak interior life, as when someone has received Baptism and the other

24 From our Father, Crónica, June 1966, p.9 25 Jn 12:24

The basis and root of effectiveness 53

sacraments of salvation but does not practice the different virtues. Then, what normally happens is that through lack of interior life, apostolic activity does not yield fruit either.”26 Without interior life, the work – if there is any – becomes uncertain or even false. Without piety, government degenerates into tyranny, collegiate government becomes impossible, and disunity with the loss of good spirit is all but inevitable.27 Without interior life it is impossible to carry out such tasks as are demanded by the spirit of the Work. The most there could be is a pretence, an appearance of being busy, condemned to sterility. Are grapes gathered from thorns, or figs from 28 Some fruit there may be because after all we are instruments thistles? in the hands of God and “just as the bodily medicines that men use are of no avail unless God gives health ..., so in an analogous way, the help of human instructions is of use to the soul when the one who makes them be profitable is God who could give men the Gospel without men and without man’s mediation.”29 But generally, God has arranged things so that our prayers obtain what he has planned,30 so we realise that in this we must have recourse to divine aid.31 And so apostolic effectiveness normally depends on the interior life of the apostle. Otherwise, with great human talents and good instruments of apostolate, the effectiveness could be nil. Hence the one who endeavours to teach the word of God, “in speaking, must do everything on his part so that he be listened to intelligently, with pleasure and with docility. But he should not doubt that if he accomplishes something and in the measure in which he does so, it is

26 St Ambrose, Expositio Evangelii sec. Lucam 1, 9 27 From our Father, 11 February 1967 28 Mt 7:16 29 St Augustine, De doctrina christiana 4, 17, 34 30 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.83, a.2 ad 2 31 Ibid., ad 1 54 The apostolic mission

due more to the piety of his prayers than to his gift of oratory. Hence, in praying for those to whom he must speak, let him rather be a man of prayer than of fine words. Then when the time to speak comes, before beginning to utter words, let him raise his thirsting soul to God so as to pour out what he has drunk in and to exhale that which fills him.”32 To inflame apostolic zeal, to prevent our being overcome by the difficulties of the task and to make that task fruitful, it is necessary to advance in interior life, to grow in charity, in love for God and, because of God, for all that he loves. “Charity, which is, as it were, the soul of the whole apostolate, is given to them and nourished in them by the Sacraments, the Eucharist above all.”33 In the Eucharist, centre and root of our interior life,34 God gives us superabundant grace. We have only to try to remove the obstacles that are opposed to His action in our soul by means of prayer and mortification. The means to sustain and improve this interior life, which is the basis and root of our effectiveness, you know well. They are our Norms and Customs, the careful and constant fulfilment of our Norms of life. Meditate and make others meditate on what I tell you, and draw out the practical consequences necessary for your personal life and for your work of government and formation.35
##
32 St Augustine, De doctrina christiana 4, 15, 32 33 Second Vatican Council, Decree Apostolicam actuositatem, 3 34 From our Father, Obras, August 1965, p.13 35 From our Father, 11 February 1967

## APOSTOLATE AND PRAYER {#cuaderno.5.5}

If the order of original grace was already a free and unmerited gift of God, the order of the Redemption – the re-establishment of that lost supernatural life – is doubly the fruit of divine mercy. The attitude of man could not be other than to implore, confidently but humbly, persistently yet fully conscious that this was a grace, a favour that could in no way be his due, because there was no action capable of gaining the salvation that was being sought. Century after century would go by before the Messiah came. The patriarchs and the prophets prayed, and the whole of the people of Israel with them: Lord, the world thirsts for 1 God required the insistent prayer of just men before the your coming! Incarnation of the Word could take place, even though this itself had already been the fruit of a special grace granted in view of the foreseen merits of Christ. Consequently when Mary communicates her joy to Elizabeth at the beginning of the Redemption, she could say that God has shown strength with his arm, he has scattered the proud in the imagination of their hearts. He has put down the mighty from their thrones, and exalted those of low degree; he has filled the hungry with good things, and the rich he has sent empty away. He has helped his servant Israel, in remembrance of his mercy.2 Christ himself, as man, prays, pleads, begs, implores, in order to

1 From our Father, Get-together, 20 September 1961, in Crónica, October 1961, pp.41-42 2 Lk 1:51-54 56 The apostolic mission

show us the right way, or the proper attitude to have toward what is purely the gift of grace. At the end of his life on earth, his extended priestly prayer is moving, and insistent throughout: Glorify thy Son, that the Son may glorify thee, since thou hast given him power over all flesh, to give eternal life to all whom thou hast given him.3 He asks the Father for his grace, to watch over them and to sanctify them,4 and commends to him the effectiveness of their apostolic action: I do not pray for these only, but also for those who believe in me through their word.5 A little later in Gethsemane, beset by sorrow and filled with anguish, he prayed more earnestly; and his sweat became like great drops of blood falling down upon the ground.6 Even then he repeatedly gives the same advice to his disciples: Watch and pray.7 Until the end of time the Church will have to go on making this same supplication of Christ; especially in the Mass, a Sacrifice of petition of infinite value, where the begging for grace is renewed unceasingly and always in the name of Jesus Christ: per Dominum nostrum Iesum Christum. This is the primary function of the Church, and therefore the principal function of her ministers, the priests: to pray, especially at Mass and in the Divine Office, which is the public prayer of the Church. Pray, bolstered by our Lord’s promise: Truly, truly, I say to you, if you ask anything of the Father, he will give it to you in my name. Hitherto you have asked nothing in my name; ask, and you will receive, that your joy may be full.8 And because all of us are the Church, we all have to participate in this prayer. In our life as Christians to pray must also be our basic attitude. To

3 Jn 17:1-2 4 Cf Jn 17:6-19 5 Jn 17:20 6 Lk 22:44 7 Mt 26:41 8 Jn 16:23-24

Apostolate and prayer 57

pray, to ask, in union with Jesus Christ, who has said to everyone: If you abide in me, and my words abide in you, ask whatever you will and it shall be done for you.9

The basis of all supernatural work Pray: this is the way,10 our Father has told us. In a meditation he commented: The Acts of the Apostles transmit to us a scene which inspires me, because it is a living example for us all; for this reason I have had it engraved in so many oratories and elsewhere: They devoted themselves to the Apostles’ teaching and fellowship, to the breaking of bread and the prayers (Acts 2:42). What did the saints do, my son, all the saints? I do not think there has been a single saint without prayer, or anyone raised to the altars who has not been a soul of prayer.11 He also affirmed in the same meditation, prayer, my children, is the basis of all supernatural work.12 God has given a supernatural aim – fruits of grace – to his Work, and the means have to be proportionate. Be always united in prayer. This is the only secret of the Work. This is the source of our happiness, of our peace, of our serenity, and therefore of our supernatural effectiveness.13 The Work itself was born after many years of prayer, of our Father’s continuous petition. Since I was fifteen I began to pray for Opus Dei, without knowing what it was. I had an inkling and prayed for many years without knowing what it was and I used to say:

9 Jn 15:7 10 From our Father 11 From our Father, Meditation The prayer of the children of God, 4 April 1955, in In Dialogue with the Lord, p. 30 12 Ibid. 13 From our Father, Letter 19 March 1954 58 The apostolic mission

Domine, ut sit.14 This is the constant teaching and example that our Father gave us. The first thing he always did was to pray, to pray a lot, literally living at times those words of the Gospel: pernoctans in oratione Dei,15 spending the night in prayer. When something became a real need or more difficult, he turned more intensely and continually to prayer and moved us to pray with more insistence and over a longer period. Have complete confidence and an unshakeable hope; keep on praying without ceasing, offering your work and sacrifice to God each day so that an adequate solution can finally be found,16 he would tell us referring, for example, to his special intention. Our Lord is willing to give, on condition that we ask. Be certain, my children, Christ’s answer to a pious, trusting and persevering prayer is fiat tibi sicut vis (Matt 15:28), be it done for you as you desire.17

Primacy of prayer This instinctive feeling, this need to pray, can only be weakened, or cease to be felt for two reasons: because of a hidden pride, which tends to attribute supernatural fruit to our own actions; or because of a deformation of the apostolate, converting it into an activity which has a purely natural aim, – be it a social, political, cultural – that could therefore be obtained with simply human means. At the close of the second stage of Vatican Council II, Blessed Paul VI affirmed once more the primacy of prayer in the saving work of the Church. “We rejoice at this accomplishment. We may see in this an acknowledgement of a right order of values and duties: God in the first place; prayer, our first duty; the liturgy ... the first invitation to the

14 From our Father, Get-together, 20 September 1961, in Crónica, October 1961, p.41 15 Lk 6:12 16 From our Father, Letter 2 October 1958 17 From our Father, Letter 25 January 1961

Apostolate and prayer 59

world to break forth in happy and truthful prayer ... For the Church is a religious society, a community at prayer. It is composed of people with a flourishing interior life and spirituality that is nourished by faith and grace.”18 To think that the salvation of souls, sanctity, the coredemption, could be the result of the talent, external activity and merely human means is to condemn oneself to failure, to the most absolute sterility. Unless the Lord builds the house, those who build it labour in vain. Unless the Lord watches over the city, the watchman stays awake in vain.19 With failure comes the abandoning of the work, because without prayer it is not possible to persevere in the apostolate.20 The apostle who does not pray does not bring other souls to God and ends up drawing his own soul away from him. “If now,” Blessed Paul VI continued, “we wish to simplify some aspects of our liturgical rites, if we wish to render them more intelligible to the people and accommodated to the language they speak, by doing so we certainly do not wish to lessen the importance of prayer, or to give it less importance than other forms of the sacred ministry or pastoral activity ... something no one would wish to disturb or to damage.”21 Even mortification by itself cannot give fruitfulness to the apostolate because sanctification depends not upon man’s will or exertion but upon God’s mercy.22 Consequently, any sacrifice has to be raised to God with a humble sense of supplication, of impetration, of petition. This is why our Father has pointed out to us this order on the level of apostolic activity: First, prayer; then, atonement; in the third

18 Blessed Paul VI, Address, 4 December 1963 19 Ps 127:1 20 From our Father 21 Blessed Paul VI, Address, 4 December 1963 22 Rom 9:16 60 The apostolic mission

place, very much ‘in the third place’, action.23 God has wanted us to share in his redemptive work above all by prayer. For we are God’s fellow workers.24 “This, of course, is not on account of any defect in God’s power, but because he employs intermediary causes in order that the beauty of order may be preserved in the universe; and also that he may communicate to creatures the dignity of causality.”25 In the Redemption, this causality is subordinated in a very special way because it is in the realm of grace, obtained from divine benevolence and mercy, not by right. Therefore we have been commanded, pray for one another that you may be healed.26 And even when we ask and pray, we are simply instruments of the saving action of God, for “the salvation of a person is predestined in such a way, that whatever helps that person towards salvation falls under the order of predestination; whether it be one’s prayers, or those of another.”27 Thus, when God gives grace to a soul in virtue of our prayers, he does no more than fulfil his antecedent will which contained that grace through that prayer. Therefore our own prayer must always be in accordance with God’s will: Thy will be done on earth as it is in heaven.28 Ours is the situation of one who has nothing and can obtain nothing by himself, but who has received the promise that if he asks, he will receive. Our adoptive divine filiation, which is also a free gift, confers on us a right to hope. If we pray to our Father God, he will listen to us. Let us pray, then, aware that by ourselves we can do nothing, that all effectiveness has to come from God. “We must pray, not in order to inform God of our needs or desires, but in order to remind ourselves

23 The Way, 82 24 1 Cor 3:9 25 St Thomas, S. Th., I, q.23, a.8 ad 2 26 Jas 5:16 27 St Thomas, S. Th., I, q.23, a.8 28 Mt 6:10

Apostolate and prayer 61

that in these matters we need divine assistance.”29 The reason for the absolute necessity of prayer of petition is that God demands it of us in an authoritative way.

Prayer with works Praying, praying is the system; and then setting to work calmly and cheerfully.30 We must work because apostolic action is also necessary for the vast majority of Christians. And this activity then becomes an indispensable manifestation of the sincerity and rectitude of intention of prayer. There are many ways of praying. I want your prayer to be that of sons of God, not the prayer of hypocrites. For they will have to listen to Jesus saying, not everyone who says to me, Lord, Lord, shall enter the Kingdom of Heaven (Matt 7:21). We do the will of his Father, after having made this dedication of our life. Our prayer, our cry, Lord, Lord, is united to our effective desire to fulfil God’s will. This cry manifests itself in a thousand different ways. This is prayer, and this is what I want for you.31 In doing the norms, our Father underlines, and without being aware of it, from morning till night and from night till morning, you will be praying: making acts of love, acts of reparation, with your heart, with your lips, with your small mortifications that enkindle the soul.32 In short, I want our whole life to be prayer.33 Our Founder has pointed out to us repeatedly that the weapon of Opus Dei is not work, it is prayer. That is why we convert work into

29 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.83, a.2 ad 1 30 Alone with God, 80 31 From our Father, Meditation The prayer of the children of God, 4 April 1955, in In Dialogue with the Lord, p. 30 32 Ibid., p. 24 33 Ibid.

62 The apostolic mission

prayer and why we have a contemplative soul.34 We offer up our request to God with our ordinary work, as we carry it out, as we fulfil the duties of our state in life, of our job; and this work is completed with human perfection and made supernatural by the intention and the way we carry it out. Prayer is thus accompanied by proof of really wanting his will to be done. In prayer we seek to identify our will with that of God: Non mea voluntas sed tua fiat,35 not my will but thine, be done. We do not assert our own desires and projects, but those of God. Let us not tell God what we want, but rather ask that he let us know what he wants us to do so that we can do it; for he knows everything we need,36 and he will arrange for things to happen sooner, more, better than we could ever have dreamed. Therefore humility must be an indispensable element of our petition. Let us not invoke God like the Pharisee of the parable: I am not like other men, extortioners, unjust ...37 We do not appeal to our own good will and conduct as a claim for our petition, but rather to the divine goodness and mercy. We need to say with the publican, God, be 38 Come what may, we accept wholeheartedly merciful to me a sinner! whatever God decides, which is always the best.

Confidence in prayer Placing everything humbly in the hands of God embodies great confidence. We know that God is our most loving Father, always ready to give, always ready to save, to move the hearts of men and enkindle them with desires of love. Neither the conviction of our unworthiness

34 From our Father, Crónica, September 1959, p.9 35 Lk 22:42 36 Cf Mt 6:10 37 Lk 18:11 38 Lk 18:13

Apostolate and prayer 63

nor the objective difficulties that might make our work arduous can cloud our confidence. God has called us to the apostolate despite our miseries, and counts on the difficulties that we encounter so that we see clearly that it is he who achieves everything, he who gives the increase. He only wants us to ask his help so that our desire become a reality: Petite et dabitur vobis: quaerite, et invenietis; pulsate, et aperietur vobis (Matt 7:7): Ask, and it will be given you: seek, and you will find; knock, and it will be opened to you.39 What greater reason can we have to ask with confidence than this promise of God? Besides, we count on the intercession of the most Blessed Virgin, who can do anything before God. In doing your work as an apostle, in enkindling the fire of proselytism our Father tells us, you should always be united to God, seeking for this union with God beside his blessed Mother.40 In the face of our helplessness, we go to Mary. “Our Advocate went up to heaven so that as Mother of the Judge and Mother of Mercy, she might work our salvation.”41 Moreover, we have confidence in the intercession of the angels and saints. When we pray for proselytism and apostolate, we have the certainty that God hears us; for he himself has given us the command, pray therefore the Lord of the harvest to send out labourers into his harvest.42 We have every reason to go to God with confidence. Nothing should destroy this faith; nothing can rightly weaken it. Ask in faith, with no doubting, for he who doubts is like a wave of the sea that is driven and tossed by the wind.43 Not to have this confidence, to doubt, is to belittle the omnipotence of God. Not to believe fully in his promises is to rely on one’s own self. “Ask him without wavering and

39 From our Father, Letter 2 February 1945 40 From our Father 41 St Bernard, In Assumptione B.V.M. sermo 1, 1 42 Mt 9:38 43 Jas 1:6 64 The apostolic mission

you will find that his great mercy will not abandon you but will grant the petition of your soul.”44 This confidence is shown also in praying again and again to God until he grants what we ask. Jesus told us the parable of the wicked judge and of the untimely friend who asked for three loaves of bread at a ridiculous hour so that we might be persevering. God will listen to him who is not discouraged and who persists with faith. For one of the qualities of good prayer is stubbornness. Don’t you remember when you were children? Daddy, buy me that ... We whined. We kept on asking, until we got it.45 Ask. Pester. “He who beseeches, let him always beseech. Even though he is not always asking, let him be ready to ask at any moment.”46 Perseverance. Help me to cry: Jesus, souls! ... Apostolic souls! They are for you, for your glory. You’ll see how in the end he will hear us.47

The basis of peace and effectiveness The divine calling has a very specific end, our Father reminds us: place yourself at all the crossroads of the world, you yourself being immersed in God.48 Our whole desire for apostolate has to be turned into a continuous, confident and humble petition. Prayer is the most effective means of winning new apostles.49 Without this prayer there can be no fruit. Bear in mind, our Father wrote, that if Christians are not more effective, it is because few of them pray; and those of us who do pray, pray little.50 The environment,

44 Pastor of Hermas, Mand, 9, 1 45 From our Father, Get-together, 20 September 1961, in Crónica, October 1961, p.42 46 St Ambrose, Expositio in Ps 118 19, 18 47 The Way, 804 48 From our Father, Meditation The prayer of the children of God, 4 April 1955, in In Dialogue with the Lord, pp. 34-35 49 The Way, 800 50 From our Father, Letter 6 May 1945

Apostolate and prayer 65

the objective difficulties, are all overcome by prayer. If there is no fruit, if souls do not respond, if our work of proselytism and of apostolate is not more effective, it is because we pray little. Let us pray, let us pray a lot, and all the difficulties will disappear. Prayer is the basis for our peace and our apostolic effectiveness.51 With prayer we will obtain everything; without prayer there can be no supernatural fruit, and it would be impossible for there to be any. We all have some very practical experience which shows that a moment arrives when we can do no more for a soul, when the only solution is to go to Our Lord, because only he moves hearts. The difficulties are only overcome when we go to Our Lord. Our longing for the harvest has to be shown through supplication. You write: ‘Our longing to see it all going ahead and spreading seems about to turn into impatience. When will things get under way, when will the breakthrough come, ... when will we see the world ours?’ And you add: ‘It will not be a useless longing if we seek an outlet for it in “coercing”, in “pestering” God: then we will have made excellent use of our time.’52 In so far as we pray, we identify our will with Christ’s saving will, we make our petition one with his, we identify ourselves with him and we become more worthy of being heard. “The more you receive, the happier he is, and the more ready he is to continue giving to you. God considers our salvation part of his richness. His glory lies in giving generously to as many as ask him, which is what St Paul declared when he said that he bestows his riches upon all who call upon him.53”54 This sense of union with Christ in our prayer of petition, with our longing to redeem men, makes us feel secure, steady and confident. It

51 From our Father, Letter 14 February 1944 52 The Way, 911 53 Rom 10:12 54 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae, 22, 6 66 The apostolic mission

takes away anxiety, the interior anguish of the soul that relies on its own strength. The instinct to pray is the way, as well as the benefit, of attaining the fullness of the sense of our divine filiation: and with this realisation comes peace. The living consciousness of our divine filiation will give you that serenity, because this characteristic feature of our spirit was born with the Work, and took shape in 1931. They were difficult times, from a human point of view, but even so I was quite sure of the impossible, this impossibility which you can now see as an accomplished reality. I felt God acting within me with overriding force, filling my heart and bringing to my lips this tender invocation: Abba! Pater! I was out in the street, in a tram: being out in the street is no hindrance for our contemplative dialogue; for us, the hustle and bustle of the world is a place for prayer.55
##
55 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1959

## SACRIFICE AND APOSTOLATE {#cuaderno.5.6}

The prophets had repeatedly announced the Passion and Death of Christ. The chosen people, however, having gradually become insensitive to divine Revelation, were awaiting a human redemption, without cross and ignominy. That is why, when Jesus Christ himself tells the people listening to him that only on being lifted up on high, only in dying on the gibbet of the Cross will he draw all things to himself, they reply: We have heard from the law that the Christ 1 remains forever. How can you say the Son of man must be lifted up? Not even the Apostles understood it. When Our Lord tells them that he must go to Jerusalem to suffer and die, Peter, who through an interior illumination from the Father had just confessed the divinity of Christ, tries to dissuade him and thus merits the harshest reproach and the clearest teaching on the need for sacrifice: “Get behind me, Satan! You are a hindrance to me; for you are not on the side of God, but of men”. Then Jesus told his disciples, “If any man would come after me, let him deny himself and take up his cross and follow me. For whoever would save his life will lose it, and whoever loses his life for my sake, will find it.”2 Later, when his Passion was almost imminent, Our Lord, taking the Twelve, said to them, “Behold, we are going up to Jerusalem, and everything that is written of the Son of man by the prophets will be

1 Jn 12:34 2 Mt 16:23-25 68 The apostolic mission

accomplished. For he will be delivered to the Gentiles, and will be mocked and shamefully treated and spat upon, they will scourge him and kill him, and on the third day he will rise”. But they understood none of these things; this saying was hidden from them, and they did not grasp what was said.3 Only with the coming of the Holy Spirit upon the apostolic College would a powerful supernatural light enlighten their minds so that they could in some way understand this mysterious need for the Cross. Then Peter, and with him the whole Church, would teach: Do not be surprised at the fiery ordeal which comes upon you to prove you, as though something strange were happening to you. But rejoice in so far as you share Christ’s sufferings.4

The redemption, work of the Cross The preaching of the Cross, then as now, was to go on surprising men who do not live by faith or whose supernatural view of life is weak. For Jews demand signs and Greeks seek wisdom, but we preach Christ crucified, a stumbling block to Jews and folly to Gentiles, but to those who are called, both Jews and Greeks, Christ the power of God and the wisdom of God.5 To the faithful of Philippi St Paul would write some of his harshest words but at the same time one of his most moving epistles: Brethren, join in imitating me, and mark those who so live as you have an example in us. For many, of whom I have often told you and now tell you even with tears, live as enemies of the Cross of Christ. Their end is destruction, their god is the belly, and they glory in their shame, with minds set on earthly things.6

3 Lk 18:31-34 4 1 Pet 4:12-13 5 1 Cor 1:22-24 6 Phil 3:17-19

Sacrifice and apostolate 69

Our redemption was wrought and is being wrought on the Cross of Christ. This brought St Augustine to say that “there was no better way to save us from our misery.”7 St Thomas explains: “That man should be delivered by Christ’s Passion was in keeping both with his mercy and his justice. With his justice, because by his Passion Christ made satisfaction for the sin of the human race, and man was freed by Christ’s justice with mercy, because since man was by himself unable to satisfy for the sin of all human nature ..., God gave him his Son to do so.”8 In Christ’s Passion and Death on the Cross, God’s goodness shines forth with blinding splendour in harmonious unity with his justice; and it shows the immense wickedness of sin which called for such reparation.

The sacrifice of the Mass perpetuates that of the Cross The work of the Redemption continues in the Church. In order to apply to all men the merits of Christ and the freedom he won for us, the Church places at the centre of her entire life the Sacrifice of the Mass, which perpetuates that of the Cross and unites our souls with the redeeming Passion. “The other sacraments, and indeed all ecclesiastical ministries and works of apostolate are bound up with the Eucharist and are directed towards it. For in the most blessed Eucharist is contained the whole spiritual good of the Church, namely Christ himself ...”9 Christus Passus, Christ in the state of his Passion. For this reason Christ “appointed certain men as ministers ... (who) were to hold in the community of the faithful the sacred power of Order, that of offering sacrifice and forgiving sins, and were to exercise the priestly office publicly on behalf of men in the name of Christ.”10

7 St Augustine, De Trinitate, 12, 1, 5 8 St Thomas, S. Th., III, q.46, a.1 ad 3 9 Second Vatican Council, Decree Presbyterorum Ordinis, 5 10 Ibid., 2 70 The apostolic mission

The whole People of God together with the priests offer this wonderful and perpetual Sacrifice of the Church. “The whole redeemed city, that is to say, the congregation or community of the saints, offers to God the universal sacrifice through the great High Priest, who offered himself in his Passion for us, that we might be members of this glorious head.”11 Together with this joint participation of the whole Body of Christ, however, there must also be an apostolic action, a co-redeeming and personal action by all Christians. “From the fact of their union with Christ the head flows the laymen’s right and duty to be apostles. Inserted as they are in the Mystical Body of Christ by Baptism and strengthened by the power of the Holy Spirit in Confirmation, it is by the Lord himself that they are assigned to the apostolate. If they are consecrated a kingly priesthood and a holy nation,12 it is in order that they may in all their actions offer spiritual sacrifices and bear witness to Christ all the world over. Charity, which is, as it were, the soul of the apostolate, is given them and nourished in them by the sacraments, the Eucharist above all.”13 This doctrine on the right and duty of every Christian in the apostolate has been preached by our Father since 1928. Furthermore this spirit, union with Christ in the Sacrifice of the Mass as the source and root of all apostolic effectiveness, is what our Father has summed up hundreds of times telling us that the Mass is the centre and root of our interior life,14 and consequently of all our effectiveness.

We are co-redeemers This sacramental union with the Sacrifice of Jesus Christ, which is the source of all apostolate, demands and, at the same time, fosters each

11 St Augustine, De civitate Dei 10, 6 12 Cf 1 Pet 2:4-10 13 Second Vatican Council, Decree Apostolicam actuositatem, 3 14 From our Father, Obras, August 1965, p.13

Sacrifice and apostolate 71

one’s personal sacrifice. The Redemption is still being wrought, and you and I are coredeemers. It is worth risking our whole life and being willing to suffer, for love, in order to further the things of God and help him redeem the world, that is to say, to co-redeem.15 If the cause of the Redemption was obtained through Christ’s Sacrifice, then with sacrifice its effects will be achieved in souls. “Christ by his Passion delivered us from our sins causally, that is, by creating the cause of our deliverance, by which all sins whatsoever, past, present, or future, could be forgiven: somewhat as if a doctor were to make up a medicine by which any kind of future ill could be cured,”16 but whose application depended on an action similar to that which was necessary to obtain the medicine in the first place. The efficacy of the suffering of Christ in souls requires our cooperation, thus also making us in everything alter Christus. In the midst of your ordinary activities you should strive to make your whole life a continuous act of praise to God: a constant prayer and reparation, petition and sacrifice for all men. And all this in intimate and assiduous union with Christ Jesus in the Holy Sacrifice of the Altar.17 Our Father teaches us that Our Lord’s Redemption on the Cross is sufficient, and superabundant, but he treats us as intelligent and free beings and has so ordained that in a mysterious way we should fulfil in our flesh, in our life, that which is lacking in his Passion pro corpore eius, quod est Ecclesia (Col 1:24).18 Commenting on this same passage of Scripture, St Thomas says: “We must understand that Christ and the Church are a mystical person, whose head is Christ and whose body are all the just: individual members of it.19 In his

15 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1959 16 St Thomas, S. Th., III, q.49, a.1 ad 3 17 From our Father, Letter 28 March 1955 18 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1959 19 Cf 1 Cor 12:27 72 The apostolic mission

predestination God declared what merits there were to be for the whole Church, both in the Head and in the members, in the same way that he predestined the number of the elect. Among these merits we find especially the sufferings of the saints.”20 Our Father tells us the same thing: Within this mystical body, in Opus Dei, you and I, with the willing fulfilment of our duties, even though they be difficult, with the dominion of ourselves, with that smile that at times is a mortification, we will obtain abundant grace from God for other souls.21

Effectiveness of mortification There is no other way to become identified with Christ than the Cross, and this holds true not only for personal sanctity but also for apostolic effectiveness. If we are children, then heirs, heirs of God and fellow heirs with Christ, provided we suffer with him in order that we may also be glorified with him.22 There is no other way. “Christ, who is the principal heir, obtains the inheritance of glory through his Passion. Was it not necessary for Christ to suffer in order to enter into his glory? We have no right to receive this same inheritance in an easier way. It is also necessary for us to attain that inheritance through suffering.”23 The apostolic fruit that we are called by vocation to obtain, which will be our glory later, but is now our responsibility, depends on this. We cannot aspire to be co-redeemers with Christ if we are not ready to make amends for sins as he did ... We want to offer our lives, our dedication without reserve and without limitations, as expiation for our sins, for the sins of all men, our brothers; for the sins committed in all ages, and for those that will be committed until the end of the world.24

20 St Thomas, Super Epistolam ad Colos. lectura 1, 6 21 From our Father 22 Rom 8:17 23 St Thomas, Super Epistolam ad Rom. lectura 3 24 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932

Sacrifice and apostolate 73

Our Lord had already clearly told us, Truly, truly, I say to you, unless a grain of wheat falls into the earth and dies, it remains alone; but if it dies, it bears much fruit.25 Like the grain of wheat, my sons, we need to die in order to bear fruit. You and I do not want to be alone. We want to extend our family; we want to leave a deep and clear furrow. That is why we must launch out on the spiritual plane, elevating all things human and the men who work in them. In order to be apostles we must bear Christ crucified within us, as St Paul would have it.26 Blessed Paul VI recalled this while he was preaching during a solemn Stations of the Cross. He pointed to a present-day danger that tends to obscure this doctrine. “If we correctly discern the orientation now being given to our modern education we will note that it leads to a certain hedonism, to the easy life, to a certain attempt to eliminate the cross from our concerns ... How often we also try to eliminate, in our interpretation of the Gospel, those pages of Our Lord’s Passion to take from it only what makes our life beautiful, serene, poetic, lyric, resplendent and spiritual! That bloody, tragic page of the Cross terrorises us, and we want never to open it ... Also, in these modern times, after the Council, haven’t we often felt the temptation to believe that the moment has come to convert Christianity into something easy, to make it comfortable, without any sacrifice; to make it conform to the comfortable, elegant and normal ways of the rest of men and to the worldly way of life? But this cannot be! Christianity cannot do away with the cross. Christian life is not possible without the strong and great burden of duty. It is not possible without this passage, this Paschal mystery of sacrifice. If we were to try to take this away from our life, we would be deluding ourselves and we would weaken Christianity. We would have transformed Christianity

25 Jn 12:24 26 From our Father, Meditation, Holy Week, 13 April 1954, in Crónica, February 1962, p.17 74 The apostolic mission

into a soft and comfortable interpretation of life, while on the other hand, our Master, Our Lord, has told us that it is necessary to carry the cross with its bitterness and sorrows, and with its absolute and, if necessary, its tragic demands.”27 As our Father tells us, we have to keep the sense of sin and generous reparation alive in the face of the false optimism of those enemies of the cross of Christ (Phil 3:18), who judge everything in terms of progress and human endeavour. These people commit the serious sin of forgetting sin. Some think they have done away with it already. They do not realise that it is part of the economy of salvation for the grain of wheat to be buried in the earth and die in order to bear fruit (cf John 12:24).28

Denying oneself True apostolate demands personal renunciation, a sacrificing dedication, and the holocaust of oneself. “Since Christ in his mission from the Father is the fountain and source of the whole apostolate of the Church, the success of the lay apostolate depends upon the laity’s living union with Christ ..., for they remember the words of the Lord, If any man would come after me, let him deny himself and take up his cross and follow me29.”30 The true apostle should be capable of dying to himself precisely because he is motivated by love: Greater love has no man than this, that a man lay down his life for his friends.31 A person who knows nothing of what sacrifice means, knows nothing of what to love means either. For love is not self-centred, love leads one gently and sweetly to the most complete abnegation of self. This abnegation

27 Blessed Paul VI, Address, 8 September 1966 28 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1959 29 Mt 16:24 30 Second Vatican Council, Decree Apostolicam actuositatem, 4 31 Jn 15:13

Sacrifice and apostolate 75

purifies, cleans, and clarifies the soul; it makes it divine. There are certain foretastes here on earth of the resurrection which comes through death, in the life of the spirit and in the process of sanctification. This spiritual resurrection is followed by a Pentecost filled with apostolic fruit. The condition for bearing fruit is abnegation, renouncing all personal glory, not seeking ourselves in the apostolate, and not claiming any human reward for ourselves. Along with this, which constitutes the primary condition, we need abundant mortification of the will and the senses, a prudent but generous corporal penance and the Cross of Christ. But we must avoid that other false cross which comes from rebellion against pain and renunciation. An apostle is a Christian who feels himself grafted onto Christ, identified with Christ through baptism; qualified to fight for Christ through his participation in the royal, prophetic and priestly functions of Christ which enable him to guide men to God, to teach them the truth of the Gospel, and to coredeem them with his prayer and his expiation.32 Our Lady is co-redemptrix in a special and most eminent way, and, therefore, she shared like no one else in the Passion and Death of Christ, and she is the Mother of salvation for all souls. Marvel at Mary’s courage: at the foot of the Cross, with the greatest of human sorrows – there is no sorrow like her sorrow – filled with fortitude. And ask her for that same strength, so that you too can remain beside the Cross.33 The consequence will be this: Only then will you live for Christ, with Christ and in Christ; only then will you be an apostle.34
##
32 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932 33 The Way, 508 34 The Way, 929

## THE SALT OF THE EARTH {#cuaderno.5.7}

Vos estis sal terrae,1 you are the salt of the earth. In saying this Jesus Christ clearly sets out the mission of the apostle in the world: to keep from corruption and to give flavour to what is insipid on this earth. It must not become just one more activity amongst many in a Christian life, but rather that whole life should be imbued with it. The calling demands total conversion, such that ever after the apostolate becomes a man’s very life, his be-all and end-all, whatever his conditions of character, environment, opportunity, or natural enthusiasm ...

Preserving from corruption All of us in the Work realise that we are apostles carrying out an imperative command of Christ.2 God has chosen to pick us out to be salt of the earth. Ego elegi vos et posui vos, ut eatis et fructum afferatis,3 I chose you and appointed you to go throughout the whole world and bear fruit. From the very moment when the choice fell on us in particular, the apostolate has become the mark of life in our soul, the warrant for exercising our faculties, just as the whole purpose of salt is to preserve and flavour. You are the salt of the earth; but if salt has lost its taste, how shall its saltness be restored? It is no longer good for

1 Mt 5:13 2 From our Father, Instruction, 19 March 1934 3 Jn 15:16

The salt of the earth 77

anything except to be thrown out and trodden under foot by men.4 We have been launched into the world by our Lord to immunise all 5 and to preserve them from evil. This is a mortals from corruption glorious and difficult mission that demands a deep and constant sacrifice. We are to be dissolved in the world in order to save it which implies that we are not to weaken or become worldly. ‘There’s no denying the influence of environment’, you have told me. And I have to answer: Quite. That is why you have to be formed in such a way that you can carry your own environment about with you in a natural manner, and so give your own ‘tone’ to the society in which you live.6 In his desire that the earth should not weaken the salt, and that the salt may fulfil its mission on the earth, our Father asks for his children that fortitude of spirit that makes them carry in themselves our own environment.7 We must always be determined in our conduct, strongly intransigent with evil; our heart must be filled with the fortitude of Christ in order to save men: Viriliter agite, et confortetur cor vestrum.8 The more corrupt our surroundings, the greater must our zeal be, just as the presence of a virus excites an organism’s defence system into an energetic response. When faced with a world separated from God, we must feel ourselves devoured by apostolic zeal. “Who is the one who is devoured by zeal for the house of God? The one who tries to correct all that he might see there of a perverse nature, who is anxious to amend it.”9 This eagerness to save will never be languid and lukewarm, but rather fervent and effective. For at times, it must cure with a certain firmness. It is in this way that the surgeon “wounds” and

4 Mt 5:13 5 From our Father, Instruction, 19 March 1934 6 The Way, 376 7 From our Father 8 Ps 30:25 9 St Augustine, De perfectione iustitiae hominis 10, 9 78 The apostolic mission

nobody feels offended by the pain he produces. The apostle has all the more right to do so because he does not just keep a few years of more or less pleasing life going but secures an absolutely happy eternity. He may not always act on the wishes of the patient – who is always a minor, supernaturally speaking – but he acts on an imperative command of God, who is Father both of the apostle and of the patient. The apostle’s manner also, full of charity, will be persuasive and encouraging, but without compulsion, because holy steadfastness is not intolerance.10 We cannot expect the medicine will always be easily swallowed, for we know we are in the world to teach men that the gate is narrow that leads to life.11 A steep path is not easy or pleasing to the flesh, to the spirit of the world, to an inordinate desire for pleasure. Those, however, who take it, who force themselves to step out with determination on the path to God, will end up understanding that first pain we had to cause in order to lift them out of their collapse.

Giving supernatural taste We must preserve from corruption and give flavour. Giving flavour is to fill souls with Christ, to enkindle in them desires for sanctity and love of God. To do apostolate and proselytism is an essential part of our vocation. We must carry our divine life to the world, in a natural manner, so that a testimony of the supernatural is given to souls. ‘And in a paganised or pagan environment when my life clashes with its surroundings, won’t my naturalness seem artificial?’ you ask me. And I reply: Undoubtedly your life will clash with theirs; and that contrast – faith confirmed by works! – is exactly the naturalness I ask of you.12

10 The Way, 396 11 Mt 7:14 12 The Way, 380

The salt of the earth 79

Our naturalness is the same as that of salt which gives flavour, without forcing itself, as something proper to its nature. If salt were to fail in such an essential requirement of its nature it could only be because it had been debased or had lost its edge, because in fact it was no longer salt. Naturalness here is a natural outpouring of its own life, and cannot, therefore, be conditioned by the world about it. Our zeal, the impetus of our apostolate, is not the result of the impetuousness of youth, of certain favourable conditions in the environment, or of any human circumstances. It arises from the supernatural life of the apostle’s soul, from the love of Christ that is in his heart. It has in its favour the guarantee of success, of a success that perhaps the apostle himself will not see, but which is as certain as the word of God. The obstacles that you will encounter in spreading your madness to other apostles are not hidden from me. Some may seem insuperable ... but inter medium montium pertransibunt aquae, the supernatural spirit of the Work and the impetus of your zeal will pass through the mountains and you will overcome those obstacles.13 The apostle should be ready for a clash between his impetus and the human standards of his environment, the all-too-human prudence of the men around him, and at times their judgements and their morals. He must be ready to be considered a fool for love of Christ.14 Although he may clash with the conventions of the world, the apostle must however be vibrant since “the discourse of him who teaches is useless, if he cannot show the fire of love.”15 Generally it is not difficult to enkindle in oneself this supernatural enthusiasm when faced with opinions and conduct openly against, and offensive to, the doctrine of the Church, or with one who declares him-

13 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934 14 1 Cor 4:10 15 St Gregory the Great, Moralia 30, 5 80 The apostolic mission

self an enemy, and acts as one. It is perhaps not as easy to overcome the insidious temptation of a lukewarm environment, such as when one lives with men who do not deny dogmas but who ignore Jesus Christ in their social life, in their work, in their ideas – men who judge everything from an exclusively human point of view, which may appear reasonable but be in fact supernaturally false. This spirit of the world can lead the apostle to be worldly. Quietly, without it being hardly noticed, it can make him cold, chain him up, and dampen his apostolic zeal.

To be vibrant with the spirit of the Work The source of true apostolic impetus is interior life. Although indispensable, doctrine is not enough. Love, too, is necessary. First, because doctrine will otherwise be ineffective. Then, too, because a perfect right intention guarantees the preservation of doctrinal purity. Because they lacked this right intention, St Paul says of the learned men of his time: dicentes enim se esse sapientes, stulti facti sunt;16 while boasting to be wise, they ended up being fools. Frequently when the heart becomes cold, doctrine is falsified. To be able to act like salt, we must be salt. In order to preserve from corruption and give flavour, we must have interior life. Personal sanctity, the struggle to obtain it, is the basis of apostolic effectiveness. Our Father tells us so: But to fulfil this Will of Christ our King, you must have much interior life: be Eucharistic souls, manly!, prayerful souls. Because only in this way will you be vibrant with the vibration that the spirit of the Work demands, so that those who are in contact with you in your profession and in your social activity repeat many times that comment of Cleophas and his companion in Emmaus:

16 Rom 1:22

The salt of the earth 81

nonne cor nostrum ardens erat in nobis, dum loqueretur in via? Did not our hearts burn within us while he talked to us on the road? (Luke 24:32).17 Interior life urges the apostle on – caritas enim Christi urget nos,18 the charity of Christ urges us. It makes us overcome all personal weakness and all human calculation and it makes us feel continually the 19 Your loving spur of that vae enim mihi est, si non evangelizavero! apostolate, our Father emphasises, must be the overflow of your life ‘within’.20 Our apostolic vocation, however, is of such a kind, and our apostolate so much part and parcel of our life itself, that our salt gets better as it salts. The more it preserves and flavours, the better salt it becomes. We are salt of the earth that must dissolve itself without disappearing, that has to preserve from corruption and give flavour without losing its strength. This salt impregnates everything it touches, immunises against evil and puts the strong tone of Christ into the staleness of the earth.
##
17 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934 18 2 Cor 5:14 19 1 Cor 9:16 20 The Way, 961

## INSTRUMENTS OF APOSTOLATE {#cuaderno.5.8}

Since we received our calling to the Work, we have been aware of the supernatural character of our way. Faced with the evident disproportion between our meagre strength and the mission that God entrusted to us, perhaps these words welled up in our hearts: Alas, alas, Lord God, I am but a child that has never learned to speak.1 However, Our Lord replied by infusing into our souls an ardent zeal for his glory and a holy daring that remains undaunted before all obstacles. But the Lord said to me, do not say, I am only a youth; to all to whom I send you, you shall go, and whatever I command you, you shall speak.2 When we do apostolate we must never forget that we are carrying out something supernatural for which God wants to make use of us, with all our miseries and mistakes. For just as we men write with a pen, so the Lord writes with the leg of a table to make it clear that it is he who is writing. That is what is so incredible; that is what is marvellous.3 This calls for prayer and a deep desire of sacrifice on our part; we are to use the supernatural means without which we can do nothing. To spread the Kingdom of God the only thing really necessary4 is to trust fully in God’s almighty power and live a life of faith, hope, and love. Jesus taught us this when he sent his disciples through the cities

1 Jer 1:6 2 Jer 1:7 3 From our Father, Letter 14 September 1951 4 Cf Lk 10:42

Instruments of apostolate 83

and villages of Palestine: Take nothing for your journey, no staff, nor bag, nor bread, nor money; neither have two tunics.5 That is how they must set out, without anything, to make it clear that neither the triumphs nor the miracles nor the conversions are their own. It will not be by dint of their personal qualities or efforts that sinners repent and prepare themselves to receive the Kingdom of God. Opus Dei also began this way: without any human means. It depended exclusively on supernatural means. In 1934 our Father wrote, The means are the same as those of Peter and Paul ..., Dominic and Francis ..., Ignatius and Xavier: the Crucifix and the Gospel;6 because to begin new works of apostolate in the early days, I sent my sons out as Our Lord sent his disciples: just as poor as they were, with the blessing that Our Lord gives them from heaven and the one that I give them on earth.7 The Work was built on prayer, our Founder insists. There was only youthfulness, joy and the certainty of doing God’s will. Everything has gone ahead with prayer and true penance. There were no human means.8 This absolute poverty of the early times has often happened again at the beginning of new undertakings. In our apostolate we always have clearly in mind that the supernatural means come first and are the only things we cannot do without.

The human means If necessary, we begin as we have always begun: with absolutely nothing. We start with whatever we can. That is very beautiful. But now that we can manage better, I realise that our Lord does not want

5 Lk 9:3 6 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934 7 From our Father, Instruction, 8 December 1941 8 From our Father 84 The apostolic mission

us to do things like that. Things will be done with the same spirit, with the same eagerness of hope and love with which we have always begun our work everywhere, with the same, not more, spirit since that is not possible, but now with more human means.9 Not to use human means when they can be obtained, always without losing sight of their instrumental character, would be a wrong interpretation of Our Lord’s words, seek his kingdom, and these things shall be yours as well.10 For grace does not supplant nature, nor must we ask for extraordinary displays of divine providence, when God himself places the appropriate human means within our reach. “If a man, instead of doing what he can, were to be content with awaiting God’s assistance, he would seem to tempt God,”11 and consequently grace would cease to make his works supernaturally fruitful. God demands an effective and dedicated cooperation from his instruments, from his apostles. A part of that cooperation is the employment of the most suitable human means to carry out the task, without sinking into the comfortable passivity of those who rashly abuse divine providence and wait for extraordinary help, which God has no reason to give if we do not use the human means within our reach.12 Christ showed his disciples this need to use human means for the apostolate shortly before his Passion, after he had taught them that first lesson of abandonment to divine providence. When I sent you out with no purse or bag or sandals, did you lack anything? They said, Nothing. He said to them, But now, let him who has a purse take it, and likewise a bag. And let him who has no sword sell his mantle and buy one.13 In order to carry out his divine mission Jesus himself frequently chose to make use

9 From our Father, Get-together, 1 January 1959 10 Lk 12:31 11 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.53, a.4 ad 1 12 From our Father, Letter 31 May 1943 13 Lk 22:35-37

Instruments of apostolate 85

of earthly means: a few loaves of bread and some little fish, a bit of clay, the riches of the pious women who followed him: He was journeying through towns and villages, preaching and proclaiming the goods news of the Kingdom of God, writes St Luke, and the Twelve were with him, and also some women ... who provided for them out of their means.14 From the very day of Pentecost the first Christians, without forgetting that here we have no lasting city,15 used the human means at their disposal in the service of the Gospel: they sold their possessions and goods and distributed to all, according as any had need.16 Financial cooperation between the different Christian communities and material assistance for the spreading of the Kingdom of Christ is an old tradition in the Church (cf Acts 4:32-37),17 our Father writes. Listen to what the Apostle of the Gentiles tells us in the First Epistle to the Corinthians (16:1-2): de collectis autem, quae fiunt in sanctos, sicut ordinavi ecclesiis Galatiae, ita et vos facite. Per unam sabbati unusquisque vestrum apud se seponat recondens quod ei bene placuerit, ut non, cum venero, tunc collectae fiant: Now concerning the contribution for the saints: as I have directed the churches of Galatia, so you also are to do. On the first day of the week, each of you is to put something aside and store it up, as he may prosper, so the contributions need not be made when I come.18 When the Philippians, on another occasion, sent him the financial help he needed, he writes; I rejoice in the Lord greatly that now at length you have revived your concern for me; you were indeed concerned for me, but you had no opportunity. Not that I complain of want; for I have learned, whatever state I am, to be content. I know

14 Lk 8:1-3 15 Heb 13:14 16 Acts 2:45 17 From our Father 18 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950 86 The apostolic mission

how to be abased, and I know how to abound; in any and all circumstances I have learned the secret of facing plenty and hunger, abundance and want. I can do all things in him who strengthens me. Yet it was kind of you to share my trouble. And you Philippians yourselves know that in the beginning of the gospel, when I left Macedonia, no church entered into partnership with me in giving and receiving except you only; for even in Thessalonica you sent me help once and again.19

Apostolic needs In our case there are also other reasons for using legitimate human means in our work with souls. In the first place there is the secular character of our apostolates. No one should be surprised that Opus Dei needs material resources for its work, our Father states. Since it carries out its supernatural task of sanctification among men and for men, it must also use a minimum of material resources, just like all other associations without exception, artistic, athletic, cultural, religious, etc.20 Furthermore, these human means, provided frequently by non- Catholics or Catholics separated from the Church, are in themselves a splendid occasion for apostolate. They are part of our apostolate of not giving which is most effective in bringing many souls who are far from God closer to him. By asking financial help and hours of professional work from these persons for the service of the apostolic enterprises we undertake, activities which always have a human effectiveness as well, we bring them into the heart of our work and offer them the opportunity of being God’s arm for the carrying out of his Work among men.21

19 Phil 4:10-16 20 From our Father, Letter 31 May 1954 21 From our Father Instruments of apostolate 87

Our Father has told us, If a person cannot materially support himself on some piece of land, though it be small and of poor quality, it is unlikely he will be able to do a permanent apostolic work.22 While we have recourse to God as if everything depended on him, we should at the same time strive as if everything depended on us, trying to get the human means necessary for our work with souls. The most important human means is work. We should not tempt God by demanding that he work miracles when we can and should use noble and upright professional work to get the material resources needed.23 It is necessary to live a life of work in Opus Dei, for supernatural reasons and to get the means indispensable for supporting ourselves and the apostolic undertakings, to such an extent that our Father has determined that all who come to the Work are to be asked what work they depend on to support themselves. Even if they are young and are studying, they can always do something: give classes, make translations, and other such jobs.24 Opus Dei belongs to all its members, and it is just that the joyful duty of supporting the apostolic works financially belongs to each and every one. Numeraries and Associates fulfil this duty by making available everything they earn from their professional work, Supernumeraries with their monthly contributions, made according to each one’s financial possibilities and generosity. Some will be able to give a great deal because they have a great deal, others very little because they have only a little. All, however, are asked to take on the responsibility of carrying the Work forward, according to their means. Calling to mind the Gospel passage about the poor widow’s mite, our Father wrote: Those sons and daughters of mine who can give very little financial help should never worry. Perhaps their constant effort

22 From our Father, Obras, June 1959, p.9 23 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, note 196 24 From our Father, Letter 15 October 1948 88 The apostolic mission

is more unselfish and generous than that of all the others: they certainly do not give from what is left over because they have nothing left over. I am certain that on seeing these gifts, Our Lord’s eyes will once again light up with divine affection.25 Besides their monthly contribution, the Supernumeraries can find a thousand different ways to help the apostolic works financially. You were asking me about practical ways to fulfil your duty with our Mother the Work. I have been shown them by your generosity: from that aristocratic lady, noble in blood and in spirit, who found it possible to hand over her own palace in very hard times of calumny and persecution, to some very poor farming people, parents of a young girl in domestic service, who sell their donkey and joyfully send the money; from that good friend from South America who, with the agreement of his family, makes one of our apostolic works a partner in his business, a partner which does not share in any loss, to the children of a Supernumerary brother of yours who send the money they received as gifts on the day of their First Communion; from the person who sends furniture in order to set up a house, to the one who pays all the expenses for the poor car which is indispensable for our work.26

Development of the apostolic works The growth of the Work is great, and great also are the needs that this growth brings with it. Opus Dei and its children are not in need of money because they work, each one of them in their profession, and they more than support themselves. But regarding our corporate works, the more we are helped the better we will serve souls.27 In spite of all our efforts, it is not possible for us to supply all the material demands of the

25 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950 26 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950 27 From our Father, Letter 31 May 1943

Instruments of apostolate 89

apostolate. No matter how much we secure the financial base of the Work with our own work, our Father has told us, considering our extraordinary growth, we will always need the help of people who love God and are capable of understanding the happiness of putting their money in the bank of heaven.28 This is what the Co-operators and friends of the Work do who help us generously with their alms, their work, and above all, their faithful affection and their prayer if they are Christians. There are very many who have affection for us; who are grateful for our fraternal friendship; who understand us because we also understand them; who help us generously to carry out our apostolic works. The sincere and loyal friendship of these souls is a great joy to us.29 Tell those wonderful friends of ours, whom I have called co-operators, that it is good that they should coordinate with you in order to make available to the Work from the start the necessary instruments and facilitate the apostolic task. With their own money and other people’s, whether Catholics or not, with the assurance of a prudent financial interest and without risking losses since the material instrument will always be in their hands, they will participate in our spiritual benefits and obtain for themselves and their families God’s greatest blessings.30 The human effectiveness and prestige of the work that you will achieve in those corporate apostolates will also motivate many upright people to help you, even though they may be very far from our Catholic faith.31 Thus arises the phenomenon, unique in the history of the Church, that non-Catholics, and even non-Christians, help in undertakings with a supernatural and apostolic aim. Through your contact with them, which is filled with sincere charity, they will begin to love

28 From our Father 29 From our Father, Letter 14 February 1950 30 From our Father, Instruction, 8 December 1941 31 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 90 The apostolic mission

the Work and you will accomplish an effective task of apostolate ad fidem with them.32 In these ways we obtain the human means, nets for our catch of souls, which are indispensable for the carrying out of a deep and effective apostolate. Nevertheless, we should also try to have the government “subsidise” our corporate works, since this is in no way contrary to justice or right order. All governments normally provide subsidies for people who run educational or charitable works, and other such activities. Thus to receive help from them cannot be regarded as a privilege, something we would detest, but rather a well-founded right. For with these apostolic undertakings we form young people, help the needy, prepare good citizens, and carry out other similar tasks that are of service and benefit to the whole of society. Moreover, these works of ours, which we carry out with an apostolic aim, relieve the public purse of enormous expenditure which the State would have to fund.33 Indispensable to the apostolic undertakings we carry out is the generous cooperation of people, Catholics or not, who with their prayer, their work, or their money strengthen our effectiveness.34 This cooperation is especially important in the apostolic activities of the St Raphael work. We are poor and we will always be so, my dear sons and daughters. I have told you on other occasions that the centres where we work with young people are born with a congenital defect, which consists in the absence of financial means together with the need for a dignified oratory, for a Domestic Staff set up in such a way that it can work well, and for work-space made available to an activity that is not financially solvent: that of St Raphael.35 It is only just, and very useful for the apostolate, that the support of the centres of St Raphael,

32 Ibid. 33 From our Father, Letter 14 February 1950 34 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940 35 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942

Instruments of apostolate 91

at least in part, be borne by the boys or girls themselves, and by their families, by their parents, for we should always try to get to know them and get them to help ... Therefore, apart from the large or small donations that the young people or their families may want to place in the hands of the Director or the Secretary of the house, in a discreet place there should be a collection box, so that each person, without ostentation and without being noticed, may put in whatever he can, according to his spirit of generosity.36 In helping the work of St Raphael, the Supernumeraries and Co-operators will also find the opportunity of channelling their zeal for apostolate. They can make houses available for outings and special courses, provide scholarships and other assistance for Summer courses and offer means of transport, schools, books and magazines, places for sports, etc.

An ever-present problem Regardless of what we may already have, we will always need more human means to look after the different apostolates because our apostolate is a sea without shores. Our Father faced with this wonderful reality comments: We are and will always be poor, because we will never have enough money to spread the work as fast as Our Lord wants. We are being asked to go to so many places where, were it not for the lack of financial means, we could go 37 Thus the human means which we manage to obtain can immediately! never be too many. There is only one thing we must ensure, and it is that they never lose their instrumental character, that no one become attached to them and thereby tarnish the brightness of our poverty, which will always be magnificent, without any external show.38 For if what

36 Ibid. 37 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, note 200 38 From our Father, Instruction, 8 December 1941 92 The apostolic mission

is instrumental and subordinate were to be considered the most important thing, if abandonment in the hands of God were to be supplanted by exclusive trust in human means, these same means would automatically lose their effectiveness and would lead us, not to sanctity and apostolate, but to the sterility and ineffectiveness which does not come from God. If, on the other hand, we always bear in mind that human means are instruments of apostolate, necessary but only instrumental in the final analysis, we will not have to wait long for the supernatural fruit. We will sanctify the human means themselves, devoting them to God’s service and to spreading his kingdom. We will sanctify ourselves personally when we use them, because their just use requires the exercise of all the theological and moral virtues. We will sanctify others, spreading the work to new souls and offering those who help us the opportunity of using their goods uprightly: make friends for yourselves by means of unrighteous mammon, these are Christ’s words, so that when it fails they may receive you into the eternal habitations.39 St Cyprian writes: “Use them, but for salutary things and good works; use them for those things that God prescribes and the Lord indicates. Let the poor know you as rich and the needy know you as opulent. With your patrimony support God, feed Christ.”40 Human means, instruments of apostolate, are an instrument that is in practice necessary for the apostolate of doctrine which we have the obligation of doing. We will spare no sacrifice in gladly fulfilling this duty. Quaerite primum regnum Dei, et iustitiam eius: et haec omnia adiicientur vobis (Luke 12:31); with an upright intention seek the fulfilment of God’s will and his glory in the service of all souls, and we will not lack the necessary means.41
##
39 Lk 16:9 40 St Cyprian, De habitu virginum 11 41 From our Father, Letter 2 October 1939

## THE LEAVEN AND THE MASS {#cuaderno.5.9}

Christ Our Lord founded his Holy Church with two characteristics inseparable character of mystical and juridical, invisible and visible society, moved and animated by the Holy Spirit and hierarchically governed, rooted in the deepest part of each soul and with an external and public existence. “Christ wanted the community founded by him to be a perfect society of its kind and endowed with all legal and social elements, in order to perpetuate the divine work of the redemption in this world.”1 Inseparable from this, in intimate harmony and continuity, with a view both to extending his work and intensifying it, he also wanted his own people to carry on a continuous and vital action as leaven.

The leaven of the apostolate The kingdom of heaven, Our Lord once said, is like leaven, which a woman took and hid in three measures of meal, till it was all leavened.2 His first followers were not to be frightened by the littleness of the beginning. The whole world, both souls and institutions, was to be inwardly transformed in order to establish the saving kingdom of Christ Jesus in the fullness of its power. “See the wisdom of Our Lord: Do not ask me – he seemed to want to say to his disciples – what are we,

1 Pius XII, Encyclical Mystici Corporis, 29 June 1943 2 Mt 13:33 94 The apostolic mission

twelve men, lost among such a multitude, going to do? This is precisely what is going to make your strength stand out. For being few and lost among such a multitude, you do not run away. That is just like the leaven that ferments the mass not only when it is surrounded by it, but when it is within it. For Our Lord did not say that the woman simply put in the leaven but that she hid it in the mass ... Just as the leaven is buried and does not disappear, but little by little goes about transforming everything into its own quality, exactly the same thing will happen with the preaching of the Gospel.”3 The Apostles, equipped with the divine institution, with the hierarchical priesthood they had received and the primacy of Peter, with the treasure of the sacraments and the power to administer them, with the deposit of doctrine and infallibility in interpreting and teaching it, and filled with love, spread out after Pentecost to every part of the world. “Thus Christianity quickly found its way not only into private homes and into the army, but also into the senate and even into the imperial palace. ‘We are a people of yesterday and yet we have filled every place belonging to you, cities, islands, castles, towns, assemblies, your very camp, your tribes, companies, palaces, senate, forum.’4 So that the Christian faith, when once it became lawful to make public profession of the Gospel, appeared in a great number of cities, not like an infant crying in its cradle, but already grown up and full of vigour.”5 Today, within the Holy Roman Church, the Body of Christ a militia, old like the Gospel and like the Gospel new, is springing up by means of a divine and also universal impulse.6 It is an army at the service of the Church for extending and intensifying her work, a divine instrument of sanctity and apostolate. The Work of God has to spread

3 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae, 46, 2 4 Tertullian, Apologia 37 5 Leo XIII, Encyclical Immortale Dei, 1 November 1885 6 From our Father, Instruction, 19 March 1934

The leaven and the mass 95

everywhere reaffirming the reign of Christ forever.7 We have come to spread the knowledge and the love of God and of his one Church with a profound and lasting activity in this world which is being de-Christianised. We act as a divine yeast. The illness is extraordinary and the medicine is also extraordinary. We are an intravenous injection given into the blood stream of society.8 In this way we must take the light and the salt of Christ to all souls, to all human activities.

The strength of unity We are leaven and yeast within the Church, by God’s grace and in virtue of a divine mission. We are not charismatics,9 our Father has told us often. Neither the activity of the Work within the Church, nor that of each one of us within the Work is anarchic. An anarchic activity would be a complete non-starter. It would be bereft of grace and of supernatural fruitfulness. It would be a branch torn off from the vine. It would be dead leaven, insipid salt. Grace, the power to be leaven, comes to us from God in a way that is normal and natural. Ours is a personal apostolate that is directed. Obedience, the sure way. Unreserved obedience to whoever is in charge, the way of sanctity. Obedience in your apostolate, the only way: for, in a work of God, the spirit must be to obey or to leave.10 Obedience, the organic link with the head, belonging to the Work, within the Church, makes each one of us the leaven of God, divine yeast, where before he was just a piece of the lifeless mass. This indeed is our vocation: to leaven the world with the grace of God, to turn what

7 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934 8 From our Father, Instruction, 19 March 1934 9 From our Father 10 The Way, 941 96 The apostolic mission

was sick or dead or even a foreign body into the living body of Christ. We are able to do this through the power of the sacraments the Church administers and through the doctrine the Church teaches. We do not make any distinction between what we are as Catholics and what we are as members of society. Not only do we not make any distinction but, without allowing them to lose their true nature, by fulfilling those rights and duties and exercising them properly, we put them at the service of our apostolic mission. We are leaven which has to turn this whole mass of dough into the bread of Christ, into the Church of God, respecting the nature that everything has. Grace does not destroy nature; it presupposes it, heals it and raises it. Not only do we not allow religion to be reduced to a private affair without power or influence in public life, but we make of both private and public matters a way to serve the Church, to co-redeem. We do not turn the world into either a sacristy or a convent; rather we make everything into a means of sanctification, doing all for the glory of God. What we do in fact is to make the street into a place of worship. If I may change around the terms of a very Spanish proverb, I would say we give a hare instead of a cat.* This way of acting must surely please Our Lord since he condemned precisely the opposite: Domus mea domus orationis vocabitur: vos autem fecistis illam speluncam latronum; My house shall be called a house of prayer; but you have made it a den of robbers (Matt 21:13).11

The co-operation of each of us We have considered why we act as we do, and why it is so effective. It has the divine effectiveness which was given by Jesus

* Translators note: This refers to those who deceive others into paying a high price for something worthless: “they give a cat instead of a hare”; or “they serve a cat instead of a hare”

– thus making people think that what was served was hare when in fact it was cat. 11 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934

The leaven and the mass 97

Christ to his Church. The calling is clear; the ability to act as leaven has already been given us. What we have to do now is to act as leaven in an effective way, by our own faithful and generous co-operation. Let us not put obstacles in the way of grace. We have to be convinced that if we are to be leaven we must be saints.12 Some years ago, in a meditation, our Father led us to consider that, a small quantity of yeast leavens the whole mass of dough.13 He went on to say that we have to prepare ourselves to be everywhere on earth the leaven that gives grace, that gives flavour, that gives volume, so that this bread of Christ can go on to nourish all peoples.14 Each of us must cooperate in a determined way if the power of God is to act in us. Our vocation launches us into all environments. Each of us is on our own. Our vocation mixes us inseparably with the mass of dough that has to be leavened. Be filled with apostolic zeal; and if you are on your own do not grumble. Could it not be, perhaps, that your loneliness is of 15 your own choosing? We are few, and we shall always be relatively few among the great mass of men, precisely because our vocation is that of being leaven, a good leaven of Christ. We are to be apostles in the midst of the world who carry out a persevering apostolate and work little by little. We must know how to wait, gaining ground every day.16 We must be filled with zeal.
