We are surrounded by a shapeless mass of dough, a mass of dough without supernatural life. This fact has to spur us on, push us forward, and keep us always from falling asleep.

12 From our Father, Meditation Leaven, 27 March 1962, in While he spoke to us on the way, p. 190 13 From our Father, Meditation With the docility of clay, 3 November 1955, in In Dialogue with the Lord, p. 37 14 Ibid. 15 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934 16 From our Father, Meditation Leaven, 27 March 1962, in While he spoke to us on the way, p. 188 98 The apostolic mission

It is for this reason, to leaven the whole mass of dough, that God has made us yeast. “Let no one then place the blame on the smallness of our numbers. The preaching of the Gospel has great strength, and what has once been leavened becomes leaven for the rest.”17 Our mission is multiplied in proportion to the faithfulness with which we carry it out. 18 Do we want to be more?, our Father used to say, then let us be better! We want to be more because we do not consider there are any limits, either theoretical or practical, to the extension of the Church; because we want the whole world to be saved; because we want to make Christ’s kingship a reality in every soul and in society as a whole; because we want to breach the walls that the enemies of God have raised against the Church, marking off whole fields as “secular”, and denying her entry to them. Our mission is to convert these strongholds and to save them. Even though the whole world were to be converted, our action would always need the same apostolic desire in order to keep up that spiritual blaze. For to be holy and to sanctify everything will never be easy while we are on earth. Though the whole world were bread, not everything would be leaven. Our vocation is precisely that of leaven, leaven that acts silently, without violence, through a power that comes from within, on the whole mass of dough.19

Open up like a fan This is why our apostolate does not usually require that there be many of us together in one place. It is quite the reverse: our vocation to the Work demands that we should dissolve like salt. The proper place for each one, the place to which we feel ourselves

17 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae, 46, 2 18 From our Father, Meditation Leaven, 27 March 1962, in While he spoke to us on the way, p. 189 19 Ibid.

The leaven and the mass 99

called by our vocation, and to which our personal directed apostolate brings us, is any place where there is a mass of dough that is not leavened. Those children of mine who might wish to live shut up at home should learn to open up like a fan, reaching out to every environment. It is a duty of ours, a duty of fundamental importance, to go out and seek souls wherever they may be in order to bring them back wounded with love, with contrition, with dedication, or at least with desires of dedication.20 Wherever there is a member of the Work there should be vocations and the spiritual temperature of the whole environment should go up. We have seen, over the years, many works of apostolate grow up. They are wonderful and most effective instruments. These have come by God’s will, and over the years he will bring many more. But none of these works should ever be an excuse for us to lessen the apostolic desire of those who work in them, a desire which is definite and personal, and which leads us to make demands on ourselves. If this were to occur, it would rob these works of their true nature. We must always remember that these works are a means, a springboard for our action which goes deep. They are never a box for the safekeeping of apostles, a hole in which to bury our talents. That is why usually there are only a few of our people needed to start these works and to keep them going, with all the dynamism and effectiveness of a completely supernatural activity. Around them there will be other people, without a specific vocation to be leaven, who will act in Our Lord’s service under the constant influence and apostolic zeal of a few who know that they are leaven and act as such. This is a characteristic which belongs to our vocation, among the different vocations that exist in the Church of God. Our activities can be defined by that phrase of the Gospel: Wherever the body is, there the

20 From our Father, Crónica, July 1962, p.55 100 The apostolic mission

eagles will be gathered together.21 Often, what we have to do is to bring leaven into human enterprises that have lost their supernatural meaning or that never had it, to bring them new life from within, and to turn them into instruments of co-redemption. Our task is not only one of creating, but also, and especially, of transforming. This we do with each one’s determined Christian action, an action which is that of true leaven: a little leaven leavens the whole mass.22 Though there may be many of us, we are still few, and we shall always be relatively few. The history of the expansion of the Work is forever teaching us this. No sooner have we grown a little in one place than we have gone at once to another place. We go without delay, without taking it easy in a work that is now more manageable, or in a task that demands less apostolic zeal, where quantity might seem to make up for quality. How have we begun in those new places, new cities, new countries or continents? With very few. From that beginning, a beginning that could hardly be seen but which was full of apostolic desire, there have afterwards come vocations and apostolates of great stability and works of all kinds. Our Lord Jesus Christ has always used the leaven of a few. He has done this, wanting ut omnes homines salvi fiant (cf 1 Tim 2:4), wanting not just a few to be saved, but all men. Look at the leaven of Tabor – follow me in your imagination and in your memory – and that of Nazareth and at that of the Upper Room. Look at the leaven of Calvary. And afterwards? Afterwards comes Pentecost, and with it, mass conversions.23 Immediately after that comes the dispersion. The Apostles scatter to all parts of the world. As the fruit of their leavening action, they leaven other countries too. Areas of Christian influence

21 Mt 24:28 22 1 Cor 5:6 23 From our Father, Meditation Leaven, 27 March 1962, in While he spoke to us on the way, p. 188

The leaven and the mass 101

open up around each one like concentric circles. First one soul, then two, then three ... a family, a group of families, a whole social environment. Are there just a few of us in this place or that? That is how things should be with us. We are leaven: we try eagerly to be more. When there are more of us, we spread out, reaching other areas of the mass, because we have an ambition that comes from God, the great ambition of sanctifying, of Christianising the institutions of nations, learning, culture, civilisation, politics, art, social relations. Everything ought to be Christian, as a collective and social expression of people’s faith and a means of saving souls, of sustaining them in their faith, of leading them to God. The Kingdom of heaven is like leaven, which a woman took and hid in three measures of meal, till it was all leavened.24 The entire mass of dough and each and every one of its parts has become the bread of Christ, his Body, the kingdom of God, the Holy Church. This was a lesson that our holy Mother Mary learned well on her journey from Bethlehem to Pentecost; a lesson that she must have taught continually to those first members of the Church at Jerusalem. Her motherly protection has also followed the action of the Work of God from its beginning. May she remind each one of us of this teaching, so that we may know how to leaven the three measures that God has assigned to us.
##
24 Mt 13:33

## OUR SENSE OF RESPONSIBILITY {#cuaderno.5.10}

Opus grande ego facio,1 I am accomplishing an important Work. God, upon desiring his Work on earth, has entrusted it to men, as free instruments of divine action. He fixed his attention upon each one of us. He showed us the extent of the task and gave us the means necessary to accomplish it. And our voluntary acceptance led us not just to belong in the Work but to be of the Work, we are the Work itself. We have committed ourselves individually to a collective task: To do Opus Dei on earth, being yourself Opus Dei,2 our Father has written. Thus, being Opus Dei means bearing upon our shoulders the responsibility for the whole task, specifically in the faithful care that we put into the part that has been entrusted to each one of us. We must devote our whole life to the task, not just a part of it. We must feel the burden of the toil of everyone, doing that which depends on us, as if everything depended on it.

Links of the same chain Although as free instruments we are individually responsible, we are not isolated pieces. We are like coordinated points of leverage of a single universal action of God on earth: Opus Dei. And we have to assume this responsibility out of loyalty to God, to the Work, to our

1 Neh 6:3 2 From our Father, Prologue to the Catechism of the Work, 23 April 1947

Our sense of responsibility 103

brothers and to all those whom we have to save. Be serene, with a sense of responsibility, knowing that we are links of one and the same chain, our Father recommends: Therefore, I do not want this link, which is me, to break; because, if I break, I am a traitor to the rest.3 Because we are free, we have a personal and nontransferable responsibility, which cannot lose itself in the crowd. Because we are part of a common action, we are responsible for the task as a whole; for the part only subsists in the whole, and the destruction of a part threatens the life of the whole. And I rejoice, our Father continues, in the strength of the other links, and I am happy that some are made of gold, some of platinum, and some have precious stones.4 A perfect individual task, finished down to the last detail, would be useless if it were not done organically, interwoven with the action of the rest, because our action is either that of the whole or it is nothing; it is either the Work of God or it is a human work which is supernaturally sterile. The daily activity of the Work is like a great tapestry that we offer to Our Lord. If all of us carry it out, if we are faithful and dedicated, this great tapestry will be beautiful and without a fault. But if one drops a stitch here, and another there, and another on the other side, instead of a beautiful tapestry we will end up with a rag in shreds.5 And the magnitude of what God has proposed by promoting his Work, is such that it absolutely exceeds the individual possibilities of men, even men considered as instruments. It is such a great good, and needs such unity, that it was entrusted to many people so that they carry it out by acting in unison, with one heart and soul,6 with the same means and the same spirit, with one single authority and one common

3 From our Father 4 From our Father 5 From our Father 6 Acts 4:32 104 The apostolic mission

fraternity. Thus the objective of each and every one of them should be the coordinated attainment of one and the same good. This is why the unity of the parts of this great ensemble is so close. This whole organism is not formed by more or less similar elements, united by the same interest. Our unity is identity: The identity of each one with the others, and of all with Christ,7 as our Father has taught us. What is personal to each one is his responsibility and his duty. The good and the rights belong to all: You have nothing. If you have truly surrendered yourself, what is yours is ours, God’s, everyone’s.8

Members of the same body The Pauline doctrine of the Mystical Body is the basis of this reality, because, in its turn, the Work is a part of the Church, part of the Body of Jesus Christ. It is an organic, living part, composed of elements which are each and every one of us. If one member is impaired, the whole body is impaired. The body needs each one of its members, but each one of my members needs the whole body. What would happen if my hand were to cease to fulfil its duty, what if my heart were to stop 9 beating! This idea and this desire are reflected in practice in the formula our Father has given us: My only aim is the corporate aim: the one which obedience indicates.10 Only authority can organise, coordinate and combine functions. But we are men, we are rational and free beings. Our obedience should not be mechanical, or inert and formal. Rather, it should be personal, voluntary, loving and intelligent, in order that we know how to grasp

7 From our Father 8 From our Father 9 From our Father 10 From our Father Our sense of responsibility 105

the spirit and aim at the common final objective of the actions of all, taking care down to the smallest detail of the individual job that has been entrusted to us. This collective responsibility becomes even greater for those whom our Father used to call co-founders, those workers called at the first hour. For they are, in a way, responsible for an undertaking that has to go on for all time, as long as there are men on earth. Their work today will have repercussions in centuries to come. As you are co-founders, you have more responsibility,11 our Father used to tell them. Perhaps the most important aspect of this responsibility is the transmission of the spirit of the Work to those who come afterwards. This spirit must be incarnate in them and in everyone else, without the slightest flaw. Our Father used to ask us for responsibility, because it is a great spiritual paternity that you are going to have over your brothers, and because later on it will be a great crown in heaven, if you are faithful.12

Ways of showing a sense of responsibility It is easy to see that this great individual responsibility for the one common undertaking affects our daily activity and every facet of it, especially our interior life, which is the only solid foundation of external activity. We must be serious about sanctity, in our daily programme of struggle, in the faithful fulfilment of the Norms; not only on account of the supernatural progress that we are personally obliged to obtain, but so that the spiritual health of the rest is not disturbed.13 For the Communion of Saints, particularly strong among those who are united by a common vocation, unites us in grace. Therefore, the fault of one

11 From our Father 12 From our Father 13 From our Father 106 The apostolic mission

will have harmful consequences for all the rest, just as the heroism of one strengthens the sanctity of all. We have to be heroic. To be saintly is very little; we have to be very saintly. The Work needs secure, firm men on whom it is possible to lean.14 This spirit of responsibility has special importance in the fulfilment of the apostolic tasks that are entrusted to us. We must carry them out thinking of the whole, and this means to procure by our work the effectiveness of the rest, not to delay things that have to be handed on to others. It means to finish off what we are doing, so as to make the task of the others easier; to leave a written account of our experience, so that whoever takes over from us can begin where we left off; to rejoice if we are replaced by someone better, to teach those who help us not to make themselves indispensable, and a thousand other details that the responsible man readily understands. A characteristic indication of this spirit is not to limit our efforts to that specific thing which has been entrusted to us, which would be typical of one motivated by a vain satisfaction in duty fulfilled, a subtle form of pride, and to know how to extend our concern to the tasks of those who live with us, in order to make up for any deficiency, to know how to help them in a natural manner, without being noticed. In this sense, care of the practical details of the house affords an ample field for our responsibility. For the same reasons, our personal apostolate is never anarchic, but directed, organic, and obedient. For, although it carries with it a grave responsibility, it is no more than a small part of the Work of God on the earth. In order that it be just that, it has to be geared to the whole with docility. This spirit of responsibility for our common enterprise, makes us

14 From our Father Our sense of responsibility 107

abundantes in opere Domini,15 fruitful in the Work of God; it allows us to surpass wonderfully our personal limitations. It makes us supernaturally effective, serene and joyful. It is a motive for great fortitude when passions try to perturb the soul and make it fall off in its undertaking. This is the reasoning that our Father has offered us for these moments: No one of you is an isolated piece. If you stop you make everybody stop. And you cannot destroy the souls of your brothers! In spite of your passions you are responsible for the sanctity of the others, for the effectiveness of all. You know that the others have a right to their reputation and, as a man of Opus Dei, you cannot, because of a weakness of yours, destroy the reputation of the rest, which is your reputation.16 The end of this responsibility lived well will be common glory, happiness that will become greater upon seeing it enjoyed by all: indivisible union in the everlasting praise of God.

15 1 Cor 15:58 16 From our Father

## Apostolate of friendship and confidence {#cuaderno.5.11}


You must show your apostolic zeal in a practical and positive concern for the sanctity of those persons who are at your side on account of your work, social relations, or any other reason. No one who comes close to you should afterwards be able to say that he did not feel urged to get to know Christ better and to love God more.1 We have been called to Opus Dei so that, becoming one with Christ, we might bring to all men the fire of his sanctifying love. Ignem veni mittere in terram, et quid volo nisi ut accendatur,2 I came to cast fire upon the earth; and would I but that it were already kindled? As fire consumes the forest, as the flame sets the mountain ablaze:3 this is how our apostolate has to be, the supernatural fruit of our desire for a harvest that God, in calling us to his Work, has placed in our hearts. Like the first Twelve, we have been taught by Christ, and from him we have learned to love all souls and bring them closer to God by our personal work of friendship and confidence. Vos autem dixi amicos, quia omnia quaecumque audivi a Patre meo, nota feci vobis (John 15:15). I have called you friends, because all that I have heard from my Father I have made known to you. My dear sons and daughters,

1 From our Father, Letter 15 October 1948 2 Lk 12:49 3 Ps 82:14

Apostolate of friendship and confidence 109

here you have some words of Jesus Christ Our Lord which show us the path we have to follow in our apostolic work. God has called us to bring his doctrine to every corner of the world and to open up the divine paths of the earth so that souls who are ignorant of Christ may come to know him. In wanting us to be in his Work, God has given us also an apostolic way of working which leads us to be understanding, to forgive, and to practise a tender charity with all souls.4

The friends of Jesus Jesus is our model. The whole secret of sanctity and of effectiveness consists in reproducing his life in ourselves. He it is who has taught us with his example to seek friends, many friends, in order to make them his friends and save them. A few scenes from the Gospel, taken from so many others, will be enough for you to understand even better the divine depths of our apostolate of friendship and confidence.5 One day John the Baptist was near the Jordan with two of his disciples. And he looked at Jesus as he walked by, and said, Behold the 6 John the Baptist gently pointed out the presence of the Lamb of God! Master to his friends, John and Andrew. The two disciples heard him say this, and they followed Jesus ... and they stayed with him that day.7 They became Our Lord’s friends and began to do a fruitful apostolate among their own friends. Andrew will bring Peter, his brother.8 Later Jesus calls Philip and shortly afterwards Philip brings his friend, Nathaniel.9 It is very likely that John, the beloved disciple, brought he brother James to Our Lord.

4 From our Father, Letter 16 July 1933 5 Ibid. 6 Jn 1:35-36 7 Jn 1:37-39 8 Cf Jn 1:41-42 9 Cf Jn 1:43-45 110 The apostolic mission

On another occasion it is Nicodemus who comes to confide in Jesus, at night. Rabbi, – says that man, a ruler of the Jews – we know that you have come as a teacher from God, for no one can work these signs that you work unless God be with him (John 3:2). Jesus answers him, my children, with a phrase that apparently has nothing to do with what Nicodemus said but that attracts him and captivates his attention. It provokes a dialogue with his questioner: Amen, amen, I say to you, unless a man be born again, he cannot see the Kingdom of God (John 3:3). You know the result: at the hour of the failure of the cross Nicodemus will be there and courageously ask Pilate for the Body of Christ. But what about the Samaritan woman? Does Jesus not do the same thing here, taking the initiative and beginning the conversation with her in spite of the fact that non enim coutuntur Iudaei Samaritanis (John 4:9), the Jews did not associate with the Samaritans? Jesus speaks of what he knows will interest that woman, of the water that she has to fetch laboriously each day from Jacob’s well, and of the living water that is so wonderful that qui autem biberit ex aqua, quam ego dabo ei, non sitiet in aeternum (John 4:13), whoever drinks of it shall never thirst. The fruit of this dialogue of Christ also appears in the Gospel: the conversion of that sinner, making her soul become an apostolic soul.10 The entire life of Christ is full of examples of sincere friendship, that marvellous channel of apostolate. Jesus was a friend to his disciples, a true friend, and they knew it. That is why they came to him with confidence when they did not understand something: Edissere nobis parabolam,11 explain the parable to us, they said to him on one occasion. Then Jesus, away from the crowd, reveals to them the

10 From our Father, Letter 16 July 1933 11 Mt 13:36

Apostolate of friendship and confidence 111

mysteries of the Kingdom of Heaven. At other times, in intimate conversation, he lets them share in his joys and concerns. He encourages them and opens up horizons for them. He looks after the Twelve with the concern of a friend, not forgetting their physical rest as well. Come away by yourselves to a lonely place, he tells them on another occasion, and rest a while. For there were many coming and going, and they had no leisure even to eat.12 With this loyal friendship, gently leading them to confide in him, Jesus was forming his Apostles. Whoever cannot see the apostolic and supernatural effectiveness of friendship has forgotten Christ: No longer do I call you servants, but friends (John 15:15). He has forgotten that friendship with his Apostles, with his disciples, and with the family of Bethany: Martha, Mary and Lazarus. He has forgotten those scenes which St John relates before the resurrection of Lazarus, that et lacrimatus est Iesus, and those confident words of the two sisters when they want to tell Jesus of Lazarus’ illness and send him this message: Lord, behold, he whom you love is sick.13 In all the circumstances of his life – amidst the crowds or in the solitude of a mountain, on the shores of a lake or in the fields of Palestine – Christ has shown us how our personal apostolate ought to be. Yet another example: I am referring to the one which Our Lord gives us from the Cross as though to teach us how the zeal for souls that moves us to get to know people, to converse with them and to dialogue with them, has to be shown even in death. It is the moving and touching conversation which Christ has on the heights of Golgotha with the two thieves who were crucified with him. This time it was not Jesus who began the conversation. But his presence on the gibbet and his sufferings are more eloquent than any

12 Mk 6:31 13 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932 112 The apostolic mission

words. If you are the Christ, save yourself and save us (Luke 23:39), said the bad thief blaspheming. The good thief replies: Do not even you fear God, seeing that you are under the same sentence? And we indeed justly, for we are receiving what our deeds deserved; but this man has done nothing wrong. Then he said to Jesus, Domine, memento mei, Lord, remember me when you come into your kingdom (Luke 23:40-42). My children, the few words with which Jesus intervenes in the conversation between the two thieves, meant salvation for the one who had repented: Amen I say to you, this day you shall be with me in paradise (Luke 23:43).14

Like the first Christians The Apostles and the disciples of the early Church learned the Master’s lesson well. One day Philip the deacon, guided by the Holy Spirit, set out walking along the road that leads from Jerusalem to Gaza. There he found an important person who had come to Jerusalem to worship and was returning; seated in his chariot, he was reading the prophet Isaiah.15 Philip went up to him and began a conversation with that stranger. He won the man’s confidence. Do you understand what you are reading? But he said, How can I, unless someone guides me? And he invited Philip to come up and sit with him ... Then Philip opened his mouth and, beginning with this Scripture he told him the Good News.16 In Ephesus, too, in Gentile territory, the apostolate of the first Christians was an apostolate of friendship and confidence. Aquila and Priscilla, an exemplary married couple and friends of St Paul, lived there. One day they heard Apollo, an eloquent man who was fervent in spirit; he spoke and taught accurately the things concerning Jesus,

14 From our Father, Letter 16 July 1933 15 Acts 8:27-28 16 Acts 8:30-35

Apostolate of friendship and confidence 113

though he knew only the baptism of John.17 Unable to contain their apostolic zeal when they heard Apollo’s words Priscilla and Aquila took him and expounded the Way of God to him more accurately.18 The early Christians, following the living example of Jesus, always acted in this way. With a personal apostolate similar to ours, they went about making converts and, during his captivity, Paul was already sending greetings to the churches from the Christians who lived in Caesar’s household (cf Phil 4:22). Are you not moved by that wonderful letter which the Apostle addresses to Philemon, a letter which is a living testimony of how the leaven of Christ – without directly intending it – through the influence of charity, had given a new meaning to the structures of that society? (cf Philem 8-12) Tertullian, after little more than a century wrote: We are a people of yesterday and yet we have filled every place belonging to you, cities, islands, castles, towns, assemblies, your very camp, your tribes, companies, palaces, senate, forum. We leave you your temples only. (Tertullian, Apologia 37).19 Now the Church once again reminds all the faithful that “since the characteristic of the lay state is a life spent in the midst of the world and of secular affairs, laymen are called by God to make of their apostolate, through the vigour of their Christian spirit, a leaven in the world.”20 The Council also tells everyone, especially young people, that they “should become the first apostles of the young, in direct contact with them, exercising the apostolate by themselves, among themselves, taking account of their social environment.”21

17 Acts 18:25 18 Acts 18:26 19 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1959 20 Second Vatican Council, Decree Apostolicam actuositatem, 2 21 Ibid., 12 114 The apostolic mission

The meaning of friendship Christ has taught us that friendship is the best channel for apostolate. The very nature of friendship demands this supernatural task from a Christian. To love is to seek the good of another. “Not all love has the character of friendship, but that only which goes with well wishing, namely, when we so love another as to will what is good for him.”22 Nevertheless, benevolent love is not enough to make a friendship, for “this requires a mutual loving; it is only with a friend that a friend is friendly.”23 No matter how true a love may be, if there is no response there is no friendship. Besides, friendship needs to be founded on a good which is shared or communicated. Thus, “when one has love-of-friendship for a person, one wants good things for him as one does for oneself; one therefore looks on him as another self, wishing him well in the same way as one does oneself. That is why a friend is described as an ‘other self’ and, as Augustine says,24 ‘Someone well called his friend, 'half of my soul'’.”25 The fruit of friendship, and at the same time its cause, is the communication of sentiments, our sharing the joys and sorrows of those whom we have beside us daily because of our work, interests, or character. “This communication is realised above all in fellowship with others. Hence fellowship is proper to friendship.”26 This living in harmony with others, making their ideals and concerns our own, is necessary for all true friendship. A friend shares his life with his friend, looks out for him, and, precisely because his love for his friend is great, loves him as he is with all his faults. He knows how to cover up his weaknesses in the cloak of understanding, to give him example and to help

22 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.23, a.1 23 Ibid. 24 St Augustine, Confessions 4, 6 25 St Thomas, S. Th., I-II, q.28, a.1 26 St Thomas, In ethica Arist. ad Nicom. expositio 9, 14

Apostolate of friendship and confidence 115

him. After all, friendship requires giving the best of oneself to a friend. With the Incarnation of the Word all human realities, noble in themselves, have been made supernatural. They have been given an undreamt of value and extension. Profession, family, business and social relations ... all honest activities of men have become a channel, a sea without shores,27 for the apostolic zeal of Christians. Human friendship is now Christian friendship, and in it the Christian ought to communicate to his friends the greatest good that he carries in his heart, namely the light and fire of God. “Gathered together in the people of God and established in the one Body of Christ under one head, the laity

– no matter who they are – have, as living members, the vocation of applying to the building up of the Church and to its continual sanctification in all the powers which they have received.”28 This obligation holds for all Christians. Because of the calling we have received which is a very special title, it is onus et honor, a duty and an honour, for the children of God in his Work. God asks us to bring him to all those who cross our path, with our exemplary conduct and with a constant apostolate of giving doctrine.29 Consequently, if at any time we did not feel the intimate longing to bring the light of God to our friends, it would indicate a false, unnatural and selfish friendship. For, as St Augustine says, “the only true friendship is that between those whom you, O Lord, join together through charity which is poured into our hearts by the Holy Spirit.”30 But we cannot be satisfied with bringing closer to God only those friends we had before coming to the Work, or those who may have happened to become our friends by chance afterwards. Our apostolic zeal goes beyond that. As the Father has sent me,

27 From our Father 28 Second Vatican Council, Dogmatic Constitution Lumen gentium, 33 29 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932 30 St Augustine, Confessions 4, 4, 7 116 The apostolic mission

even so I send you.31 Jesus wants us to identify ourselves with him so that we can say with St Paul: Vivo autem iam non ego; vivit vero in me Christus,32 it is now no longer I who live, but Christ who lives in me. It is Christ who wants to make use of us, of our dealings with men, and of that capacity of ours (which he has given us) to love and make ourselves loved, so that he may continue making friends here on earth. Just in this way he used John the Baptist to meet that other John who was going to be his beloved friend.33 Jesus wants us to have many friends and to look for them. If we did not have many friends, we would not fulfil this ardent desire of Jesus. If we did not lead them to God, even though we were to do a lot in other apostolic activities, we would have usurped the place of Christ in this friendship. What a pity if in the end you had carried out “your” 34 apostolate, and not “his” apostolate!

Becoming friends We have to develop friendships. For this it is necessary to live alongside others. But people “cannot live alongside each other if they do not have the same likes, if they do not rejoice or become sad for the same reasons.”35 We will attain this unity of hearts if we are genuinely interested in the problems and concerns which affect our particular environment; if we work shoulder to shoulder with our companions; and if we partake in similar activities, likes, and interests. We need to dedicate time to our friends and to be with them. This is the essential channel for building up that flow of affection which makes friendship deeper and truer. We must know how to overlook the bothersome

31 Jn 20:21 32 Gal 2:20 33 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932 34 The Way, 967 35 St Thomas, In ethica Arist. ad Nicom. expositio 9, 3

Apostolate of friendship and confidence 117

trifles which crop up in any human relationship and might disrupt this friendship. Thus, your conduct towards others will have certain characteristics born of charity: considerateness in your relations with them, good manners, love for the freedom of others, cordiality, and affection. The Apostle says it so clearly! Free though I am from all men, I have made myself a slave to all that I might win the more ... To the weak I became weak, that I might win the weak. I have become all things to all men, that I might by all means save some (1 Cor 9:19-22). Our Father tells us: you should take advantage of your human relationships to bring souls closer to Jesus Christ. It is logical not to let the opportunity pass. Moreover, since you are apostles, it is necessary to foster these opportunities with an apostolate of friendship and confidence. You cannot shut yourselves up behind any wall that would isolate you from your companions.36 You will act in this way, my daughters and sons, certainly not to instrumentalise friendship as a tactic for social penetration. This would cause friendship to lose its intrinsic value. But we do know that friendship is the first and most immediate requirement of human fraternity which we Christians have the obligation to foster among men, no matter how different they may be from one another. At the same time the love of God motivates us. For friendship makes confidence easier, and this in turn makes possible the apostolate of doctrine by which we draw these souls, these friends whose good we seek, closer to God.37 Our friendship is noble and sincere. We want to give our friends the greatest good, God himself. We are friends always and in every circumstance, and we must make sure that it is never otherwise. Every friend will say: “I too am a friend”; but some friends are friends only

36 From our Father, Letter 16 July 1933 37 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940 118 The apostolic mission

in name.38 When difficulties come, they abandon their friends. On the other hand, a true friend can never be two-faced with his friend. Vir duplex animo inconstans est in omnibus viis suis (Jas 1:8), a double-minded man, is unstable in everything. Friendship, if it is to be loyal and sincere, demands sacrifice, integrity, an exchange of favours, and of noble and licit services.39 Sacred scripture teaches us what this loyal and apostolic friendship is like when it narrates the story of Tobias, a man in need of a faithful friend who would guide him to far-off countries to collect an old family debt. As he was getting ready to depart, his father told him, Find a man to go with you and I will pay him wages as long as I am here; and go and get the money. So he went to look for a man; and he found Raphael, who was an angel.40 Raphael was the loyal friend appointed by God to protect him on his journey and to help him to success on his mission. When friendship is genuine, a confidence follows which is spontaneous and frank. It is so logical to open our soul to one who truly loves and understand us! We love our friends and we feel at one with them. We want to participate in their deepest concerns, their problems and their ambitions. Nothing in their lives is foreign to us. To their trust we respond by opening ourselves in sincere confidence. We speak to them of our own life and of our love for Christ. Friendship then has become purer, it has become apostolate. Those well-timed words, whispered in the ear of your wavering friend; the helpful conversation you managed to start at the right moment; the ready professional advice that improves his university work; the discrete indiscretion by which you open up unexpected horizons for his zeal. This all forms part of the ‘apostolate of friendship’.41

38 Sir 37:1 39 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940 40 Tob 5:3-5 41 The Way, 973

Apostolate of friendship and confidence 119

Nothing can undo a friendship founded on Christ. If at any stage it were to break up, “it would be because that friendship did not have supernatural roots. For if it did, nothing on earth, no material thing, could destroy what is spiritual. Love which is based on Christ is firm, unbreakable and indestructible. Nothing, neither calumnies, nor dangers, nor death, nor any such thing, will be able to tear it out of the soul. One who loves like this, even though he may have to suffer much, will never stop loving if he considers the reasons for his love. He who loves because he is loved will stop loving as soon as he suffers something unpleasant. But he who is bound with the charity of Christ will never separate himself from this charity.”42 We should lose not even one friend. For how could we then bring him to Christ? We do not want any one to leave us. Rather, let us take positive steps to make friends and to win friends so as to make them friends of Jesus Christ ... Let us have friends among all our professional colleagues and among all those who live in our environment, even though they be far from God.43 They all need us, especially those who have come close to the warmth of the Work and have later, for whatever reason, gone away. We have to be especially affectionate and understanding with these, showing them that our friendship, founded on Christ, has not died out: “it is characteristic of a friend to do good for his friends, especially for those who are in greater need.”44 Who indeed is in greater need than he who has separated himself from the love of God? Moreover, our Father tells us: Even though a man be very far from God, even though he may show great animosity, we must think with St Augustine that we should not despair of his conversion, because even among those who are openly opposing us there are hidden some persons who are

42 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae, 60, 3 43 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932 44 St Thomas, In ethica Arist. ad Nicom. expositio 9, 13 120 The apostolic mission

predestined to be our friends even though not even they themselves realise it (St Augustine, De civitate Dei 1, 35).45

Make them friends of Christ We should develop personal friendship with Christ which is the source of all effectiveness so that our apostolate of friendship and confidence may bear abundant fruit. By living in friendship with God, which is the first friendship we have to cultivate and nourish, you will know how to make many true friends.46 By getting to know Our Lord in prayer and through contemplating his life, we will learn to deal supernaturally with our friends and to give friendship its full meaning. We will show them that our life is a life of love, and we will encourage them to follow Christ closely. You will shake them out of their slumber. You will open broad horizons for their selfish and bourgeois existence. You will complicate their lives for them, leading them to forget themselves and understand the problems of those around them. And rest assured that, when you complicate their lives, you will bring them that gaudium cum pace, of which you have personal experience joy and peace.47 If we are truly apostles, we shall pass on our drive to others and we will lead our friends to a better life. You lack drive. That is why you sway so few. You do not seem very convinced of what you gain by giving up those things of the earth for Christ. Just compare: A hundredfold and life everlasting! Would you call 48 that a poor bargain? The personal work of apostolate does not finish here. After setting souls on fire, we have to help them to be practical: with a plan of life

45 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940 46 Ibid. 47 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1959 48 The Way, 791

Apostolate of friendship and confidence 121

and norms of piety. With holy cunning and always respecting their freedom, we must push them on little by little. We push constantly, without haste and without pause. Let us not forget that at times we have to help souls to walk little by little. We have to encourage them with patience to press on slowly, in such a way that they are hardly aware of movement though they are in fact advancing.49 We can make use of means that help their interior life to take root. Thus we may do short periods of prayer, a few minutes of spiritual reading, or a pilgrimage together. We may invite them to teach catechism and to visit the poor of Our Lady ... It is also very important that we should know how to talk to each one in his own language, with the gift of tongues. In this too we must learn from Jesus Christ: sometimes he speaks to them from the boat while they are seated on the shore. At other times he speaks to them from the mountainside so that the whole crowd can hear him well. There are occasions when he does it amidst the noise of a banquet, in the quiet of a home, walking through the fields, or seated under the olive trees. He addresses himself to each one in terms he can understand: he uses examples of nets and fish for seafarers; of seeds and vines for those who till the soil; to the housewife he speaks about the lost coin; with the Samaritan woman he talks of the water she has to fetch from Jacob’s well.50 In this way our friends become the friends of Christ. This is the moment for them to be fully incorporated into the work of St Raphael or St Gabriel, to receive spiritual direction from the priest and begin to attend the classes of formation. Our apostolic zeal will ordinarily show itself by putting our friends in contact with the apostolates of the Work and by bringing them to take part in the means of formation. In doing this individual apostolate, our Father has written, you should try to

## From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931 50 From our Father, Letter 16 July 1933 122 The apostolic mission

bring the persons you are dealing with to the collective means of spiritual and doctrinal formation which the Work organises – days of recollection, conferences, circles, etc. – and to spiritual direction with our priests. These are very effective, indeed necessary, means to complete the attention given by each one of you to these souls ... that attention which you pay to souls through your working life, through the position you hold in the world, and through your family situation. You must make use of everything, because everything is a means of apostolate.51 God wants us to have a lot of friends, to speak to them about God and to bring them closer to the Work. There are so many people around us who are waiting for this light that will lead them to know and love Jesus Christ! They are persons who, even in the midst of the crowd, often find themselves alone. Woe to him who is alone, Scripture says, when he falls, and has not another to lift him up.52 It can well be said, my dear children, that the greatest fruit of the work of Opus Dei is that which its members obtain personally through apostolate of example and loyal friendship with their professional colleagues: in the university or in the factory, in the office, in the mine or in the fields. It is a work of giving light, example and doctrine. It is a constant, humble, silent, but most effective work, whose fruits cannot easily be reflected in statistics.53

51 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1959 52 Sir 4:10 53 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940

## THE CONSEQUENCES OF A DEEP FRIENDSHIP {#cuaderno.5.12}

Our Lord wants to make use of us and of our dealings with others, of that capacity of ours which he has given us to love others and to make ourselves loved, so that he can continue making friends on earth. In the same way he made use of John the Baptist to seek out that other John, the one who was going to be his dearly beloved friend and whom we see leaning on Jesus’ breast on that memorable night of the Last Supper. Erat ergo recumbens unus ex discipulis eius in sinu Iesu, quem diligebat Iesus (John 13:23).1 Our Lord still walks alongside men. He walks along, making use of each and everyone of us as his instruments by virtue of the Christian vocation that sets us on fire with love for the things of God. Any moment in our life can provide us with an opportunity for apostolate because Our Lord makes use of everything to see that his call reaches the heart of each individual. He is present in our work, in our conversation, in our joy, and especially in our friendships, in the affection that we put into our relationships with those around us. Ecce sto ad ostium et pulso: si quis audierit vocem meam et aperuerit mihi ianuam, intrabo ad illum et coenabo cum illo, et ipse mecum. Behold, I stand at the door and knock. If any man

1 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932 124 The apostolic mission

listens to my voice and opens the door to me, I will come in to him and will sup with him, and he with me (Rev 3:20). Get the souls you are trying to win for the Work to think about these words of St John. It is 2 This is a reason that not you who are calling; it is he, Christ himself! excuses any imprudence we may commit in our apostolate, an imprudence which springs from a desire to see that all souls find Our Lord and fall in love with him. It is not we who push them. It is Jesus himself calling and knocking insistently at the heart of each individual. It is Our Lord who seeks souls. It is he who wants their love. It is he who makes the first advance. He comes out to meet us without waiting for us to approach him. You and I, my son, must act in the same way. We must go out to meet souls, anxious to bring them closer to God. We must tell them lovingly and with daring, Christ is looking for you too.3

Getting into the lives of others Our Father has taught us that proselytism is the best sign of love for souls.4 It is a proof of authentic and sincere friendship, since it seeks what is truly best for one’s friend: the happiness that can be found only in God. St John Chrysostom points out: “There is no sign or mark that more clearly distinguishes a Christian and one who loves Christ than concern for our brothers and zeal for the salvation of their souls.”5 Get involved in the lives of others through this apostolate of friendship and confidence, just as Jesus Christ got into our lives, and do proselytism tirelessly. No one with a vocation to the Work should be able to excuse himself as those lazy workers of the parable: quia nemo nos conduxit (Matt 20:7), because no one told them anything.6 Christ

2 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934 3 From our Father 4 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 5 St John Chrysostom, De incomprehensibili homiliae 6, 31 6 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940

The consequences of a deep friendship 125

urges us to seek the happiness of our friends with a daring that surpasses our all-too-human calculations and prudence. In this context our Father liked to speak of the phrase compelle intrare, oblige them to come in, with which Our Lord concludes one of his parables: I have always told you that each one of us after entrusting things to Our Lord, ought to bring at least two vocations each year, carrying out that divine command, compelle intrare (Luke 14:23), which implies an invitation, a help to make up one’s mind, but never coercion even in the slightest way.7 That cry of the parable should be applied directly to the life of each one of us. Our Lord is asking for more. He wants us to do a lot of apostolate, making use of all the circumstances of our life. For us, the motor for our love for God and for the Church and of our sanctity is the vocations that we bring doing proselytism. Are there vocations? Then we are doing well. Are there few vocations? Then we are not doing well. Are there many, many vocations? Then we are doing very well.8 The grace of God is never lacking. What may be lacking is our cooperation, the sacrifice, struggle or dedication needed to carry out any supernatural enterprise. My children, I think each one of you will have meditated on this in your personal prayer many times. But now, considering the needs of the Church, of souls, and of the apostolate that God has entrusted to us, I feel it is necessary to insist on this. Do not imagine that you can obtain supernatural, apostolic fruit without sacrifice. What God asks of us to make his grace fruitful is our spirit of sacrifice.9 The supernatural life of souls, their coming closer to God, is a grace that we must beg from God with the insistence of prayer, mortification and work. It is not possible to be effective in any other way. Thus, with

7 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 8 From our Father 9 From our Father 126 The apostolic mission

a refined respect for the freedom of others, our apostolic action becomes insistent and daring because it is backed up by the reality of a life of dedication. Our Father has told us: Hence, the compelle intrare that you have to practise in proselytism is not like physically pushing somebody but rather an abundance of light and doctrine. It is the spiritual stimulus of your prayer and your work which is an authentic witness to doctrine. It is the sum of the sacrifices you offer up, the smile that comes to your lips because you are children of God. It is the filiation that fills you with a serene happiness that others will see and admire, even though at times you suffer contradictions. Add to all this your graceful bearing and your attractive human qualities, and there you have the content of that compelle intrare.10 The sense of urgency contained in this command of Our Lord is that of our dedication, of thinking more about the others in order to serve them selflessly. Our Father has written of this in The Way: Apostolic zeal is a divine craziness I want you to have. Its symptoms are: hunger to know the Master; constant concern for souls; perseverance that nothing can shake.11

A requirement of human brotherhood By living in friendship with God, which is the first friendship we must cultivate and develop, you will know how to make many true friends (cf Sir 6:17). The work which God has done and continues to do with us to keep us in his friendship is the same work that he wants to do with many other souls, using us as his instruments.12 We must work then, in such a way that our friendship brings the people around us closer to God. You must cultivate a friendship with your professional

10 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 11 The Way, 934 12 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940

The consequences of a deep friendship 127

colleagues, with the people with whom you have to deal for any reason. My daughters and sons, you will certainly not act in this way in order to instrumentalise friendship as a tactic for social penetration. This would make friendship lose its intrinsic value. You will act in this way because it is a requirement of human brotherhood, its first and most immediate demand, one which we Christians have an obligation to foster among men no matter how different they be from each other. At the same time, you will act in this way for love of God, because friendship facilitates confidence, thus making the apostolate of doctrine possible, and bringing those souls, those friends whose good we desire, closer to Our Lord.13 Friendship is the human basis on which the proselytism of the members of the Work is built. It is a sincere, deep and loyal friendship in which our friends will find support and strength. For the security of finding understanding, interest, attention, and a stimulating word that teaches one to give supernatural meaning to ordinary problems and difficulties, will move them to open their hearts trustingly and with the certainty that they are well loved and that their friend is ready to help them and to fight alongside them. With friendship and frank conversation we can give true spiritual direction to persons who may not even know what the term means. Pope St Leo the Great teaches that, “a strong friendship arises among men when they are united by common customs.”14 Apostolic zeal leads us to foster such similarities by understanding and taking an interest in the affairs of others. We begin to consider their interests as our own, involving us personally because we have become good friends. But even if this similarity of life-styles, of interests, or of character is lacking, “Christian grace has given us greater reasons for loving our

13 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940 14 St Leo the Great, Homilia 12, 1 128 The apostolic mission

neighbour.”15 The grace of God is the source of an affection capable of arousing the strongest and most stable sharing of enthusiasms and interests. “A friendship that is based on Christ is firm, unbreakable and indestructible. Nothing, neither calumnies, nor dangers, nor death, nor anything of this sort, is capable of tearing it out of the soul.”16

Treating people with affection A Christian knows how to find points of unity and of understanding with his companions, and with other people. He overlooks things which divide people, and in personal matters of no great importance he gives in, providing no offence against God or harm to souls will result. In this way he obtains that grace, that knack17 of dealing with people that our Father sometimes spoke to us about. That knack is a question of heart, of affection, and of God’s grace which is never lacking.18 On other occasions he told us, we have to go after each soul, to win it for Christ. 19 He Treat them all with affection, drown them all in love for Christ! also added: How does one bend iron? It can’t be worked cold. It has to be heated in the furnace until it glows red hot. Then it’s hammered and forged to the desired shape and becomes a graceful filigree. My children, that’s how to handle souls: in the fire of charity and the forge of fortitude.20 Thus, you will be able to enflame the hearts of so many companions along your way through life.21 As St Paul writes, creation waits with eager longing for the revealing of the sons of God.22 Our friends and companions expect us

15 St Leo the Great, Homilia 12, 2 16 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae, 60, 3 17 Cf The Way, 803 18 From our Father 19 From our Father 20 Alone with God, 129 21 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934 22 Rom 8:19

The consequences of a deep friendship 129

to be loyal. Often, without realising it or accepting it explicitly, they are waiting for us to speak to them about God and to reveal to them the reality that gives meaning and strength to our whole life. We have an obligation not to let down God who has chosen us out, but also not to let down all those people who expect so much from our apostolic work.23 We must speak. Our friendship cannot remain on the level of working together or of sharing common interests or of personal likes, no matter how worthwhile these may be. Friendship penetrates everything. It reaches everything. It necessarily leads to proselytism. We would be disloyal to our friends if we did not let them know about the Christian spirit which moves us. As St Jerome writes, True friendship should not hide what it feels.24 We do feel – we do want to feel – the love of God in our Christian life as followers of Our Lord. Hence, if our friendship is authentic, it will overflow into proselytism. It will lead us to speak about God, about the Christian spirit, about the Work, about the universal call that God sends out to all souls. One must speak with a friend as with another self.25 Our Father insists: My children must look for the opportunity to speak, to communicate these wonders that God has entrusted to us. Socalled presence is not enough for one to act in a Christian way. No! Those who are ashamed of Christ are the ones who say this. Jesus made himself present, and he spoke and gave doctrine! Presence is not enough. Whoever says this is wrong. You have to speak, with the gift of tongues, in a congenial manner.26 We cannot keep quiet through laziness or false prudence, because of a human fear of hurting or saddening someone, or for any other reason that conceals our love of comfort and lack of supernatural outlook. On the

23 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931 24 St Jerome, Epistola 81, 1 25 St Jerome, Epistola 105, 2 26 From our Father, Get-together, 25 August 1968, in Crónica, 1968, p.991 130 The apostolic mission

contrary, everything should encourage us to speak about God, to complicate our lives in trying to complicate the lives of others. A proselytistic man is aggressive. “But, you will ask me, Father, did you say aggressive?” Yes, but it is an aggression that, humanly speaking, is politeness, and, supernaturally, the charity of a soul who understands, who dialogues, who knows how to get along with others. Without hurting anyone, the truth must necessarily be a bit aggressive. It must be spoken without hurting, with charity, lovingly. But apostolic and proselytistic zeal must be converted into action. We must enter into the lives of others with violence and charity – compelle intrare, our Lord tells us – with imprudence, making use of that right which God has to dwell in all souls. Hence, when guided by a false respect for freedom, people say nothing, do nothing, and don’t help, it is a clear sign that they are far from God. The indifference implied by a lack of concern for others is born of the comfort-seeking attitude of a weak and wavering faith. I repeat, my children, that we must avoid the danger of a disguised lukewarmness which could lead us to distance ourselves from God and thus be ineffective. This is the lukewarmness of one who thinks that he has done something because he has friends and he gets around, but he has not set them aflame, nor has he raised the temperature in the atmosphere around him. It is not a question of doing pious things, but of speaking as Jesus did, as a good Christian does. In this way we will inflame the hearts of our friends just as the hearts of those disciples on the road to Emmaus were enkindled when Our Lord was with them. They asked each other with wonder: Did not our hearts burn within us while he talked to us on the road, while he opened to us the scriptures? (Luke 24:32) If each one of you tries to spread the fire that you have in your soul The consequences of a deep friendship 131

to a companion at work, a relative, a friend, a neighbour, then God will be pleased. Examine yourself at this moment, search your conscience. Whom are you thinking about now that might be brought closer to God, to the Work? These should be three, or four, or five. When these five have whistled, you will look for five more, and they also will do the same.27 We also have to think about the fact that we have the right and the duty to make certain that all the children that God has prepared from all eternity come to this wonderful family of ours. In this way it will last as long as there are men on earth, and Jesus Christ can take possession of so many souls who feel hunger and thirst for God (cf John 6:35).28 This is one more reason that obliges us to love the Work and be loyal to God. It makes us feel the urgency of the love of God who wants to make use of his Work and of each one of us. We must live for bringing souls to God. Only thus will we find the wonderful spiritual paternity that God wants for us. We will see those who come after us, and will experience the joy of knowing that they are the fruit of our prayer, of our work and of the divine grace that has made use of our hands to win those souls. Ours will be a joy without measure, because we will see many souls who only want to live for God.29

Giving the best we have My children, I have already told you that I believe in human friendship: amico fideli, nulla est comparatio (Sir 6:15), there is nothing comparable to a faithful friend. Friendship is a treasure we must appreciate for its great human value, and must also use as a

27 From our Father 28 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940 29 From our Father 132 The apostolic mission

means to bring souls to God.30 If a good friendship motivates us to do proselytism, to pass on to our friends the good things of God, the reverse is also true: apostolic zeal, proselytism, will encourage us to make more friends, to want to have a broad circle of acquaintances, of companions, with whom we can do real apostolate. Hence, our concern for all people, for we are not indifferent to anyone, because neither was Jesus. This concern must always be ruled by a human refinement that goes beyond merely social forms since it is a manifestation of our faith itself. Thus it may be readily understood that this spirit of the Work has to attract the affection and the help of so many non-Catholics and even non-Christians, among whom you will have to live and work, and on whom you will count as close friends.31 Our relationship with God, interior life, has this immediate effect: it fosters in us an attitude of openness, of understanding, one that makes us capable of winning the friendship of many people. The heart has an enormous coefficient of expansion. When the heart of a Christian loves, it expands. It becomes filled with love which is both supernatural and human, for if the love be not human, it cannot be supernatural either.32 This capacity of loving for Christ’s sake refines the soul. It gives it a special ability to understand and to show affection. It makes the path of friendship easier. We can give ourselves wholeheartedly in this affection because it goes by way of the Most Sacred Heart of Jesus and the Most Sweet Heart of Mary. It has the power to shorten distances, to lead to apostolate, to clear a way to souls. Let us make a positive effort to gain friends. Let us make friends in order to make them friends of Jesus Christ.33 Our Lord, who went

30 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940 31 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940 32 From our Father 33 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932

The consequences of a deep friendship 133

about doing good,34 and who won the hearts and the affection of so many people, is our model. Moved by his grace we are able to follow our way with a spirit that will bring us close to many individuals. We will get close to them with a true and dedicated friendship, a friendship that demands renunciation, honesty, an exchange of favours, of noble and just acts of service. A friend is strong and sincere in the measure in which, according to supernatural prudence, he thinks generously about the others even at the cost of personal sacrifice.35 Our proselytism can never be something cold and detached, a technical skill. Friendship is not a tactic that brings good results, but rather a real sentiment. It is sincere and made up of human affection and of supernatural concern for others. If you behave as I imagine my children behaving, your friends will very often open their hearts to you and ask you a question in confidence. That will be the moment to do a great apostolate. Bring them to God gently, with refinement, without ever taking away their freedom. If there is a loyal, noble, and pure friendship, apostolate will follow on immediately. You will give true spiritual direction to these friends of yours and you will be able to bring them to God. My children, be convinced that, if we possess God’s love, we are all-powerful. We would like to set all souls on fire with divine love, placing them within the heart of Jesus Christ, showing affection and understanding for all. My children, we have to pray! We have to love them! Don’t let anyone come close to Opus Dei and leave emptyhanded. Let them feel attracted by the fact that we respect them, that we understand them, that we seek what is best for them.36

34 Acts 10:38 35 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940 36 From our Father

## Life and doctrine {#cuaderno.5.13}


You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart, and with all your soul, and with all your mind. This is the greatest and first commandment. And the second is like it, you shall love your neighbour as yourself.1 You shall love God: this forms the sweetest and most fundamental commandment. It embraces, contains and gives birth to the others. Since we love God, we love all that is his, especially his sons and daughters among whom we number ourselves, too. Sanctity is summed up in this: in loving God above all things, and loving everything else, including ourselves, for God’s sake. We have to love our neighbour as ourselves, and ourselves as our neighbour. A lack of care, a lack of love in one or other of these aspects, would always imply a limit to our love for God. For this reason, as we have often heard, sanctity, the ascetic struggle, interior life, is the soul of all the apostolate, the condition of its effectiveness, the guarantee of its uprightness.

The one thing necessary Similarly, and in an equally fundamental way, we say that sanctity and apostolate are the purpose of our vocation. They are two inseparable aspects of the same end. We could not be saints if we were not apostles, and our apostolate would be null if it were not backed up by a fervent interior struggle. “Whoever has the mission of preaching

1 Mt 22:37-39

Life and doctrine 135

great things, is, by that very fact, bound to practise them.”2 Furthermore, only if he puts them into practice will he speak of them in a really effective manner. Only if he lives them will his preaching be truly fruitful. Only if he is all afire himself, and to the extent that he is afire, will he be able to enkindle others. If we are other Christs, our Father has told us we will enkindle wherever we are.3 This doctrine has its root in the words of Our Lord: Sine me nihil potestis facere,4 apart from me you can do nothing. It is completed with those other words: The branch cannot bear fruit by itself unless it abides in the vine.5 Thus only one thing is necessary: personal sanctity. This is the secret of the effectiveness of Opus Dei.6 The power to bind others to the vine and to bring them to sanctity which is the end of all apostolate, depends on our personal sanctity and our own link with the vine. Our apostolic word has to take root within us, working on our lives as we wish it to work on those of others. Our apostolic action will be effective in so far as it begins by sanctifying the apostle, just as a branch will bear more fruit the more nourishment it receives from the sap of the vine. This is what made Saint Bernard say these famous words: “If you are wise, you will be a container and not a duct.”7 It also prompted our Father to tell us: You have got to be a ‘man of God’, a man of interior life, a man of prayer and sacrifice. Your apostolate must be the overflow of your life ‘within’.8 Sanctity, interior struggle, is the foundation of apostolate. They are inseparable since they have the same root and they proceed from the same love. But this is true also in the point of view of effectiveness

2 St Gregory the Great, Regula pastoralis, 2, 3 3 From our Father 4 Jn 15:5 5 Jn 15:4 6 From our Father 7 St Bernard, In Cantico Canticorum sermo 18 8 The Way, 961 136 The apostolic mission

because the fruit depends on our union with Christ. No one gives what he does not have, and the weapons of the true apostle are these: First, prayer; then, atonement; in the third place, very much ‘in the third place’, action.9 The apostolate also requires interior life for its effects to be lasting. God counts on the passing of time for the crop to yield its fruit and in the meantime the newborn plant must be looked after. The apostle needs constancy and perseverance in the work begun, without yielding to discouragement should he not see the fruits, doing it only for the love of God and of souls. Only union with God can give the soul of the apostle the virtues which determine a good apostolate. Rabbi, Nicodemus said to Jesus, we know that you are a teacher come from God, for no one can do these signs that you do unless God is with him.10

Unity of life Truly, truly, I say to you, we speak of what we know, and bear witness to what we have seen,11 Our Lord continued explaining. This is another point that reinforces the need to back up our apostolic action with interior life. The word which invites to sanctity has to come from one who knows what this means. To ensure that the blind man’s guide is not blind himself, hearsay knowledge is inadequate. He has to practise it, experience it, he must feel the work of grace and the obstacles it encounters within himself. He must face the difficulties and the hard times that have to be suffered, the glorious moments to be enjoyed. A theoretical and vague knowledge of the way is not enough in leading souls towards sanctity. The living and practical knowledge that comes from having walked the path before is needed. For the difficult and

9 The Way, 82 10 Jn 3:2 11 Jn 3:11

Life and doctrine 137

dangerous paths, the mountain stretches, a map is not enough. Nor are those signs on major roads that point to such a place without going there themselves.12 We need a guide who accompanies us. Moreover what this guide has to do, rather than talk, is to mark out the road by his footsteps, going on ahead and applying to himself all that he knows. Everything else follows on from this. “We bid him be a guide with his works, that he show his subordinates the way of life by living it.”13 If prayer and interior experience teach us what we should say, mortification tempers and regulates the way of saying it. At the same time it purifies our intention. The spoken word becomes pleasant as well as firm. All bitterness disappears and action is made truly benevolent. On the other hand, there is the enormous practical effectiveness of example. A detailed and living knowledge of the way cannot be better transmitted than through our own lives, and we have been sent to transmit it. Our Father has told us: Go and give doctrine! Light, light, give light everywhere! And salt! With example, which is the best sermon. I have often observed people give in on seeing our conduct, on seeing how you match your conduct to your beliefs, on seeing your unity of life.14 When Our Lord wanted to show his followers how they should live charity with one another, he girded himself with a towel and washed the feet of his disciples. Afterwards he was able to tell them: I have given you an example, that you should also do as I have done to you.15 This was the constant rule of his divine teaching, as Pius XI pointed out: “If the multitude proclaimed him it was not so much because no man has spoken like this man16 as because he has done all things well17.”18 St

12 From our Father 13 St Gregory the Great, Regula pastoralis, 2, 3 14 From our Father 15 Jn 13:15 16 Jn 7:46 17 Mk 7:37 18 Pius XI, Encyclical Ad catholici sacerdotii, 20 December 1935 138 The apostolic mission

John confirms this at the beginning of his first epistle when he writes that he was setting out that which was from the beginning, which we have heard, which we have seen with our eyes, which we have looked upon and touched with our hands, concerning the Word of Life.19 The apostles followed this apostolic norm so faithfully that St Paul was able to write: what you have learned and received and heard and seen in me, do; and the God of peace will be with you.20 He recommended the same to Titus: show yourself in all respects a model of good deeds.21 St Peter also asked all the bishops to be examples to the flock.22 The spoken word alone would have a certain effectiveness, even if it were not practised, through the very power of the doctrine of Jesus.23 But apart from the fact that such fruits usually do not last, this good does not make up for the incalculable harm that they do by killing the souls of leaders, of apostles, who turn away in disgust from those who do not practise what they preach.24 These false apostles would merit that Pauline denunciation, an echo of the judgement to come: You rely upon the law, and boast of your relation to God, and know his will, and approve what is excellent, because you are instructed in the Law, and if you are sure that you are a guide to the blind, a light to those who are in darkness, a corrector of the foolish, a teacher of children, having in the Law the embodiment of knowledge and truth – you then who teach 25 others, will you not teach yourself?

19 1 Jn 1:1 20 Phil 4:9 21 Tit 2:7 22 1 Pet 5:3 23 The Way, 411 24 Ibid. 25 Rom 2:17-21

Life and doctrine 139

For love of souls In the dogma of the Communion of Saints we see a further reason for founding the apostolate on our sanctity. For we are all members of Jesus Christ, parts of the same body. In one way or another the spiritual health of the apostle necessarily influences the soul of the one whom he is trying to win over. The personal struggle to improve thus becomes apostolic in intention too. ‘Ideo omnia sustineo propter electos, so I bear it all for the sake of those who are chosen, ut et ipsi salutem consequantur, so that in the end they may have the salvation quae est in Christo Iesu, that is in Christ Jesus.’ What a good way to practise the Communion of Saints!

– Ask Our Lord to give you this spirit of Saint Paul.26 Jesus gave himself up on the Cross for us, taking upon himself an expiation that he did not need. With even more reason the apostle has to purify and sanctify himself with an eagerness to redeem by supplying in himself what he sees is still lacking in others, so that their needs may also be fulfilled. If you are generous ..., if you correspond, with your personal sanctification you will obtain that of the others.27 This is why our Father has often told us: everything depends on our personal sanctity. I see it so clearly! Pray a lot for me, therefore, so that I may be as God wants.28 Prayer and mortification are the basis for our apostolate. This petition is the more readily heard the more it is coupled with the sanctity of the one who asks. The apostle has to practice that specific virtue he wants to promote in the soul of his friend; he must refine it and perfect it. There are many fundamental reasons for this. Grace has

26 The Way, 550 27 From our Father 28 From our Father 140 The apostolic mission

to come through union with Jesus Christ, and it must spring forth for the apostolate from a great love for God. The apostle has to know well what he teaches. His weapons are prayer and sacrifice. The best book from which he can teach is his own life. He has to supply himself what is lacking in others, meriting what he wants to give them and making God hear him with the uprightness of his own conduct. Those words of our Father, so often repeated, may be applied here: I’m not interested in any enterprise, any apostolate, if my children are not improved by doing it. I measure the effectiveness and value of undertakings by the degree of holiness acquired by the people involved in them.29 Hence comes the perfect harmony of these two commandments which are the sum of the Law and the Prophets: You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart, and with all your soul, and with all your mind. This is the greatest and first commandment. And a second is like it, you shall love your neighbour as yourself.30 This general teaching is put into practice, for example, in the work of friendship and confidence that we carry out with our friends, in fraternal correction, and in the internal tasks of formation which the Work entrusts to us. It is here that we get down to specifics. We are dealing with souls who want to sanctify themselves, and the person who, in whatever way, has to lead them along, must examine himself closely. Here it is not simply a case of seeing the beam in our own eye before the speck in another’s, but of finding even the speck in our own eye before the beam in another’s.

Beginning with self For this work we have to be particularly united to God. He who guides souls in one way or another must be a channel of grace, a

29 Alone with God, 35 30 Mt 22:37-39

Life and doctrine 141

fruitful branch. It thus becomes necessary to apply to himself the advice that is to be given to others. If he already lives the virtue in question, he should refine it and improve in it. In this way he acquires the practical effectiveness of that which by its mere presence enkindles and inflames.31 The immediate and real experience of the effort required to root out a defect, gives the spoken word a living model which points out what has to be done and what has to be said to get it done, even down to the smallest detail. We know the resistance the old man can put up and we establish a link of sympathy and charity, a strong bond of common struggle. We know how far to go in making demands and how best to do so. The one who receives the encouragement and advice, given without sharpness and with a benevolent restraint, feels won over by an implied request made to him which he is able to read between the lines: the front must not be broken either in you or in me.32 The advice becomes all the more effective when it contains the means of achieving it. “The voice that comes with the life of the speaker as recommendation penetrates more readily into the heart of the listeners, because what he commands by speaking, he helps others to do by example.”33 These words do not come with the backing of some generic virtue but rather with the very virtue which is being recommended, brought to life. It is true that the fact of not possessing it should not hold us back from suggesting it, or from making a fraternal correction, for example. But counselling it ought to spur us on to practise it, and to do this conscientiously, without delay. We do this out of love of God, for sincerity’s sake, and in order to be effective. We know, furthermore,

31 From our Father 32 From our Father 33 St Gregory the Great, Regula pastoralis, 2, 3 142 The apostolic mission
##
that if God asks it of us, he will give us all thee graces sufficient to obtain it. You will see how, if you live in this way, others will begin to do the same. You will be like a blaze that enkindles everything.34 Like Christ, for this we have come into the world: Ignem veni mittere in 35 terram! Our Lord came to teach, but by deeds. Facere et docere.
