He came to teach, but by being a model, being both the Master and the textbook of doctrine with his conduct.36 This method of teaching that we see in the gospel has always been indispensable, but perhaps especially so today when men have their ears filled with empty doctrines and intellectual speculations that are sold cheaply. “Today, certainly, more than ever, men allow themselves to be persuaded, more than by words, by the specific and clear examples of those who live close to Jesus Christ.”37 In this way, the friend who listens to us, the brother who receives our advice, can end up saying: Then I saw and considered it, I looked and received instruction.38 Thus our Father describes it in the particular case of temperance: They have seen you: what do you do, what do you not do; that you struggle, that you mortify yourself. The first day they do not notice it, but finally it outweighs everything. This alone, which appears so crazy, carries the bonus odor Christi in such a way that it attracts.39 He who counsels is obliged to pray and mortify himself for the one counselled. In this he finds the best way of making up for the other with eagerness to redeem. Thus he can merit for the other and obtain the grace without which it is impossible to put things right. For this reason

34 From our Father 35 Lk 12:49 36 From our Father 37 Pius XII, Letter I felici sviluppi, 25 January 1950 38 Prov 24:32 39 From our Father Life and doctrine 143

our Father has told us: When you want to obtain the improvement of someone in a specific point, your best prayer for him will be for you yourself to achieve an effective improvement in the same point, and offer it for that intention.40 The effectiveness of this struggle is assured by those words spoken by Jesus about the smallest commandments: he who does them and teaches them, shall be called great in the kingdom of heaven.41 This way of acting, besides being the only really effective one, adorns the soul of the apostle with splendid virtues. It gives a love that covers all deficiencies, and that will lead you to understand and to forgive the personal things of others, and to be intransigent with error and with yourselves.42 Seeing so clearly his own difficulties and shortcomings, the apostle increases in humility and he is never surprised at those of others. He is never scandalised by anything. His understanding makes him patient. It distances him from all intemperance and he knows how to wait while grace does it work. If it takes a while, he thinks more about the weakness of his own apostolate than about the lack of correspondence on the part of others. He demands more of himself. Then he lives to the letter that advice of St Paul that our Father has reminded us of so often: Veritatem autem facientes in caritate.43

40 From our Father 41 Mt 5:19 42 From our Father 43 Eph 4:15

## THE NEED TO REPEAT THINGS {#cuaderno.5.14}

The work of Opus Dei consists in spreading doctrine to all environments, and so it is that, although our apostolates have many different expressions, they all have one and the same aim: that of giving doctrine.1 We can say then that we have a special need for formation and for acquiring a solid preparation in doctrinal matters because we cannot give what we do not have. Even though this is the most important thing, it is still not enough. We also have to learn also how to pass on the doctrine we have acquired. Part of that task consists in scattering the seed of truth with the generous persistence of the sower so that it takes root and becomes effective: We need to be constantly fresh, to say the same thing every day with a new attractiveness. It is the gift of tongues, part of the gift of tongues.2

In a thousand different ways The mysteries of our faith, the wonders God has worked and continues to work for men, possess an inexhaustible richness: O the 3 exclaimed St depth of the riches and wisdom and knowledge of God! Paul. The very greatness of the truths that we must teach requires that we go over them time and time again to bring out their full value. When it is a matter of giving information about some ordinary event, it is

1 From our Father 2 Alone with God, 44 3 Rom 11:33

The need to repeat things 145

usually enough to say just a few things and give one or two details for the listener to grasp the situation. But when it comes to the eternal truths, the truths of salvation, the great principles and the more exalted supernatural events, a single explanation of them is not enough. We must keep insisting and explaining them tirelessly for people to understand this whole treasure of doctrine deeply and clearly. There are times when the need for repetition derives not from the actual content of what is being taught, but from the listener being insufficiently prepared, either through lack of previous knowledge, or because he lives in a cultural atmosphere full of prejudices. This explains why, for example, the rights of the Church in matters of marriage and education, the freedom of Catholics in temporal affairs, and certain characteristics of the Work that are a novelty in the life of the Church, may not easily be grasped. There are some very, very clear matters that people do not manage to understand at times, because we fail explain them properly. On other occasions, however, it is their understanding which is weak. We are always saying the same things, and we keep insisting on ideas that are abundantly clear, but when people do not manage to understand them we must repeat them in fifty different ways so that finally, little by little, they begin to grasp them. Out of a hundred people sometimes only three catch on the first time; a bit later, ten and after a certain amount of time, thirty. There are some people who will never understand. Therefore, my daughters and sons, we should have a great deal of patience.4 On other occasions, the obstacle is clearly and simply that some people just do not want to listen, for “those who love something other than the truth would that that which they love were the truth. Nevertheless, since they do not wish to deceive themselves, and at the same time neither do they wish to acknowledge that they are mistaken,

4 From our Father 146 The apostolic mission

they hate the truth because of that which they love in place of the truth.”5 Their attitude, and at times even their words, seem to be saying: Depart from us! We do not desire the knowledge of your ways.6 In the long run, however, affection, understanding and constancy can ordinarily manage to overcome all forms of prejudice, while good doctrine eventually asserts itself, because it is more powerful than anything.7 Nevertheless, repeating things does not mean we should tire people out, because the only reason for saying something again is to make the truth understood, and repetition must be accompanied by the concern not to be either dull or monotonous. “The things that are repeated today are no longer new either to your ears or to your hearts; and, nevertheless, they stir up the sentiments of those who hear them. Just bringing them to our memory gives us a kind of feeling of newness. Although they are already well known, the things of God are not tiring, because they are always sweet. What happens with Scripture also happens with its explanation: Scripture is already known, but one reads it again to recall it; one hears it explained many times, but it must still be repeated so that those who have forgotten it remember it anew, so that those who perhaps did not hear it before may listen, or so that those who retain it do not forget it by dint of hearing it many times.”8 As men get to know things better, these things start making an impact on their personal lives. It is, therefore, necessary to repeat things also so that the fundamental principles of our faith, the seminal ideas come to be practised and thus form a habit whereby events may be viewed from a Christian perspective. Action then follows suit. “In order to acquire the habit of giving opinions of a particular kind, many acts of

5 St Augustine, Confessions 10, 23 6 Job 21:14 7 Wis 10:12 8 St Augustine, Sermon 125

The need to repeat things 147

the reason are required.”9 Besides, when we repeat something in a different way, with varied examples and diverse points of view, we educate people and prepare them better for putting into practice what is taught. It is not enough, as we have heard our Father say many times, to want to do something or to have good intentions. You have to learn how to do it, and for this you must not only know things very well but also know about them on a practical level and in detail. This is only achieved by dint of considering over and over again what has to be done. “Perhaps,” explained St John Chrysostom, “someone will say: every day you speak to us of charity and of giving alms. Well, for the time being I will not stop speaking to you of the same thing. I would not abandon the topic, even supposing that you already practised what I preached to you, just so that you should not become negligent.”10 Good intentions tend to lose their intensity. Although the truth is beautiful and lovable, at times it is difficult to live up to, because it means giving up other personal interests or tendencies or simply because at the moment of putting it into practice it can get crowded out by other, perhaps legitimate, yet less important occupations. For this reason it is necessary to come to the help of a good will, making clear the things that are truly essential so that they remain in the forefront. For what does it profit a man, Our Lord said, to gain the whole world and suffer 11 Porro unum est necessarium:12 only one the loss of his own soul? thing is necessary.

Insisting on personal formation In our family, too, things are repeated to us often so that we may

9 St Thomas, S. Th., I-II, q.51, a.3 10 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae, 88, 3 11 Mk 8:36 12 Lk 10:42 148 The apostolic mission

acquire the spirit of our Mother, the Work. Our formation never comes to an end, and through talks, meditations, Circles, the chat, annual courses, and so on, the same topics of our asceticism, the same indications and reminders are put to us from time to time. We do not find it tiring to be told the same things again because we know that we need it and that we can always improve. When we hear the same things again, we discover new small points about this or that norm or virtue, or ways of doing our work or apostolate better, which we had not even thought of before. The fact, then, that the same things are frequently repeated to us is a sign of the maternal concern the Work has for us. In this way we are always very aware of what is truly important. This is why we have been told a thousand times, in the words of our Father, that the Norms come first;13 that we must be very much given to prayer, for prayer is the weapon of Opus Dei;14 and that we each have to sanctify our job, our ordinary work; we have to sanctify ourselves in our job; and we have to sanctify others with our job.15 That our aim is to see that there are many souls dedicated to God in the midst of the world;16 and that the first expression of proselytism is to help one another to persevere and become saints.17 The virtues we must practise, our norms, work and apostolate, are indeed always the same, but each day we have to improve the way we do them. It is not a question of inventing new things, but rather of doing those same things with greater love of God each time. Hence it is necessary for us to consider them and go over them again in our prayer and in our study. My daughters and sons, our Father advises us,

13 From our Father 14 From our Father 15 Alone with God, 55 16 From our Father 17 Alone with God, 153

The need to repeat things 149

meditate on the same subjects often. Do not consider things only once, but keep working at it until you discover new horizons. How is it that I have not seen this point so clearly before? It is because at times, and for long periods, we are like stones that let the water flow over them without absorbing even a drop. That is why it is necessary to keep going over the same things, so that we can soak in each particular blessing of God.18 It is also true that all the aspects of the asceticism of our Work are intimately interwoven in a unity of life, to such an extent that when we understand and live one of the facets of our vocation better we understand and improve in all the rest as well. Thus, when recommended to do so in the chat, we should be quite happy to struggle for a long time on a particular point, without getting discouraged or tired of having to fight in the same things. The spirit of Opus Dei is not a single thread, it is an entire fabric: a whole series of virtues which are interwoven and gathered together by charity. When there is a ripe piece of fruit on a tree, you also find many others at the point of ripeness, if the tree was pruned on time. The taste, the size and the right season for one piece of fruit announces the ripeness of the others.19

The same but in a fresh way Do not be afraid to keep on insisting, our Father encourages us. My experience is that you have to repeat things.20 There are many reasons for doing so. But all this would have little effect, and even be completely useless, if we did not bear in mind that we do indeed have to say things continually but expressing them in a hundred different ways, pleasantly and without being tiresome. We have to repeat the same thing again,

18 From our Father 19 From our Father 20 From our Father 150 The apostolic mission

but in different ways. It is the way of saying things that ought always to be new and different, not the doctrine, which stays the same.21 It is a question then of presenting it under varying guises, with different examples and forms of expression. “You have to reach the hearts of your listeners with one single doctrine, certainly, but not with one and the same speech.”22 At times, our Father has said, one single story can explain the spirit of the Work better than four pages full of doctrine.23 One and the same reality can be seen from many points of view, and each different angle enables a particular aspect to be seen better, or makes a specific characteristic stand out more. “Are not the souls of the listeners like the different strings of a lyre which the musician plucks in a different way so that they do not produce a discord? Melody comes out because the strings are plucked by the same plectrum, indeed, but not with the same touch.”24 At times a certain line of reasoning fails to convince or to move the feelings. When put in another way, however, it can touch the heart and light up the understanding. We need foresight and must be ready to meet the possible obstacles when something is to be insisted on. These include the laziness and carelessness of not preparing what is to be said with the gift of tongues; the lack of supernatural outlook which lead to discouragement when there is no immediate response to what we say; the lack of constancy in our faith which dampens the tenacity required to remind people of the same thing when it is necessary; the fear of being tiresome, or even that a further reminder might not be well received. To write the same thing to you is not irksome to me, wrote St Paul, and is safe for you.25 It is a duty of charity, and very often of justice, to overcome all

21 From our Father 22 St Gregory the Great, Regula pastoralis, 3, prologue 23 From our Father, Crónica, January 1965, p.10 24 St Gregory the Great, Regula pastoralis, 3, prologue 25 Phil 3:1

The need to repeat things 151

these possible temptations, and go on insisting. “Let us suppose that one of you suffered from an affliction of the eyes and I were a doctor,” St John Chrysostom once said. “If after bathing them and applying lotions without result I left, would not the patient come to the door of my surgery, shouting and reproaching me for my negligence, for having gone off and left the sickness as it was? If I answered his reproaches by telling him that I had anointed him and treated him once already, would he be satisfied? Quite clearly not, and he would answer me: What good is that, if I continue to be ill? Well, then, apply the same to your souls.”26 Souls are waiting for us. Even without knowing it, they want that good doctrine from us which must be sown not once but often. Our Lord has wanted to count on our cooperation to spread his Kingdom, and with St Paul we can say to all men that we are ambassadors for Christ; God making his appeal through us.27 God himself will give us his help and his grace, and it is up to us to be faithful and keep insisting on the good news, repeating it as often as necessary, without fear and without hesitation: opportune et importune, but better if it is opportune.28

26 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae, 88, 3 27 2 Cor 5:20 28 From our Father

## At God’s pace {#cuaderno.5.15}


The apostolate is a divine task which cannot be carried out without the grace of God. It is Christ himself who performs his work in souls, removing the difficulties and setting hearts on fire. Our duty is to be instruments, whether of gold or steel, or iron or platinum, ... big or small, rough or delicate.1 For this it is necessary, together with the theological virtues, to practise all the other Christian virtues which are habits built upon a human foundation but raised to a higher order by divine grace. These invigorate and sustain our efforts, giving us the necessary drive to go ahead without discouragement, in accordance with the needs of each soul and at the pace set by God for each one.

Prudence and daring Our Lord gathers together his disciples and sends them off, two by two, around the towns of Israel to preach the Kingdom of Heaven. He forewarns them however about the difficulties which they will encounter in their apostolic mission. He wants them to be prepared, and to warn them he uses this simile, Behold, I send you out as lambs in the midst of wolves.2 In any work of apostolate it is possible for discouragement to set in because there is no shortage of difficulties, and sometimes quite

1 The Way, 484 2 Lk 10:3

At God’s pace 153

considerable ones. What is needed to overcome this, in addition to the theological virtues, is an enterprising prudence and a determination to be both daring and simple, without any kind of complex. So be wise as serpents and innocent as doves.3 Prudence shows us how to push ahead, while daring, which is the fruit of love, leads us to put into practice without any hesitation what ever supernatural sense dictates. The Apostles must surely have asked themselves what were the human resources for the apostolate, the means with which to fight dangers and overcome obstacles. Carry no purse, no bag, no sandals,4 is the first and disconcerting reply of Jesus. Before anything else, Christ demands of those he is sending a total abandonment and an absolute trust in his word. At the outset he dispenses with all human means so that they learn as soon as possible to launch out with daring, relying only on the supernatural means. Later he will tell them: When I sent you 5 They will out with no purse or bag or sandals, did you lack anything? agree that, indeed, they had all they needed. When he afterwards advises them that, from then on, they should obtain and use those human resources, it would have been quite firmly impressed upon them that the only thing which cannot be lacking in apostolic work is faith in both the divine command and the message of peace they were to carry. Our Lord also instructs them how to do apostolate. Whatever house you enter, first say: Peace be to this house ..., and remain in the same house.6 Their preaching has to begin in a family group. Afterwards, it will spread to the rest of the town. It is a measure of prudence which shows them how apostolic daring has to follow certain channels and develop gradually in stages. The disciples set off with enthusiasm for the mission they had

3 Mt 10:16 4 Lk 10:4 5 Lk 22:35 6 Lk 10:5-7 154 The apostolic mission

received and with the fire of Christ’s word in their hearts, forewarned that they would find places where they would not be well received. Even then they were to announce: Know this, that the Kingdom of God has come near.7 The difficulties of the apostolate, with the internal and the external obstacles the apostle has to combat, have not changed substantially. The word of Our Lord is still valid today, with the same force and identical demanding nature as then. The same weapons are still needed, and the same virtues and dispositions, in order to overcome resistance. In the first place, the apostle must fight against the inertia that slows him down from within. This is the weight of our fallen nature holding us back from getting on with the task. It is because of this that our Father told us: To begin is already to have half the job done. It requires an effort to shift ourselves, but we have to get moving, we have to launch out, with a sense of responsibility and with the awareness that we are leaven for the whole mass.8 We must also act in this way when human means are lacking. We need to bring into play a daring which the prudence of the flesh would call rashness, but which is grounded in a firm faith. A real and living faith is called for to begin a work of apostolate without adequate human resources, especially in the case of corporate activities. Yet the spirit of the Work, which always prompts us to approach things realistically, demands the development of the corporate works, for these add to the field of personal apostolate, even at the start. Nobody should feel satisfied with himself if he has a certain number of friends whom he is drawing closer to the Work and from among whom vocations come. We must all feel in addition, the weight of the corporate works and lend a hand in the measure and way the Directors suggest.

7 Lk 10:11 8 From our Father At God’s pace 155

Our prudence is not human but supernatural. That is why prudence does not hold us back, but rather pushes us to launch out, to make ourselves available, to make suggestions, to offer solutions, and to show daring. To get a corporate work going, even if at the beginning it be modest in scope, when stable work has hardly begun in the place, is not recklessness but a prudent and heroic way of being daring. What we want is souls for Christ. Prudence dominates reckless impulses. It rejects projects that lack foundation, but it points out the path to follow. Daring makes us launch out without hesitation in this direction, sustained all times by faith, especially when exclusively human considerations lead us to think the project is too difficult and risky. We are afraid neither of life or death, which is why we do not back away from the obstacles. The means we have are: the specific grace of our divine calling, the formation the Work gives us, and our own correspondence. These are powerful enough means to overcome them all obstacles. Our conviction that the Work is the Work of God, Opus Dei, gives rise to our daring, our readiness to Christianise all environments, and our determination to start all kinds of apostolic works, both corporate and personal. Our friends are attracted by this living faith and by our determined and energetic way of putting into practice what we advise them. They feel themselves drawn and encouraged to play a part. Thus are born those solid vocations which are the fruit of a daring and heroic apostolate. If this were not the case, false vocations would come, attracted by a comfortable life which is not our way. With such means, being the light and salt of Christ, with the faith and formation we receive, we launch out as determined apostles, relying on that promise of Our Lord: Heaven and earth will pass away, but my words will not pass away.9

9 Mt 24:35 156 The apostolic mission

The freedom of self-giving God has made man free. Freedom forms an integral part of his whole existence and marks out the path he must take to reach eternal life. His meritorious acts must proceed from a totally free will, moved by divine grace, which continually opts for God above finite goods at every crossroads in his life. Internal freedom of the will and freedom of action, does not admit any external coercion. In the Work we love freedom. We have given it to Our Lord, freely, out of love, and because we wanted to, in order to gain it back in a much higher form:– in libertatem gloriae filiorum Dei.10 We show those who regard this self-giving as a loss the reality of our lives with the beauty of a constant readiness to serve. We show them the force of our serviam!, which requires our complete freedom so that we are able to surrender our will to God. For us any other freedom would be slavery. We love our freedom and that of others because it is only when we are very free that we can serve God. This is what we are from the first moment of our dedication until the hour of our death. We came to the Work because we wanted to,11 corresponding to the grace of God who called us, and we carry on wanting to at every moment of our life. This fact is of particular importance in the apostolate and in proselytism, since it is vitally important to safeguard the full freedom of those who come to the Work. The decision we made to follow this divine call was totally free, and it must always remain so for those who join our way. Our proselytistic action is never done by force and we always take into account the freedom of souls, because the grace of God and this holy freedom are the very conditions for our proselytism. It is God who gives the vocation and the grace necessary to correspond, while at the same time respecting man’s freedom.

10 Rom 8:21 11 From our Father At God’s pace 157

Our proselytism is Opus Dei, cooperation with divine grace. We do this by means of prayer and mortification, by word and by example, so that souls, with the grace of God, may come to know and love our way. We talk to them about generosity with God, and about the greatness of a vocation. We help them to correspond to the call of God, but in the end it will always be the fruit of free choice. In this way, with the help we can give, not only is their freedom to follow their vocation or not diminished in any way, but it finds in fact its fullest expression. To choose a path a man has to know it. The better he knows it, the more freely he will decide; the better he understands the greatness of God’s loving choice in a vocation, the more voluntary his response will be: veritas liberavit vos.12 Love for souls leads us to try by all means that those called by God may be generous towards him. When it comes to a final decision, however, we carefully avoid any coercion. It is grace that must work in the soul. We intensely dislike any forcing of the will, for force is always the weapon of the weak, unnecessary for those who cooperate with God. It is with this freedom of spirit that we have come to the Work. Once we are on this divine path the same freedom governs our perseverance which is the fruit of God’s grace, and on our part, of love. Our freedom continues. In the Work, nobody could feel held against his will because there is no room for any coercion amongst us. It is completely opposed to our spirit. The door is open at all times. Anyone who decides to leave can do so whenever he wishes and his freedom will find no obstacles. In this matter, however, just as in proselytism, there is perfect harmony between love for freedom and love for souls. Charity moves us to give our lives in order that everyone should persevere. It is a logical consequence of our proselytistic zeal that we should be

12 Jn 8:32 158 The apostolic mission

concerned above all else for the perseverance and faithfulness of those with a vocation for the Work. We pray for them and, always allowing the fullest freedom, we do all that is necessary to help them persevere. To act otherwise would not only be a lack of charity, but also of justice, because we have been in part the cause of their vocation, and for someone to lose their vocation is the greatest misfortune imaginable. If one day, somebody were not to see his way clearly, our Father has said, it would be pointed out to him that he has complete freedom to leave. Furthermore, in the Work, although we are all needed, nobody is indispensable, not even the Father who is the Founder. He would be made to see that he should not feel coerced. However, while respecting this freedom, we would immediately use all the necessary human and supernatural means to give him back his sight, and to prevent him throwing overboard his happiness and his very life. We would pray for him and talk to him with infinite affection, with the very same refinement and concern that we would like to be shown to us if we happened to be in the same sad situation.13 Freedom and the grace of God; freedom and love: both combinations are necessary for proselytism and for perseverance. Whoever comes along our way comes because he wants to, and because God has given him his grace to which he corresponds out of love. Thereafter he perseveres because he wants to and because God carries on giving him his grace to which he, for his part, carries on corresponding out of love, with a completely free desire to serve.

Tenacity in the apostolate The means which our Father laid down for each of us to cooperate in the divine task of presenting souls with the problem of their vocation

13 From our Father At God’s pace 159

are these: Pray, offer sacrifices, and work on them with your example and with your word.14 When we seek this end and use on our part all the means to achieve it, without omitting any of them, there is no trace of violence or of undue interference in souls. We are fulfilling an imperative command of Christ,15 who says to us: go into all the world and preach the Gospel to the whole creation.16 He has set us an example: Follow me,17 he said to the first Twelve, who immediately left everything behind and followed him. In the same way, he invited the rich young man who, on hearing his words, chose not to accept Jesus’ invitation and went away sad.18 Ecce sto ad ostium et pulso,19 behold I stand at the door and knock. Present these words of St John, wrote our Founder, for the consideration of those souls on whom you are working for the Work. It is not you 20 who are calling but Christ himself! It is because of this that we are persevering in proselytism. If you persevere, if you are tenacious, and tenacity is indispensable for proselytism, a moment will come in which you can cry out to them in nomine Iesu Christi Nazareni, surge et ambula!, in the name of Jesus Christ of Nazareth, rise up and walk! (Acts 3:1-10)21 This tenacity certainly makes our proselytism persistent but it never takes the form of coercion. This is what our Father has said: We are more concerned than anybody to ensure that there is nobody in the Work who lacks a vocation.22

14 From our Father 15 From our Father, Instruction, 19 March 1934 16 Mk 16:15 17 Mt 4:19 18 Cf Mt 19:22 19 Rev 3:20 20 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934 21 Ibid. 22 From our Father 160 The apostolic mission

Tenacity in proselytism cannot take the place of the will of God. If God does not grant a specific soul the grace of a vocation, we, who are simply vehicles of his grace, cannot give it to him. Thus, our insistence must match Christ’s zeal but it can go no further. This very constancy leads us to do whatever we can so that the voice of God may not be ignored by any soul to whom he has made his call: amice, ascende superius,23 friend, go up higher. Jesus, furthermore, is urging us on this task at every moment: Go out to the highways and hedges, and compel people to come in, that my house may be filled.24 This holy coercion is necessary. Compelle intrare,25 Our Lord tells us, because in the struggle between the grace of God and the old man, the latter, clinging on to his own miserable things, can end up losing sight of the greatness of the divine vocation he has received. In these circumstances, when there is clear resistance, we are under an obligation to give the holy help that raises the soul up to a high place, from which vantage point it may have a better view of the divine demands and the glory he has allotted to those who follow him: The eyes of your hearts enlightened, that you may know what is the hope to which he has called you, what are the riches of his glorious inheritance in the saints.26 Man does sometimes resist the divine call. He shuts himself up in the castle of his own thoughts and personal ambitions. He tries to take no notice of the special demands that God makes on his life because he wants him to be holy. When presenting the crisis of vocation – a task we members of Opus Dei are under an obligation to carry out – this attitude of resistance on the part of souls becomes more evident. Ecce sto ad ostium ...,27 behold I stand at the door and knock. But at

23 Lk 14:10 24 Lk 14:23 25 Ibid. 26 Eph 1:18 27 Rev 3:20

At God’s pace 161

times on the other side the soul, in its own dwelling, decides to turn in on itself and defend itself. Even confronted with the example of the lives of those of us who have left everything and the “good bargain” God offers us, the desire for an earthly way of life lived at its own sweet pace, continues to predominate in man, wounded by original sin. “God is here as well, isn’t he?”, he appears to argue from a merely human standpoint. Our tenacity makes us reply that there are souls whom God wants closer to himself. In these cases, to ignore the specific calling of God, even for humanly legitimate reasons and motives, can prove fatal for the soul. For the divine call to a higher life is a very special gift which God does not grant to everyone. He also gives the person upon whom he bestows it, the grace to correspond. To say No to this call constitutes an outright opposition to the will of God, even if it is a question here not of precept but of counsel. In these cases, when the voice of the divine summons is heard the person invited puts up a shield made of human prudence. Then the voice has to pierce the shield for the person to hear. In our work of proselytism we have witnessed the existence of this shield composed of defensive expressions full of practical sense, coldness, and reasoning which must not be allowed to diminish our tenacity and which must make us turn with hope to God. We then propose the direct route to the summit – which with all its difficulty, is a happy one – and the person replies speaking of easy and uncertain wanderings along indirect paths, a roundabout route and taking the longest possible way. Don’t you long to shout to those young men and women all around you: Fools, leave those worldly things that shackle the heart and very often degrade it ..., 28 leave all that and come with us in search of love? To tell these people that God wants them in his service, that with

28 The Way, 790 162 The apostolic mission

him there is no room for calculation, stinginess, opportunism or superficiality; in a word to say that God wants everything, is not to force, but to cooperate with God in his desire for labourers. The immense wheat-field of this world lies waiting for them. There is freedom of choice as to whether an advice is followed or not, therefore, to reject the call is not in itself a serious sin. It does, however, undermine the ability to look at God face to face, knowing oneself to be faithfully fulfilling his specific will. Obeying God in secondary matters cannot, of course, make up for that prior disobedience to the divine call. Fear, however, can never be a justifiable reason for giving oneself unconditionally to God. Love is the only cause of this self-giving. This is the argument we use. Our proselytism spreads the divine fire in the hearts of men. Let us relive, once again, that scene from the Gospel. We are walking along the road with Jesus; or rather, Jesus is there but hidden in our lives. At that early hour of the morning we have met some men going in the same direction and, going up to them, we have walked along a stretch of the way with them. We have spoken to them about the marvels of the call of Jesus. At the beginning, perhaps, their attitude was that of the disciples of Emmaus: Their eyes were kept from recognising him.29 Yet we carry on tenaciously, despite their failing to get to the point at the beginning. On reaching a crossroads, we make as if to carry on, and they say to Christ who is there within us, Stay with us, for it is toward evening and the day is now far spent.30 It is Love that has obtained a free response and the love He demanded has been the result of our tenacity and zeal.

29 Lk 24:16 30 Lk 24:29

At God’s pace 163

Humility and magnanimity Go into the village opposite you, and immediately you will find an ass tied, and a colt with her; untie them and bring them to me. If anyone says anything to you, you shall say, “the Lord has need of them,” and he will send them immediately.31 The disciples went off to look for us, children of the slavery of original sin and its consequences. The difficulties that we raised were dissolved with just a few words: The Lord has need of them. Proselytism always has this strength and this confidence. In this way, we were brought along. Jesus Christ wanted to enter Jerusalem on us, the most humble beast of burden he could find, so that all men should understand that their King was gentle and lowly in heart,32 that their Lord arrived in a peaceful fashion. For those who could not cope with a divine clarity, there would be a human voice, with a message from God. For those who at the beginning were not even able to understand this, there would be the clumsy and stumbling trot of a donkey. Let us set out once more along the way, carrying Jesus in our souls in grace. We are not the rider, nor even the best of the beasts of burden. We are not better than anyone else. We are, quite simply, the object of a free and disproportionate choice. Quite deliberately so, for thus we will be just one more among men and the drinking trough should be no higher than lowly beasts can reach. It will also be clear that whatever good arises will be entirely the work of God. It is not the stone drinking trough that the beasts of burden seek, but the water itself and also the capacity to hold water which the craftsman has carved out of the stone. Although people do not always realise it and they sometimes tell us otherwise, what they are looking for in us is God, the spirit God has given to his Work, and the grace of which we

## Mt 21:2-3 32 Mt 11:29 164 The apostolic mission

are inadequate depositaries. The way we follow has no other purpose. At the entry into Jerusalem it was not for the donkey that the Jews laid down their garments and palm branches, but for Jesus. This is a permanent conviction which is present to us in all our actions. It is something we must keep telling ourselves over and over again: I can do nothing on my own authority.33 If at any time we were to do something by ourselves, it would not then be the work of God, Opus Dei. In understanding this and putting it into practice, lies all effectiveness. It is a question of disappearing, and for this it is necessary to be humble and to grasp the spirit of the Work very well.34 We have been chosen to bear God with us, and to transmit the spirit of his Work. This is the sole reason for our apostolic work. This confers upon us the great responsibility of being always vigilant so as not to change anything, so as to see that souls do not get attached to ourselves, to know how to refer souls to God, and to make them see that whatever they come to admire in our life, our words and our actions, belongs to God. We must help the people with whom we are dealing to reason in the same way as the man blind from birth: From the beginning of the world, it has not been heard that any man has opened the eyes of one born blind. Unless this man were of God, he could not do anything.35 Moreover, the more you are of God, the more effective you will be, and this is exactly what our Father tells us: If my children are holy and humble, they will be effective throughout the world; the more humble they are, the more effective they will be. We have not come to command, but to obey. We have come to serve. Non veni ministrari sed ministrare (Matt 20:28). How often I have meditated myself and made others meditate upon that Illum oportet crescere, me autem minui (John 3:30).36

33 Jn 5:30 34 From our Father 35 Jn 3:30 36 From our Father At God’s pace 165

We are not better than others, but we do have more responsibility, namely, that of carrying God within us and of spreading his spirit. In order to do this, we must have a holy hunger to fill ourselves with God and to know his doctrine; to identify ourselves with him; to be a living incarnation of the spirit of the Work that must shine forth in us like the splendid form of a work of art which derives from the artist. At most, matter offers only docility, that is to say, the capacity to allow itself to be formed. Possessing all this richness, we are to fill men with good things, according to those words of Holy Scripture: My son, perform your tasks with meekness; then you will be loved by those whom God accepts. The greater you are, the more you must humble yourself; so you will find favour in the sight of the Lord.37 We will find grace for ourselves, and grace for the souls with whom we are dealing. All that is great, noble and worthy in human creatures is a free gift from God, and whatever is defective and imperfect comes from the weakness, feebleness and limitations of our nature. You would take a tumble every day, our Father used to remind us, if you did not have all the graces which God gives you, and in particular the grace of your vocation.38 Humility enables us to see ourselves small and incapable when we consider our own defects and our own nothingness. Whereas magnanimity, which is greatness of spirit and a capacity for great undertakings, makes us feel ourselves all powerful, capable of doing marvellous things, as long as we rely entirely on God. We are then able to cry out with St Paul: omnia possum in eo qui me confortat, I can do all things in him who strengthens me.39 It is precisely the fact that we cannot rely on ourselves which more than anything else helps us to rely

37 Sir 3:19-20 38 From our Father 39 Phil 4:13 166 The apostolic mission

on God, and which also most moves God to sustain us. We have been called to a great undertaking. It did not matter at all that, for his triumphant entry into Jerusalem, the disciples brought Our Lord a simple donkey to be his mount. Jesus had said that it was all that he needed. All that is asked of us is that we be humbly faithful.

## WRITE WHAT YOU SEE {#cuaderno.5.16}

The following words of St Paul sound today more promising than ever: for, every one who calls upon the name of the Lord will be saved.1 This is why the questions that the Apostle asks immediately afterwards implies a special responsibility for Christians. But how are men to call upon him in whom they have not believed? And how are they to believe in him of whom they have never heard? And how are they to hear, 2 The promise contains a special fruitfulness without a preacher? nowadays, stemming from God’s call to us to consider the tremendous scope that there is for spreading his teaching by means of the printed word. Hence, when we hear it said that faith comes from what is heard, and what is heard comes by the preaching of Christ,3 our apostolic zeal leads us to look at the means available for spreading ideas far and wide. In a sense, perhaps, we are in a situation analogous to that of the Apostles when they received this command from Our Lord: Go into all the world and preach the gospel to the whole creation.4 The whole world opens up before us, and the task is immense and difficult. Our personal dealings, which are indispensable, and a key element in our apostolate, are not sufficient. God continually points out to us great expanses of the world where, at least for the present, we cannot work personally. He also shows us enormous masses of people, very near to

1 Rom 10:13 2 Rom 10:14 3 Rom 10:17 4 Mk 16:15 168 The apostolic mission

us, in the places where we work, whom the saving word of Jesus Christ does not reach, or reaches in a distorted form. God insists, urging us: Cry aloud, spare not, lift up your voice like a trumpet.5 We must multiply our words, make them resound more widely, and cry out in such a way that we are heard everywhere.

Apostolate of public opinion A good part of the mission of a Christian nowadays consists in disseminating the doctrine of Our Lord and in refuting the errors that oppose it. This must be done in and from every place, from every profession and intellectual condition, from every political and social situation. It is especially true of our apostolic mission which we carry out in the world, and which makes use of the world. “Among the principal obligations that we owe to God and to the Church is the duty that each person has of using all possible means to defend Christian truths and to refute errors.”6 Today, a great Christian protest must be staged; and all of us, making use of the means offered by modern technology, the organs of public opinion, have a part to play in it. No one should be happy with the personal possibilities that his own environment offers him. We must make ourselves heard much farther afield. There should be no limits to our work of spreading the doctrine that we have received, which is for all a pledge of salvation. Of those who embark upon this mission, “those who write in defence of religion, especially in the newspapers,” Pope Leo XIII says: “We know well their ideals and their keenness to attain those ideals, and we cannot but render them due praise.”7 Apart from the personal apostolate that we carry out at all times and

5 Is 58:1 6 Leo XIII, Encyclical Sapientiae christianae, 10 January 1890 7 Leo XIII, Encyclical Cum multa, 8 December 1882

Write what you see 169

in all places, we must reach, as a matter of urgency the great multitudes hungering for doctrine. You cannot turn your back on your fellowmen: you have to be anxious to make them happy.8 Consequently, we should be deeply moved on seeing them turned so often into playthings of error, or of the malice of a few. “Look at the great havoc that ignorance of divine things wreaks in souls ... In preference to every other work, and calling upon all the effort, earnestness and constancy of which you are capable, we urge you to watch carefully so that the knowledge of Christian doctrine may penetrate completely into the minds and hearts of all.”9 A terrible divine threat is heard for those who make use of those countless souls for their own commercial purposes. Woe to those who decree iniquitous decrees, and the writers who keep writing oppression, to turn aside the needy from justice.10 We must use positive action to oppose this evil. The Popes, particularly in the twentieth century, have never tired of recalling the urgent need of producing a lot of very good publications, and of working intensely in this field, “especially in magazines and newspapers which are all the more effective when they are more widely circulated. By “good publications” we mean those which do not contain anything contrary to the principles of faith and to the rules of morality, and which at the same time propagate, proclaim and illustrate such principles and rules. There is no need to demonstrate the quality and extent of the educational effectiveness of such publications, because it is obvious from everyday experience. Equally, we can see proof of the enormous harm that is worked, especially among young people, by bad publications, which are frequently more widely circulated than good ones. This confirms the words of Christ: the sons of this world are

8 The Way, 32 9 St Pius X, Encyclical Acerbo nimis, 15 April 1905 10 Is 10:1-2 170 The apostolic mission

wiser in their own generation than the sons of light.11 Therefore we must, at all costs, use good publications to oppose bad ones, applying that ancient principle: ‘contraria contrariis curantur’.”12 Apart from those who devote themselves to the press as their profession, it is necessary for all who are able – and many are able – to take advantage of opportunities to write, drawing upon their work and professional knowledge. In this way they can give their fellow workers, those who share their condition, and everyone generally, a positive Christian viewpoint on specific problems or events. Every discerning person is ordinarily in a position to comment in a Christian manner among his friends and acquaintances on whatever happens around him. It is just a case of doing the same thing, but in writing. When St John the Evangelist was on the island of Patmos, he received the vision of the Revelation from God, together with the command to write it down. I was in the Spirit on the Lord’s day, and I heard behind me a loud voice like a trumpet saying, “Write what you see in a book and send it to the seven churches”.13 Moses, too, had received this explicit command: And the Lord said to Moses, “Write this as a memorial in a book”.14 In order to extend God’s teaching widely among men and to ensure its permanence, we must make use of the written word. This is because a book, or a magazine, or a newspaper, once printed, acquires a life of its own. It is like another apostle. It is handed around. Its ideas are commented upon, assimilated and repeated. It is difficult to calculate just how far it reaches. By it the activity of the apostle is multiplied. We have first-hand experience, for example, of the enormous effectiveness in the apostolate and even in proselytism of The Way and

11 Lk 16:8 12 Pius XI, Letter Ex officiosis litteris, 10 November 1933 13 Rev 1:10-11 14 Ex 17:14

Write what you see 171

other works of our Father. The ideas that faith and love suggest to us for our professional work acquire, through the printed word, the power of breaking through the limitations imposed by the “here and now”.

Looking for the opportunity The members of the Work must realise how important this task is, and treat it as their personal responsibility. In an old internal document, our Father wrote that it would be good to set up something like “speech training” and “writing classes”. In this way we will avoid the common situation wherein men of outstanding talent are unable to write, with at least a certain degree of correctness, or to speak in public.15 We can detect in these words the desire that we should all be in a position, no matter where we are or what our job is, to help in the apostolate of public opinion. Each one of us must respond to this concern of our Father by doing something definite and practical, in accordance with what the Directors suggest and with own personal initiative. In order to be able to play an active and direct part in the apostolate of public opinion we must begin with the necessary basic preparation. For example, assuming of course that we already have proper doctrinal formation, we have to make the effort to learn how to be at home in using the written word. Correct and lively writing is both an art and a science and it can be mastered by many people. But it requires study, preparation, training, practice, knowledge of rules, familiarity with the classical works of literature, and so on. Only in this way can we learn to handle words skilfully. It is essential that we know how to spell, and that we be aware of the structure and syntax at least of our own language. We must learn how to write with style and good taste. This is

15 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 172 The apostolic mission

all part of the gift of tongues.16 Although it is not an arduous task, reserved for a select few, this preparation cannot be left to chance nor simply taken for granted: it requires a continuous, orderly, and controlled effort. After this preparation comes the moment to put it into practice. Among people who are very conscientious and upright it is not uncommon to find a sort of false humility that leads them to think that they are never properly prepared for putting into writing what they think. They remain passive and idle, and perhaps give an opportunity to those enemies of God, whose heads are devoid of ideas, to pass themselves off as scholarly, and so reach positions they should never attain.17 In the face of this passivity and neglect on the part of many Christians, the enemies of Christ with their unbelievable arrogance fill the world with their words. We have to make up our minds to write, and to write a lot. We have an abundance of doctrine and it is up to us to distribute it liberally. We must publish. We must resolutely set about filling the world of the printed word with light and with salt. Many publications are awaiting and in need of this personal collaboration on the part of each of us. We must sow Christian encouragement everywhere. Even a simple pamphlet can be a good vehicle of apostolate. Now write what you see,18 God made his angel say to St John. And I heard a voice from heaven saying “write this”.19 Throughout the whole of the Apocalypse this word, which is like a command for us as well, is repeated again and again: write. As a further consequence of the urgent task before us, we need to pay particular attention to personal apostolate and proselytism with people who work in the press, in cinema, in radio and television. We

16 From our Father 17 From our Father 18 Rev 1:19 19 Rev 14:13

Write what you see 173

must get to know them and talk with them, laying the foundations for a deep and sincere personal friendship with them. We must teach them the doctrine of Our Lord, and offer God a lot of prayer and mortification for them in order to win them over to the cause of the apostolate of public opinion. Perhaps also the flame of a vocation to the Work may be lit within them. We shall fill the world with printed matter,20 our Father has said. This is how we shall fulfil the aim of the apostolate of public opinion: disseminating the doctrine of Christ; refuting errors; making known to Catholics throughout the world the activities of their brothers in the faith; trying to bring to everybody our vibrant zeal and our spirit, which is the spirit of the Gospel; endeavouring to make the truth shine forth at all moments, at every opportunity, and from every part of society. This is the Christian protest the world needs. This apostolate of public opinion, which has produced and will continue to produce so much fruit in service of the Church, was entrusted in a special way by our Father to St Catherine of Siena. I have always had devotion to Saint Catherine, he said on one occasion, because of her love for the Church and the Pope, and because of the courage that she showed in always speaking clearly whenever it was necessary, moved precisely by that same love.21 May love for the Church move us all, with the help of Our Lady, to take an active part in this great and fruitful apostolate of public opinion.

20 From our Father 21 From our Father, Get-together, 30 April 1964, in Crónica, May 1964, p.61

## THE SEED-BED OF THE WORK {#cuaderno.5.17}

The Work of God has to be extended everywhere, affirming the reign of Jesus Christ forever.1 Fitted into the army of the Church, our apostolate and our work with souls in all parts of the world is the Work of God, the fulfilment of the divine will. We are instruments of God’s action, free instruments endowed with a clear understanding of their mission which is a supernatural sowing in soil belonging to God. We are instruments who have to do our work lovingly and faithfully. The work of St Raphael is an important part of this universal work, indeed it is the seed-bed of the Work.2 Its immediate goal is to select young people of all races, nations, and social classes, in order to give them a deep and practical Christian formation and to turn them into Christians whose lives are consistent with their vocation. It makes them souls of interior life and apostolic zeal within their own environment, in the practice of their profession, and in the fulfilment of all their religious, family and civic obligations. This ascetical, doctrinal and apostolic formation that the work of St Raphael gives them is the armament needed for their battles as Christians, as well as light and strength for their supernatural way, and does not necessarily suppose that they have any particular vocation. All this corresponds to the general Christian vocation, to the universal call to sanctity. It gives these fellows the means to correspond to this

1 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934 2 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942

The seed-bed of the Work 175

vocation in the circumstances of their ordinary lives. It is among these souls already forged in the ideals and practice of Christian life that later on decisions of dedication will spring up. It is God who gives the vocation. In fact, ordinarily, the greater part of our vocations will come from this previous work of formation, namely, the work of St Raphael, the mediate aim of which is to form vocations for the Work of God, moving the best of them to dedicate themselves to God in an apostolic celibacy (the work of St Michael) or forming them to be parents of families and people who help in our apostolates (the work of St Gabriel).3

The right time for sowing Youth is the time for formation. It is a period when, together with physical growth, the direction and meaning of an entire lifetime are fixed. It is a time for ideals and for love, a time when the soul opens with a dynamic receptivity to the light of doctrine, to the attraction of love, and to the possibility of engaging generously in executing ambitious enterprises. Once youth has passed, normally men of any worth have mapped out the course of their lives. Then they have either achieved their aims already and they believe themselves successful with the result that it is quite useless to speak to them of ideals that are not part of their egoistic plans; or, if they have not reached their targets, they are convinced they are failures or beaten men, and they resign themselves to being just a part of the general mass of their social class or profession so that it would take a real miracle to make them react and to fill them with the ambition of forming themselves as leaders. I am not saying, our Father concludes, that we cannot find vocations among mature people, but it is certainly difficult.4 From this

3 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 4 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934 176 The apostolic mission

group there will indeed come vocations of Associates, Supernumeraries and also Numeraries, and these will even be abundant in absolute numbers. Relatively speaking, however, the greater part will come from the work done with young people. It is the best time of life for an effective sowing, and, just when they begin to struggle, the best time to give them all the means with which to win. We must teach people to love God Our Lord, to love chastity, purity, with all the struggle it entails, because it is really not all that hard. We have to give them all the means with which to win in the ascetical struggle, and to teach them to go forward for love’s sake.5 Youth is a broad concept and is not fixed by strict age limits. It may depend on the country, personal circumstances of upbringing, surroundings and way of life. By youth we mean the time in which the needs of the soul make themselves felt, when the intellect responds to doctrine, and the heart makes known its demands. It is the time when man begins to face up to his future, when his freedom seeks a guideline and his love for an object. It is the age in which the greatest ideals and the most noble ambitions slowly but surely take root. The age of readiness for formation begins very early, especially in some countries and circumstances of life. Very young people, many of them in the early years of secondary school, are already the good soil in which you can effectively sow the seed of an ideal of sanctity and of professional prestige. This is especially true in places where beginning university or work already involves grave dangers of ideological and moral disorientation. Already at this early stage, therefore, young people need the help of the formation that the work of St Raphael is able to give them. This is of a spiritual, doctrinal and apostolic kind, and is given person to person and through all the various activities of this work. All of the work with the people of St Raphael, even when

5 From our Father The seed-bed of the Work 177

they are very young, is marvellous. They live in the midst of the world, they know about life and they learn how to struggle, so that if afterwards they give themselves fully they know what they are doing. I have insisted a lot, our Father said, on your doing work of St Raphael with young people, not only with students but with youngsters of all social classes.6 On the other hand, even before the young people are ready, in terms of age or other circumstances, to receive the specific formation of the work of St Raphael, there is a prior stage of preparing the furrow in which the seed we sow is later to fall. This is because sowing the seed is not the first task. There are others that come before it. The sowing is neither the first nor the last task.7 Among these previous tasks, which can be done at any age and in any circumstances, is that of choosing the ground in which you are going to sow. A rigorous selection is necessary so that youngsters can enter fully into the work of St Raphael and take part effectively in the different means of formation. The selection is done through personal contact, the apostolate of friendship and confidence, and also, once friendship has developed a little, through those other means of formation which are a means of selection themselves: the visits to the poor of Our Lady, catechism classes, and so forth. The youngsters can take part in these activities without needing to be St Raphael people. We have to form these young people as the leaven of Jesus Christ in the heart of society, as leaders capable of exercising a deep and wide influence within their social, professional, and family environments. For this reason they must possess certain qualities which can perhaps be summarised by saying that all of them should have at least the prospect of a possible vocation to the Work, even though not all will

6 From our Father 7 From our Father 178 The apostolic mission

actually have one. The criterion of selection is, therefore, similar to that required for membership of the Work. There is no room for egoists, cowards, the indiscreet, pessimists, the lukewarm, fools, loafers, the timid, or the frivolous. There is, on the other hand, a place for the sick, who are God’s favourites, and all people who have a big heart, even though their weaknesses may have been quite considerable.8 From among our friends, who must be very many as we are interested in all souls, we have to bring along the very best to take part in the work of St Raphael. These, too, must be many because Christians with interior life have to be many and many again the vocations which should come from among them: he who sows sparingly will also reap sparingly, and he who sows bountifully will also reap bountifully.9 Every St Raphael fellow has be the good soil10 in which the divine seed of sanctity and apostolic zeal can germinate and grow. God himself gives intrinsic effectiveness to the seed of formation they receive, but the soil has to be of good quality. If the soil is stony or shallow, if it has not been tilled or is full of weeds, the grain either cannot take root or dries up, the harvest is lost. “Why was the greater part of the seed lost?” asks St John Chrysostom. “It was not the fault of the sower but of the earth that received the seed: that is, through the fault of the soul that did not want to receive the word.”11 It is, therefore, necessary to prepare the ground properly, to prepare it thoroughly, well, and generously so that the effectiveness of the sowing will be all the greater. Later on, the youngsters, whether they receive our vocation or not, have to be seed corn, select wheat, which will produce a much greater harvest for the granaries of Holy Church and for the Kingdom of Heaven.

8 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934 9 2 Cor 9:6 10 Mt 13:8 11 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae, 44, 3

The seed-bed of the Work 179

With the necessary care Taking part in the various means of formation of the work of St Raphael binds these youngsters in same way to each other and also to the Work of God. These are holy bonds that bring obligations upon us all and demand something of all of us. They unite us in our concern for sanctity and for the apostolate, and hence for acquiring the necessary formation. These ties are the foundations of effectiveness and the first step towards a possible vocation, to which the one who receives it must afterwards freely respond. These youngsters come to our Centres to receive formation when already inclined to piety and to the apostolate. They have a certain grounding in the virtues and in an upright life, the very conditions of selection, and this is reinforced by personal contact. We clearly explain to them, before they enter fully into the work of St Raphael, what it consists of, in such a way that the decision they make is not just free but keen and wholehearted. This decision binds them to us and leads to a mutual interchange of rights and obligations, of responsibility and affection. This whole background enables the youngsters to be ready to receive a vocation in the atmosphere of our Centres. We give spiritual and human formation to the people who come to our Centres of St Raphael and are old enough to know well what they are doing. This formation will enable them to receive the grace of vocation, and they are, in general, fully aware of what this formation implies.12 That is why we can and should consider them united to us in a certain way, and they themselves should soon be aware of this and feel that the things of the Work are their own. Although the St Raphael people, as such, do not form an association of any kind, this task of formation has a definite unity about it and is directed. It is perfectly well structured and has specific activities. It is a work that has its different phases and courses like the preparatory

12 From our Father 180 The apostolic mission

course, professional courses and monthly days of recollection. At first these are more elementary and directed to the heart. Afterwards they are deeper and more doctrinal, but always adapted to the capacity of the youngsters and suited to their condition as Christians who must sanctify themselves and sanctify others in the ordinary course of their working lives and of their human relationships in the world. The seed we have for these youngsters is divine: the seed is the word of God.13 It is seed of the best quality and we make every effort to guard it faithfully, according to the instructions we have received. It is a seed that will always give fruit if before, during and after the sowing all the necessary care has been taken. This takes time and careful attention. “We do not have to think that our work has been reduced just because Our Lord said a sower went out to sow.14 For the sower goes out on many other occasions to do other work; for example, to work on fallow land, to pull up weeds, to root out thorns, or to do other such things. But Our Lord went out to sow.”15 Our Lord went out to sow the seed that is effective in itself while to us he has entrusted many other tasks that are necessary for the seed to bear fruit. Our work is that of the farmer, and it is the hard working farmer who ought to have the first share of the crops.16 He has to till the soil well. The field will be ploughed and, besides a generous sowing of seed, will receive all the care of the farmer. Afterwards, with the blessing of God, will come the harvest.17 God always blesses, and he blesses all the more when there has been greater self-denial, when the work has been more faithful and the intention more supernatural. Dominus dabit benignitatem, et terra nostra dabit fructum suum (Ps 84:13). This

13 Lk 8:11 14 Mt 13:3 15 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae, 44, 3 16 2 Tim 2:6 17 From our Father The seed-bed of the Work 181

blessing of God is the origin of all good fruit and of the climate necessary for us to become saints in our lifetime and to “cultivate” saints who are his children. Dominus dabit benignitatem ... God is waiting for the fruit. If we do not give it him, we are taking it away from Him. It must not be fruit that has not developed or has been spoiled because we have not known how to give ourselves. God provides water, rain, sun and soil, but from us he expects sowing, transplanting and pruning. He expects us to look after the fruit for him with love; if need be, keeping the birds of the air from eating it.18 The work of St Raphael is a true nursery of select souls. It is a cultivation of great value that leads us to follow step by step the interior growth of each youngster, helping him overcome obstacles, strengthening and encouraging him, opening up horizons for him. At whatever cost in sacrifice we use all the right and proper means that are laid down. This growth of the soul requires time and good will, always presupposing, the action of grace. Selection guarantees good will, and continual care its continuance and even its increase. But time has to pass since the harvest cannot be rushed. Then the seed of divine life can mature and bear fruit. They are the good soil who, hearing the word, hold it fast in an honest and good heart, and bring forth fruit in patience.19 Besides all the work that is needed, God also counts on time for his work in souls to be done: The Kingdom of God is as if a man should scatter seed upon the ground, and should sleep and rise, night and day, and the seed should sprout and grow he knows not how. The earth produces of itself, first the blade, then the ear, then the full grain in the ear. But when the grain is ripe, at once he puts in the sickle because the harvest has come.20

18 From our Father, Meditation, 3 December 1961 19 Lk 8:15 20 Mk 4:26-29 182 The apostolic mission

We have to know how to wait, for the fruit will all come in good time. Ours must be a time of waiting that is active, busy and confident. Control your impatience, our Father asks of us, do the apostolate of proselytism calmly, slowly, at God’s pace, but without ever interrupting 21 There has to be a firm, heroic, the work, no matter how difficult it is! unshakeable constancy, like that of a good farmer, ready to wait for a long time if necessary. For tenacity is indispensable for proselytism.22 This tenacity must be optimistic and full of faith because God, who began a good work in you will bring it to completion.23 It will be active, ready to cooperate, untiringly determined, insistent in prayer, and always vigilant. The seed will grow and will bear fruit, thirtyfold, and sixtyfold, and a hundredfold.24 This now depends on God. We sow a bare kernel, perhaps of wheat or some other grain. But God gives it a body as he has chosen.25 No one goes away without fruit. The wheat produces its grain. With the work of formation we do good to many young people. They improve. They acquire light and virtues for their whole life. Ever after, though the new seed sometimes springs up a bit later, there remain ties of love and grateful affection that bind them to the Work and lead them to cooperate in its apostolates. From this work which is the seed-bed of the Work,26 many vocations blossom to a life of dedication in forms that are various but always the same and complete. How frankly you laughed when I advised you to put the years of your youth under the protection of Saint Raphael: ‘so that he will lead

21 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934 22 From our Father 23 Phil 1:6 24 Mk 4:20 25 1 Cor 15:37-38 26 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942

The seed-bed of the Work 183

you, like young Tobias, to a holy marriage, with a girl who is good and pretty and rich’, I told you jokingly. And then, how thoughtful you became! ... when I went on to advise you to put yourself also under the patronage of that young apostle John; in case God were to ask more of you.27 There will be abundant vocations of Supernumeraries, and vocations of Numeraries and Associates, all coming as the mature fruit of grace, with the correspondence of sower and soil.

The work of everyone “We are but simple farm hands, because it is God who does the sowing.”28 We are the instruments of God in this work of his, in his field. Neither he who plants, nor he who waters, is anything, but only God who gives the growth.29 Those who, called by God, dedicate themselves to these tasks “are just the servants of God, having nothing that does not come from God and, moreover, they act only exteriorly, whereas God acts upon the interior of people’s souls.”30 We can say to those who receive formation in our work of St Raphael: you are God’s field,31 “a field that produces fruit because of God’s action”.32 The work of St Raphael is a part of the Work of God, Opus Dei. We ourselves are simply instruments. The whole structure and the entire development of the work of St Raphael, taking into account the necessary flexibility and possibility of adapting itself to all environments and circumstances, has been outlined in all its essentials. We have to carry it out in this way, as instruments, just as our Father has

27 The Way, 360 28 St Augustine, Sermon 73, 3 29 1 Cor 3:7 30 St Thomas, Super I Epistolam ad Cor. lectura 31 1 Cor 3:9 32 St Thomas, Super I Epistolam ad Cor. lectura 184 The apostolic mission

taught us and, just as God has wanted it. The fruits are divine and so are the means to obtain them. These specific means, and not others, are demanded by the supernatural aim of our work. We have to sow a bare 33 in all its purity, and then we shall be able to obtain supernatural kernel fruits: who sows to the spirit, will from the spirit reap eternal life.34 In asking us to carry out this work God has entrusted this youth, the hope of the Work35 to us. These young people, whom God himself has placed in our hands, deserve all our care and affection. We have to love them really and truly. We do not regard them as strangers but as part of the Work, because in fact they are so juridically and ascetically. That is why it is very nice that there should be a lot of people around. In this way we can do them a lot of good. There should be a large nursery bed. Though there will also be people coming along who have not passed through the work of St Raphael, it is the apple of our eye and we have to prepare it very well.36 Though some of us are more directly dedicated to this work, it is the responsibility of all in some way. In whatever personal situation we may find ourselves, we have to keep up a lively concern for this work and pray for it with affection. It is, moreover, always possible to take part in some way, looking out for and preparing those who have to form themselves, or helping out in some particular task. When we have the work of St Raphael deep in our hearts, when we understand well its importance, we will naturally find ways of participating, in a thousand different ways which are always effective. Let us constantly entrust this vital work of ours to the maternal protection of Our Lady, who is our Mother and the Mother of these youngsters. Mater boni consilii, Spes nostra, Sedes Sapientiae.

33 1 Cor 15:37 34 Gal 6:8 35 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 36 From our Father

## A time to build {#cuaderno.5.18}


For everything there is a season, and a time for every matter under heaven: a time to be born, and a time to die; a time to plant, and a time to pluck up what is planted; a time to kill, and a time to heal; a time to break down, and a time to build up.1 A time to build. That time above all the time of youth, which is why our Father insists: You must try to get young people to come so that you can form them, and so save them a lot of difficulties. Vocations have to come from every social environment, from all fields; and it is easier to get them from among young people. Of course, people of all ages come to the Work: they come when they are forty and when they are fifty, and even older still, but we have to see it as a joyous thing, and be eager that young people should come along, who can begin their lives and their struggle with this ideal.2 It is when one is young that one has to lay the foundations and begin to build the interior edifice. This is why people come to our centres: for us to form them, for us to construct the spiritual edifice in them, for us to make them authentic Christians, living temples of God. We have to ensure that these youngsters seek God and that they find him in an intense Christian life, many of them in a vocation of dedication, to the point where we can say to them: “Think what a great

1 Eccles 3:1-3 2 From our Father 186 The apostolic mission

gift has been given to you, what a wonderful temple the Holy Spirit made in your soul.”3 For no one but God performs the work of sanctification in souls: Unless the Lord builds the house, those who build it labour in vain.4 God is the architect and in his name our Father, through a special divine vocation, has given us the rules of construction. The work of St Raphael contains all the means for building the unshakeable edifice of the interior life of the youngsters, solid all the way up from its foundations. In this work we are God’s fellow workers5 and they are God’s building.6 Our Lord gives the grace to build, grace that he infused into our Founder, who like St Paul can say: According to the commission of God given to me, like a skilled master builder, I laid a foundation,7 using the authority which the Lord has given me for building up and not for tearing down.8 A good part of the success of a structure lies in knowing the building regulations. The construction of any material building is governed by certain laws which have to be followed. If these rules are not followed, if the strength of the materials is not known, if you do not know how to calculate what foundations are needed, and so many other things, how stable will the house be? And when it is a case of building interior life, it is also necessary to observe certain specific regulations if we do not want the construction to totter and collapse. Our Father has taught us the precise way of building the spiritual edifice of the youngsters who approach Opus Dei, and he has given us well defined norms for the

3 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae, 73, 3 4 Ps 127:1 5 1 Cor 3:9 6 Ibid. 7 1 Cor 3:10 8 2 Cor 13:30

A time to build 187

Courses of Formation, which are the essential element in the work of St Raphael,9 and which therefore are unchangeable, identical, whatever the place or time. As in any building, there are also regulations which do not relate simply to the structure of the edifice, but which refer to finishes and decorations. These are necessary because they complete and perfect the work, but they are accidental.
