These details will and in fact do change: but the Courses of Formation, the Circles, never change, because they form both the foundation and the structure of the whole building which, with God’s grace, we have to erect in each youngster.

Broadening the base The first thing to do when you think about building is to look for a good site; and if you want to do a lot of building, you need a lot of land. A good work of St Raphael demands a broad base, getting to know a lot of youngsters, spreading out. Then, before beginning to build, before inviting a person to Courses of Formation, there must always be a period of getting acquainted. This is true especially for the younger fellows and for those who are further away from the faith. In this way, we make an initial selection, choosing the best from among all our friends: we do not want numbers but selection.10 Furthermore, we try to make sure that other brothers of ours get to know them as well. And before a fellow takes part in the weekly meeting, or rather, before he can attend the class of formation, the Director has to talk with him on his own.11 The Director should explain to him that he is coming to better himself, to give, to surrender himself; that he needs to make a sacrifice, even if it demands renunciation.

9 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 10 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 11 Ibid.

188 The apostolic mission

You should take precautions to avoid any young fellows coming for human motives, for what they can get out of it.12 Therefore, in that private conversation with the youngster who intends to attend the courses, make him see – our Father tells us – that our house is not a place for recreation – we do not have, nor will we have, even a billiard table – it is rather an “unpleasant” place, where they often ask if you pray, etc.; if you are good to your parents ...; if you study, because studying is a serious obligation.13 When the initial good disposition has been achieved, when you see that they have a true desire to better themselves, to form themselves, then that site is ready for a good building, and you can begin to dig the trench so that at the right time you can lay the foundations and then erect the structure.

The first steps in the interior life Before beginning to build you have to dig the foundations, open up the ground, prepare the youngsters so that they can assimilate the doctrine of Christ. Ignorance is a strong obstacle to grace, to the divine life within the soul: alienati a vita Dei per ignorantiam.14 And this ignorance, the ruin of the soul, usually has its origin in blindness of heart: propter caecitatem cordis ipsorum.15 But he who hears and does not do them is like a man who built a house on the ground without a foundation; against which the stream broke and immediately it fell, and the ruin of that house was great.16 It is not sufficient to hear doctrine; it is necessary to assimilate it. What serves as a foundation will not be the many words the youngsters have

12 Ibid. 13 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 14 Eph 4:18 15 Ibid. 16 Lk 6:49

A time to build 189

heard but rather what they incorporate into their own lives and makes them act accordingly. That is why the Preparatory Course, which has an eminently supernatural end, to the exclusion of any strictly learning activity, and ends up with the youngsters fitting into the Work,17 is aimed preferably at the heart; but it is also directed to the mind, because the fire which we enkindle in the youngsters would go out without the fuel of doctrine. Let us put Christ in our hearts and in the hearts of the youngsters.18 If Our Lord and with him the Blessed Trinity, occupy the soul of a young person through faith and love, we will have opened a safe trench where we can lay the solid foundation of a more developed doctrine, “because if the Spirit of God does not give interior assistance to the heart of the one who hears, the word of the one who teaches is of no avail.”19 That is why from the beginning we try to lead them to piety. We talk to them about supernatural life, about prayer: if you do not make the youngsters men of prayer, you have wasted your time.20 And we help them by means of meditations, days of recollection, eucharistic acts in our centres, spiritual direction and so on. We tell them that they have to study because it is a serious obligation, and because it is a means of sanctification, a way to reach God without any need to leave their place. We help them with our example, and with the atmosphere of work and prayer that there is in the house; by going over some subject or preparing an exam together, or by having some of the more advanced fellows clear up the more obscure points for some of the younger ones. Thus, little by little, without being forced, they enter into the ways of interior life “because those who have to be led into virtue, have to

17 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 18 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 19 St Gregory the Great, Super Evangelia homiliae 30 20 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 190 The apostolic mission

put their foot on the bottom steps and from there work their way up the rest of the staircase, and so proceed gradually until finally they reach the heights.”21 And as they progress, we instruct them in the apostolate: Get you up to a high mountain, O Zion, herald of good tidings; lift up your voice with strength, fear not; say to the cities of Judah, “Behold your God”.22 We also talk to them about a few solid, constant devotions, which do not hinder their plan of life. We talk to them about holy purity; about charity and fraternity. We talk to them about everything which leads them closer to God. “That is the way to instruct the soul, which has to be a temple of the Lord.”23

To fit them into our work As they progress in their interior life and begin their small apostolic work, we have to make sure that the youngsters make the concerns of the house and of the Work their own.24 The family atmosphere of the centre helps achieve this and so, too, does the fact that the circles are for small groups of only a few people, in this way we avoid giving the formation talk the tone of a lecture, and the circles acquire the cordial, family atmosphere which is one of the characteristics of the work of St Raphael.25 The fellows do not go to an association of friends: they come to their home. It can truly be said of each one that he will flourish in the courts of our God.26 To direct this growth there is the talk with the priest and the conversation which each youngster has with the person looking after

21 St Basil, Homiliae in Psalmos 1, 4 22 Is 40:9 23 St Jerome, Epistola 107, 4 24 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 25 Ibid. 26 Ps 92:14

A time to build 191

him, where they can tell, in the confidence of younger brothers, their little secrets and worries of all kinds. In the beginning, it is difficult for them. Afterwards, they need it. 27 We teach them. We guide And how much good it does for souls! them. We encourage them to love God, we help them make specific resolutions, going over in detail what they have been told in the talk, so that they can put into practice the doctrine they have received, today in one way, tomorrow in another. Urged on by the interior life which is enkindled in their hearts, fraternity grows among them, as does the desire to bring their friends to the centre. May the Lord make you increase, writes St Paul, and abound in love for one another and for all men.28 Fraternal charity grows, and with it God causes eager desires of apostolate and proselytism to spring up in the youngsters, and they see more clearly the need to stand out among their companions in order to be good apostles. That is why we encourage them to acquire a good professional preparation, and we take great care to establish a demanding timetable and the right atmosphere of silent and hard work. In this way they learn how to be daring, in order to deal with God with confident simplicity and with manly piety; daring, through their determination and perseverance in study, in order to obtain the maximum yield from their talent, because we cannot forgive those who are able to be scholars and are not.29 This youthful daring, together with simple obedience and naturalness, and a deep feeling of concern for others, characterises the youngsters of St Raphael. And to these virtues they add industriousness, study, and a sense of professional responsibility, with a view to sanctity

27 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 28 1 Thess 3:12 29 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 192 The apostolic mission

and the apostolate. Thus the foundations are well laid, solid and deep. And the structure that will then have to be built with the Professional Courses and with the final work of perfection is already planned. There is land, the building materials are of good quality, and the building procedures are precise and safe. Our Father could tell each one of us working and who will have to work in this great task, in the words of St Paul: According to the commission of God given to me, like a skilled master builder I laid the foundation, and another man is building upon it. Let each man take care how he builds upon it.30

Souls of sure doctrine Thy foundations shall be of sapphire; thou shalt have turrets of jasper, and gates of carved gems, and all thy boundry stones shall be jewels.31 When the boys of St Raphael have begun to have interior life, once the channel of the love of God has opened in their hearts and they have received the first doctrinal foundations, the time comes to perfect those foundations and the structure of doctrine which will sustain their spiritual edifice. They come, then, to learn. That is what they have been told right from the first moment. Their docility is active and diligent. Our fellows do not come to the St Raphael classes to waste time but to make good use of it. That is why their role is that of disciples, who go to listen to their teacher. They do not argue.32 Arguments promote speculations rather than the divine training.33 And in order not to spend the whole time clearing up things which are usually of interest only to the one who

30 1 Cor 3:10 31 Is 54:11-12 32 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 33 1 Tim 1:4

A time to build 193

asks about them, there should be a question box. The students can express their doubts in a written note showing the date and their signature, and put the note in the box.34 Thus the interest of the pupil is stimulated because his questions are always answered; imprudent and indiscreet questions are avoided; and when the question is the result of deficient formation in the youngster, he can be corrected tactfully and alone. Afterwards, they are grateful for it, and they follow the classes with even more docility. Besides, one can get to know them better, because their questions usually relate to a personal problem, which provides an opportunity to go deeper in that friendly confidence and in the work of their formation. The St Raphael fellows come to build, and only by good doctrine, 35 from its foundations. Doctrine is the only by wisdom a house is built base on which our interior life rests: build yourselves up on your most holy faith.36 A life of piety and apostolate thus established is capable of resisting all adversities, “and the reason is that it is built on solid rock. And solid rock is the firmness of its doctrine.”37 The function of the Professional Courses is to finish laying these foundations and building this structure. The Courses provide this living doctrine. You will give them criteria on current issues and on topics related to their profession; and doctrine of the Church which will lead to desires to act as responsible Christians in the society in which they live and work. It is not a question of giving them a smattering of culture, or of expounding theories, opinions and subtleties. We want to form men of sure doctrine,38 pious, apostolic men. That is why the Courses are not improvised, nor are they left to the discretion of each

34 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 35 Prov 24:3 36 Jude 20 37 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae, 24, 2 38 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 194 The apostolic mission

one, but rather the themes which are of greatest interest bearing in mind the particular circumstances of the pupils, are brought together in an organic plan. And the classes are well prepared, by studying the theme well in order to be able to expound it profoundly and in a simple way, with the gift of tongues. Talk to them clearly and without getting over-excited. Do not exaggerate, I insist, but try to be very objective. Thus you will have indisputable authority. I usually take special pains over this and I tell them, especially when I make affirmations about the supernatural life: I cannot say anything different from what I am saying, even if I wanted to. Because if this is a table – referring to the table of the class – how am I going to say that it is a grand piano? It is a table, and it would be impossible for any man in his right mind to tell you that it is a piano. And with this and other perhaps not very academic comparisons, how 39 much the Faith of my boys is strengthened! In this way errors are dispelled and disappear, and the fellows are confirmed in the truth. See – says God – I have set you this day over nations and over kingdoms, to pluck up and to break down, to destroy, and to overthrow, to build and to plant.40 That is what we are aiming for: to raise up, to build interior life and apostolic life, with the foundation of doctrine. Without doctrine, piety is inconsistent; nor can our works be filled with God. Those who lack the necessary knowledge will only have the form of religion but deny the power of it.41 Once more the cry of the Apostle resounds in our ears: Let the word of Christ dwell in you richly, as you teach and admonish one another in all wisdom.42

39 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 40 Jer 1:10 41 2 Tim 3:5 42 Col 3:16

A time to build 195

Spiritual formation Teaching and encouraging are two things which cannot be separated. The foundation and the body of doctrine, the word of life, have to be rounded off with an atmosphere of piety. Neither piety without doctrine, nor doctrine without piety, for knowledge only breeds selfconceit, it is charity that binds the building together.43 Thus, we have to strive, as the Apostle said, to have Christ dwell in your hearts through faith, so that you be rooted and grounded in love.44 “For just as the tree without roots and the house without a foundation easily fall down, so if the spiritual edifice is not founded on charity it cannot last.”45 That is why around the Courses, right from the start, as a complementary task, weekly meditations are given, and there are monthly days of recollection, with a Eucharistic act whose purpose is to foster piety and devotion to Jesus in the Most Holy Sacrament. And all this helps our dealings with the youngsters as well. Thus they become more closely linked to the Work, and we can get a more complete knowledge of them, because once any act is over the fellows naturally spread out through the whole house – through their house – and it is a wonderful opportunity to study them, to get to know them better and to do them a lot of good,46 following closely their interior growth, helping them to put into practice the things they hear. For our Gospel, we can assure them, came to you not only in word, but also in power and in the Holy Spirit with full conviction.47

43 1 Cor 8:1 44 Eph 3:17 45 St Thomas, Super Epistolam ad Ephes. lectura 4, 172 46 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 47 1 Thess 1:5 196 The apostolic mission
##
Work that is never finished Zelus domus tuae comedit me,48 zeal for the house of the Lord has to consume us. “And who has zeal for the house of God? He who struggles to correct all the evils that he sees there. He who has this desire and does not rest until he accomplishes it ... For example,” St Augustine comments, “do you see your brother in danger? Stop him, warn him, feel for him in your heart, if you are consumed by zeal for the house of God. With your affection, attract as many as you can, and do not ever tire of doing so ... I am going to give you some advice, rather, let the one who is within you give it, because even though he gives it to you through me, he is the one who gives it: ... if God comes to you and opens the door with his word, do not rest until you win over others for Christ, now that you yourselves have been won over by Christ.”49 Jesus has shown, and our brothers have shown, such loving patience towards us! They have not denied us their words, their advice, their correction, their encouragement. And we should act likewise with the St Raphael fellows, the hope of the Work.50 Whereas it was in your heart, God says, to build a house for my name, you did well that it was in your heart.51 Let it not be said: this man began to build, and was not able to finish.52 Through constancy we shall finish, we shall crown this work, teaching the fellows love of God and the doctrine of God, all at the right moment. We shall go through the Courses of Formation step by step, with neither haste nor delay, and with prudence; for by wisdom a house is built, and by understanding it is established.53

48 Ps 69:9 49 St Augustine, In Ioannis Evangelium tractatus 10, 9 50 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 51 1 Kg 8:18 52 Lk 14:30 53 Prov 24:3

A time to build 197

Each thing at its proper time: preparing the foundations, positioning the pillars, raising up the structure. Thus, the results will not be long in coming. Our St Raphael fellows will grow interiorly; they will build up their life of piety with a spirit of uprightness and of loyalty, of charity, of energy, of sacrifice, of simple joy and of supernatural confidence.54 Human and supernatural virtues, land which is made ready so that the divine vocation of a dedication to God in the Work can spring up. This will be the best possible final touch to the building. And to obtain this “ease the way”, without brusqueness. In this period of transition, “be prudent” in imposing and even in manifesting the obligations which our people have.55 But do not carry “prudence” to the extreme of hiding that “something transcendental” which, as a son of mine said a little worriedly, there is in the atmosphere of the house.56 And those who do not receive the calling from Our Lord to a complete dedication, will be able to form part of the work of St Gabriel, which will continue the work begun, with similar means of formation and spiritual help. There, many of these fellows will later find their own vocation. Others, good Christian professional people, will feel the need for corresponding to those benefits which they have received, and we shall count on their help as Co-operators. All this work, following the norms pointed out by our Father, is a supernatural task, a work with souls. It has to be preceded, accompanied and followed up by the support of prayer. That was the way St Paul did it: I commend you to God and to the word of his grace, which is able to build you up and to give you the inheritance among all those who are sanctified.57 Then the voice of God will perhaps make itself heard in the depths of the soul: I have heard your prayer and your

54 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 55 Ibid. 56 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 57 Acts 20:32 198 The apostolic mission

supplication, which you have made before me; I have consecrated this house which you have built, and put my name there forever; my eyes and my heart will be there for all time.58 Work and pray tenaciously. That is the way. And be very close to Our Lady. It is by woman’s wisdom a home thrives.59 And what woman is there like Mary, the very seat of divine Wisdom, domus aurea – house of gold, temple and tabernacle of the Most Holy Trinity!

58 1 Kg 9:3 59 Prov 14:1

## THE CATECHISM CLASSES {#cuaderno.5.19}

All our apostolates can be reduced to only one: the apostolate of giving doctrine.1 This is the work’s mission that we are all engaged in carrying out, for the greatest obstacle to the growth of the Kingdom of God on earth is lack of due doctrine. Only ignorance can allow a man to commit crimes and not be aware of it. We must give doctrine.2 The work of St Rafael plays an important role in this task by picking out young people of all races, countries and social conditions, and giving them a deep Christian formation. Through the work of St Raphael these youngsters are given first of all doctrine; afterwards or at the same time, they are taught to use the traditional means in order to win in the ascetical struggle. In short, they are given interior life. It is this same interior life that makes them apostolic and inspires them to take the supernatural warmth of the doctrine of Christ into their own environment, their work, and the fulfilment of all their duties. They do this by example, with the integrity of their Christian conduct as exemplary members of their families, as good professional people and good citizens, and also by their words. In carrying out this formative task of St Raphael’s work we count on a great variety of apostolic means and activities. Some of these means are already traditional and have been effective for the good of souls from the beginnings of our Work: the Courses of Formation, the Catechism classes and the visits to the poor of Our Lady, the

1 From our Father 2 From our Father, Crónica, June 1955, p.12 200 The apostolic mission

meditations, the spiritual retreats and in general the liturgical acts of piety celebrated in our halls of residence. Our Father writes, These means are perennial and must be used always and everywhere when we are doing the work of St Raphael. For they are characteristic of this apostolate and give life to all the other activities which take place around this work of St Raphael.3

Auxiliary apostolates The Courses of Formation are the essential element in the work of St Raphael.4 We must put special effort into looking after them, setting ourselves ambitious and specific targets when we plan the work of St Raphael each year. But an abundant harvest is not normally the result of sowing and of rain alone. In order to get the most out of his land the farmer ploughs it and also has the earth analysed. He chooses the most suitable seed, and increases the yield of the soil by using fertilisers. He waters the land in time of drought. In short, he spares no effort to obtain the golden ears of grain. Apart from the essential means our Founder has also foreseen for the Work of St Raphael the use of, the auxiliary apostolates. Some are universal and perennial, while others depend on circumstances. They are all those activities that help us get to know the youngsters and in many cases create opportunities for the work of spiritual formation. There are so many activities of all kinds, so many external undertakings that should be used. They are like nets for the fishing – a divine fishing – of the souls of those who are involved in the work of St Raphael! Among these external undertakings, of course, two of them are obligatory: the catechism classes and the visits to the poor. Then there are sporting activities, and those to do with science,

3 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 4 Ibid.

The catechism classes 201

literature, the cinema or whatever you like! There are trips and a hundred thousand other things. All, however, have a deep apostolic inspiration, otherwise they are useless.5 These innumerable and widely varied activities will change according to circumstances of time, country or interests. But the Courses of formation together with the other specific means of the work of St Raphael will not change. They are the foundation and framework of the whole building.

The work of giving Catechism My dearest daughters and sons, writes our Father, you must have great confidence in the supernatural effectiveness of your apostolic work. If you use the traditional means faithfully all will go well. On occasions there may be difficulties of one kind or another, but they will always be overcome. They are routine affairs that you will surmount by means of your sacrifice, prayer and cheerfulness.6 Giving catechism classes is a particularly good means for preparation of future vocations. For apart from the work of apostolate with souls in need of doctrine, it is at times a means of selection from among the youngsters. It is a cause of progress in doctrinal formation on account of the study of the catechism and the apologetic preparation that are required. It fosters charity and generosity in their ascetical formation. Moreover, because the youngsters want to bring their friends to the catechism classes it is an opportunity to introduce them to the work of proselytism. Everyone who calls upon the name of the Lord will be saved. But how are men to call upon him in whom they have not believed? And how are they to believe in him of whom they have never heard? And 7 The catechetical tradition in how are they to hear without a preacher?

5 From our Father, Meditation, 5 March 1963 6 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 7 Rom 10:13-14 202 The apostolic mission

the Church is ancient and deep-rooted. Preaching the doctrine of Christ so that all could be saved was an urgent task, and so the catechumenate was established in the early Church with a view to candidates for baptism knowing “what the faith is and what the love of a Christian ought to be.”8 When the catechumenate disappeared and the baptism of infants was established, the Church took care to instruct children in the faith when they began to have the use of reason. Doctrine must be given always and constantly. Catechism classes are a simple and effective way of giving it. In our times, faced with the contrast between a wealth of scientific and cultural knowledge and an almost absolute ignorance of the things of God, the Roman Pontiffs have been insistent in their recommendation: “we ought, therefore, to do all that lies in our power to maintain the teaching of Christian doctrine with full vigour, and where such is neglected, to restore it; for ... there is nothing more effective than catechetical instruction to spread the glory of God and to secure the salvation of souls.”9 The aim of Catechism classes is to give doctrine and to lead to the faith, while at the same time increasing charity, since he who loves is born of God and knows God. He who does not love does not know God; for God is love.10 As the end of the whole process we teach how to put into practice the teachings of Jesus Christ: by this we may be sure that we know him, if we keep his commandments.11 “The secret of Christian life is in the catechism. It contains everything that God wants us to know and to do in life.”12 It begins its teaching by explaining the truths of the Creed, because to love God and to serve Him we have to know Him and realise how many motives

8 St Augustine, De fide et operibus 6, 9 9 St Pius X, Encyclical Acerbo nimis, 15 April 1905 10 1 Jn 4:7-8 11 1 Jn 2:3 12 Pius XI, Address, 4 March 1928

The catechism classes 203

there are for corresponding. Then, since love is shown in deeds, the commandments of the Law of God are explained. As it is impossible for man to do anything without God’s help, we are taught to pray and to have recourse to the sacraments of the Church which give us the supernatural strength to overcome difficulties. The catechism is the “summa veritatis et caritatis: it is the ‘manuductio’” – that which takes us by the hand – “in the present and in the future.”13
##
The Catechism classes in St Raphael’s work In the work of St Raphael the Catechism classes are a traditional undertaking which can never be neglected wherever we are working. From the start it has been our practice to choose the poorest parishes because they are often the ones that most need this work, and it is easier in this way to foster the generosity and charity of the catechists. What wonderful horizons are opened up in the souls of those fellows who are prepared to give time to catechesis! How enthusiastically they take on the responsibility and find ways of involving their friends! Under the guidance of a member of the Work the weight of organising and giving the classes falls on them. By helping them, guiding them, clearing up their doubts, we make sure that the catechists prepare their classes well from the beginning, since no one “will ever be able to teach Christian doctrine to children or to adults without first giving himself to very careful study and preparation.”14 We also instil in the souls of the youngsters the need to have recourse to God in order to prepare the classes, because et in meditatione mea exardescit ignis. And in my meditation a fire shall flame out.15 This is the fire of charity that must be passed on to the children.

13 St John XXIII, Homily 8 December 1958 14 St Pius X, Encyclical Acerbo nimis, 15 April 1905 15 The Way, 92 204 The apostolic mission

This is the objective of the classes: “The Love of God is like the goal towards which you should direct all things,” says St Augustine. “Whatever you say, say it in such a way that the person you are speaking to, on hearing will believe, on believing will hope, and on hoping will love.”16 In order to improve their doctrinal formation we encourage the youngsters to buy books on apologetics and the teaching of catechism, and to leave them in the library of the centre so that those who follow them in this task may use them. Those who are not sufficiently well prepared can always help out, for example, by teaching the smaller children to learn prayers off by heart. The way in which the catechism classes are organised has always depended on the specific circumstances of each place. But once the time and day have been fixed we must insist that the catechists turn up regularly and punctually. They usually understand immediately that a great part of the success of the classes depends on this. We ask them to put great interest into looking after the group of children that has been entrusted to them, following the example of St Paul: I will most gladly spend and be spent for your souls.17 We encourage them to show affection, to be patient, and to be cheerful always, since example is the best way of preaching. We help them to acquire the gift of tongues and adapt themselves to the mentality of the children, striving like the Apostles “to put every care into preaching simple and accessible things to the ignorant.”18 Finally, we show them the need to maintain the interest of the children, for “if the food for the body taken without the appetite or by a person who is already full, not only does no good, but can do great harm, with greater reason the bread of the soul if taken with repugnance is not nourishment but torment for the conscience.”19

16 St Augustine, De catechizandis rudibus 4, 8 17 2 Cor 12:15 18 St Gregory the Great, Moralia 17, 26 19 St Bernard, In Cantico Canticorum sermo 35, 9

The catechism classes 205

Abundant fruit He transplanted it to good soil by abundant waters, so that it might bring forth branches, and bear fruit, and become a noble vine.20 The catechism classes are fruitful for the catechists themselves. They study Christian Doctrine and practise the Christian virtues, especially charity. They show concern for others, generosity, responsibility, patience, and so forth. They are also involved in the traditional Christian task of teaching the truths of faith, and experience the joy of seeing the seed they have sown grow in the souls of the children, the hope of the Church. Furthermore, when they try to attract their friends to the work of catechesis the young people begin to do proselytism by introducing the best of them to the work of St Raphael. So neither he who plants nor he who waters is anything, but only God who gives the growth.21 The fruits are not ours but God’s. He is the owner of the field and he waters it with abundant graces. Nor have we determined the means to be used to till the land and sow the seed. Our Founder received that task from God and has handed it on to us in all its fullness. We are instruments. We are labourers without whom, because the Lord of the field has wanted it this way, there would be no ripe fruit. The outcome of the sowing, an abundant harvest, depends on our cheerful, self-denying, and constant work, in which we follow the indications we have received from our Father. Only in this way will the seed you are to sow in many nations produce abundant fruit; and you will be astonished to the depths of your souls, because the fruit will be out of all proportion to the means we employ, which are very little by comparison. 22

20 Ez 17:8 21 2 Cor 3:7 22 Alone with God, 278

## OUR LADY’S POOR {#cuaderno.5.20}

Opus Dei was born among the poor of Madrid, in the hospitals and in the poorest neighbourhoods, and we carry on caring for the poor, the children and the sick.1 From the beginning of the Work the sick and the poor have been especially dear to our Father’s heart. A sign of this special affection is reflected in the wonderful tradition of visiting the poor which we practise in honour of Our Lady. Our Founder once told us the origin of this Custom which we will always practise with courtesy and affection. He wrote: This refinement of charity began very early on, with the first steps of the Work. Declina pauperi sine tristitia aurem tuam ..., et responde illi pacifica in mansuetudine (Sir 4:8). Listen carefully to the poor man ..., and always speak to him with mildness and words of peace.2 These visits were born at the very beginning, when there were no material means of any kind. It was the time “when we did not even have a residence and our Father gathered the fellows together in the Grandmother’s house, or took them to the Sotanillo, a quiet bar on the street of Alcalá very near the Plaza de la Independencia.”3 Our Father also told us what those early visits were like. When we went we put a lot of human and supernatural affection into them and we began to call the people we were going to visit “Our Lady’s poor”.

1 From our Father, Instruction, 8 December 1941 2 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 3 Don Alvaro, note 135 to the Instruction, 9 January 1935

Our Lady’s poor 207

A fellow who had no apostolic concern would loathe going and would not go. In this way we were already making a selection. The addresses were given us by the parish priests in the outskirts. We went no more than once to each house. We took them some money, something amusing to read, and some sweets that only the rich could afford to eat. We always left a packet of something that perhaps they had never seen in their lives. It was not a question of doing a continuous work with them but rather with the fellows who made the visits.4 Our Father had already written this in 1935 when he pointed out that these visits, like the other auxiliary apostolates of the work of St Raphael, are not an end in themselves, but a means of acquiring formation.5

To honour Our Lady These visits to Our Lady’s poor have a deeply human and charitable meaning. We want to bring a bit of joy and affection to people who often have never heard a kind word, and neither felt the gaze of friendly eyes nor the fraternal gesture of Christian aid. Certain impoverished forms of charitable work have been so misrepresented and satirised that specific acts proper to the spirit of Christianity seem archaic to some. I want you, therefore, to understand very well, and to make others understand, the deep supernatural and human significance of these means such as we have practised them from the beginning.6 In the first place, with these simple visits we are not going to solve any social problem,7 because this is not their purpose. Of course, each of us, individually or together with others, can make his own free choices and put into practice any means he considers appropriate to

4 From our Father, Get-together, 12 November 1960 5 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 6 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 7 Ibid.

208 The apostolic mission

help solve this or any other problem. Explain it in this way to the fellows, our Father wrote. It is a matter of taking a small gift he would not normally receive to comfort a poor person, a sick person, or someone who is lonely. We thus bring a bit of enjoyment into his life and perhaps do some little favour for him, and nothing more. They will understand it right away if they are acquiring interior life, and if they know that we do this also to honour Our Lady.8 If it is not a question of trying to solve a social problem, nor are we with these visits trying to awaken superficial social concerns. As I have said, it is a matter of bringing these young people to their neighbour in need. Our St Raphael fellows come in a practical way to see Jesus Christ in the poor, the sick, or the invalid, in the one who is lonely, in the one who suffers, in the child.9 The visits to Our Lady’s poor show our friends in a really practical way that “the Church encompasses with her love all those who are afflicted by human misery and she recognises in those who are poor and who suffer, the image of her poor and suffering Founder. She does all in her power to relieve their need and in them she strives to serve Christ.”10 Our friends, and we also, learn to recognise and to love the human and divine figure of Christ in the poor and the sick. For Christ said, I was hungry and you gave me food; I was thirsty and you gave me drink; I was a stranger and you welcomed me; I was naked and you clothed me; I was sick and you visited me; I was in prison and you came to me ... Truly I say to you, as long as you did it to one of the least of these, my brethren, you did it to me.11 It is a great work of charity and justice to strive for the elimination of poverty, illiteracy and ignorance, but there will always

8 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 9 Ibid. 10 Second Vatican Council, Dogmatic Constitution Lumen gentium, 8 11 Mt 25:35-40

Our Lady’s poor 209

be a need for charity because justice in this world will never completely achieve this happy outcome for men. Besides, there will always be those who suffer the poverty of loneliness or lack of understanding.12 That is why in the work of St Raphael we have to teach them to fight a great battle against misery, against ignorance, against sickness and against suffering.13 Direct contact with these realities, which the fellows are sometimes unaware of, cannot but help our friends. I repeat that this is why the visits to the poor of Our Lady are especially formative. In this way they learn to experience a living and practical fraternal charity. On seeing others who are materially or spiritually in need they will thank God for the benefits they have received from him.14 Nor is it unusual for these youngsters to reflect and draw out consequences for their own interior life when they encounter faith and hope in God in many of these families so afflicted by misery or illness. At the same time that this Custom, which is a work of mercy blessed by Jesus Christ,15 is contributing to their formation, it also brings to mind for them the life of the early Christians. In the first centuries of the Church, caring for widows, comforting the afflicted and alleviating the needs of the poor were clear signs of having received the Gospel in its entirety.16 It is not right that actions born of a true Christian spirit should be shelved merely because some have converted them into an ostentatious and empty gesture, or a sedative for their own remorse of conscience.17 Our friends will obtain many other benefits if we know how to teach them this Custom as our Father has taught it to us. They will learn

12 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 13 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 14 Ibid. 15 Ibid. 16 Cf 1 Tim 5:10; Acts 24:17; 2 Cor 8:1, ff 17 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 210 The apostolic mission

to love the Blessed Virgin more, for we do this work of mercy in her honour. She is a mother, Mother of God and our Mother, and she knows what some young hearts are trying to say with these tiny acts of love for their needy brothers.18 Besides, with these visits to the poor it will be easier to make a first selection which is indispensable so that the fellows acquire an intimate closeness to the warmth of the Work. If a fellow does not have a true concern for others, neither will he have the necessary conditions to form part of the work of St Raphael, still less to receive the vocation to Opus Dei. This contact with misery or human weakness is an occasion that Our Lord usually employs to enkindle untold desires of generosity and of divine adventures in a soul. At the same time it gives the younger ones a greater awareness so that they always have a deep concern for justice and charity.19

With everybody’s help Our Father has taught us the specific way to practise this Custom. In 1935 he wrote, Every Saturday, and on the 19th of each month in honour of St Joseph, we will make a discreet collection for the poor of Our Lady. One or other of the boys passes around the bag and the alms are given to the person in charge of the charitable funds. There should also be a box for this in the Oratory.20 But even though a part of the alms collected is used for the visits to the poor, our Father, “has pointed out on many occasions that these collections have more than just one purpose. In the first place they must contribute to the support of the house, and then to this refined form of charity with the poor.”21 When the fellows begin to come regularly to one of our centres it is

18 Ibid. 19 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 20 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 21 Don Alvaro, note 153 to the Instruction, 9 January 1935

Our Lady’s poor 211

natural for us to explain to them the reason for this collection, and we can even give them the job of passing the bag around sometimes. They will thus feel more at one with the work of the centre and will more easily have affection for the Work. It is also logical when they first come round to explain to them the purpose of the box placed in a discreet and visible place in the Oratory or, in larger centres, in some other accessible place outside the Oratory. Then, in an appropriate way, we should try to stimulate their generosity. Once enough money has been collected we can do a visit to the poor. If there is not enough, it is better to wait. In any case, our Father says that we must not allow the youngsters to give money directly out of their pockets for the poor of Our Lady.22 They must get used to passing unnoticed when they give, without anyone knowing the measure of their generosity which only God should see. “He who can give a lot will do so if he is generous, and the one who cannot give anything will not feel humiliated because the collection is not public. In any case he is certain to give us instead the best of all alms, which is his prayer.”23 In this way our youngsters will learn to be humble and always to act with the right intention. That is why the Father adds: it is better for them to put their alms anonymously into the box in the Oratory or the collections on Saturdays and on the nineteenth of each month.24 Since the money that is spent on these visits or on other similar works of mercy is not theirs but the result of everyone’s contribution, we cannot allow them to pay out of their own money on the train or underground or whatever means of transport is used to go to the catechism classes,25 or to the neighbourhood where the poor family lives that we want to visit. On the day fixed beforehand, preferably on the feasts of the Blessed

22 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 23 Don Alvaro, note 153 to the Instruction, 9 January 1935 24 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 25 Ibid.

212 The apostolic mission

Virgin, two of the St Raphael fellows go to visit a particular poor person.26 It is helpful to know the route you are going to follow beforehand. On the way it is easy to direct the conversation along apostolic lines. The one who is already a St Raphael fellow (or a member of the Work if he is making the visit) then has the opportunity to get to know the friend he is going with more deeply and to judge better whether he has the conditions necessary to take part in the ordinary means of formation proper to the work of St Raphael. To make these visits easier it is always useful to have in the Centre a list of addresses of needy persons. Normally it is easy to get them through the St Vincent de Paul Societies,27 or through the parish priests in the more needy parts of town. “Usually we have chosen the parishes in the poorest districts, both for catechism and for the visits to the poor of Our Lady.”28 We have also been taught that it is best not to go too often to the same family. Without our intending it, the family might come to expect our visits and might get the wrong idea about the purpose of this means of formation, expecting us to solve some of their problems for them.

A tradition that must always be kept up Our Father and our older brothers have shown us in specific ways the tact we have to show in each of these visits. It is not a matter of proving our compassion for the needy since that would give us a paternalistic air which is not proper to the spirit of the Work. On the contrary, these are visits full of warmth, in which we listen to people with affection, bringing them kind words that are Christian and fraternal, with a little something to enjoy that they would not normally

26 Ibid. 27 Cf From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 28 Don Alvaro, note 133 to the Instruction, 9 January 1935

Our Lady’s poor 213

have.29 Hence, following the advice of our Founder, we avoid their feeling embarrassed or offended. We cheer the poor person up, and give him some small alms of money, something good to read, and some sweets or something that people who are better off eat. Some people will not understand the latter, but we have always done it this way right from the beginning and Our Lady is pleased by it. To me it seems a refinement of charity.30 We cannot forget that often they need a bit of affectionate conversation more than just material help. Hence, when they notice that we do not put on airs, that we do not intend to do a dry and mechanical work of official charity, when they realise that our interest for their situation and family is genuine, they usually feel very much understood and happy. Some small practical favour can help them see more clearly our right intention. In the early years our Father “had the youngsters accompany him to visit the poor in the most densely populated and poorest areas of the city, and to care for the sick in the hospitals. They used to wash their hands and feet, cut their nails, clean the bedpans, and take care of them as best they could, while at the same time consoling them with their cheerful conversation.”31 Perhaps today it is not appropriate to do these types of service, but the spirit is the same. It is a question of practising Christian charity in a truly fraternal way. “Today this work has so many different outlets. Besides the visits to the poor of Our Lady, there are, for example, the out-patient clinics set up by members of both sections for people who lack financial means. There are also training schools for men and women workers, set up and developed in the poorest areas of large cities.”32 Visiting the poor of Our Lady is a tradition that will never cease in
##
29 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 30 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 31 Don Alvaro, note 135 to the Instruction, 9 January 1935 32 Ibid.

214 The apostolic mission

the Work.33 Remember that, whatever the circumstances of the country, we will always be able to practise this affectionate charity: pauperes enim semper habetis vobiscum (John 12:8). There will always be poor people, there will always be some people who are more needy, even if the majority of the people have a minimum of material well-being. They will be happy to receive a small gift out of the ordinary, something that they cannot normally afford and which comes as something a bit special for them, whereby they experience a bit of extra attention and fraternal companionship.34 Even in the most highlydeveloped countries there is always a certain section of society which suffers some need. I would venture to say that when social circumstances seem to have cleared the environment of misery, poverty and suffering, precisely then it is more urgent to live this refined form of Christian charity which is able to recognise where there is need of consolation in the midst of apparent general well-being.35 It is certainly true in the modern world that the State, through its welfare institutions, etc., shows concern to alleviate basic needs and encourage social progress. But the general presence of social remedies to combat the plagues of suffering and need, which makes it possible today to achieve humanitarian conditions not even dreamt of in times gone by, can never supplant the true affection, both human and supernatural, found in direct, personal contact with our neighbour, because welfare solutions are on an altogether different plane. Our neighbour is that poor person in a nearby area, that other sick person having to bear with his suffering in some huge hospital, or that other person, perhaps wealthy, who needs a few minutes of affectionate conversation, a Christian friendship to ease his loneliness and a

33 From our Father, Instruction, 8 December 1941 34 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 35 Ibid.

Our Lady’s poor 215

spiritual refuge that will solve his doubts and his scepticism.36 At times, our Father also said, in the work of St Raphael the visits to the poor are not made to those who are in desperate poverty, because it is not always easy to find them. Instead, we go to those poor people whose need is affection and the warmth of good human friendship.37 The experience of these years has shown how this tradition “has not only not been interrupted but, thanks be to God, has been enriched with new facets and new charitable tasks. For example, we go to visit those who are not poor from a material standpoint, but who are isolated or alone, and we help them spiritually. We visit them to bring them a bit of friendship, a bit of human warmth so that, in the words of our Father, they do not feel the bitterness of indifference.”38 That is why we will always make these visits, even though there are people who do not understand them or criticise them. Perhaps this will not be understood in places where a materialistic outlook prevails. That is why I said before that to understand requires a minimum of interior life, of Christian outlook, of love of God and neighbour.39 If we know how to practise faithfully this tradition our Founder has taught us, we will honour Our Lady, help many people to feel the warmth of the charity of Jesus Christ, and form the youngsters who approach our apostolate by arousing their generosity and awakening noble decisions in their souls. Using these traditional means which must never be lacking,40 and which are indispensable in the previous selection for the work of St Raphael, we will effectively prepare the fertile and well-disposed soil in which one day, with the grace of God, vocations to the Work can take root.

36 Ibid. 37 From our Father 38 Don Alvaro, note 186 to the Instruction, 8 December 1941 39 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 40 Ibid.

## THE LETTER-WRITING APOSTOLATE {#cuaderno.5.21}

“The Church was founded to spread the kingdom of Christ over all the earth for the glory of God the Father, to make all men partakers in redemption and salvation,”1 as accomplished by Christ on Calvary. All of us Christians, the Mystical Body of Christ, have been entrusted with the “noble obligation of working to bring all men throughout the whole world to hear and accept the divine message of salvation.”2 No Christian is exempt from this apostolic mission which is necessary for the number of the elect to reach its fullness and for the Kingdom of God to be established definitively on earth. God himself demands our cooperation. As St Paul said, If I preach the gospel, that gives me no ground for boasting. For necessity is laid upon me. Woe to me if I do 3 not preach the gospel! The apostolic mission belongs to all Christians, but this obligation takes on a greater urgency for us who are dedicated to the apostolate by divine vocation. God has chosen us to unite all things in Christ, things in heaven and things on earth.4 My children, this is what we have been called for. This must be our task in the apostolate, a task which, with our own spirituality and our special asceticism, fits beautifully into

1 Second Vatican Council, Decree Apostolicam actuositatem, 2 2 Ibid., 3 3 1 Cor 9:16 4 Eph 1:10

The letter-writing apostolate 217

the one and only mission of Christ and his Church.5 Our apostolate is one of friendship and confidence.6 We live among the rest of the human race, united by common interests and concerned with the same problems. In a word, we share the life of all the people around us and in this environment we do our apostolate. Our Father has written: In this sharing of the life of other people you will find the opportunity to bring souls to Christ Jesus, and it is only reasonable for you not to refuse it. Moreover, you need to seek and develop the opportunity because you are apostles, with an apostolate of friendship and confidence, and you should not enclose yourselves behind walls that cut you off from your companions: neither physically, because we are not religious; nor spiritually, because your noble and sincere relationship with everybody is the human means for your work with souls.7 This is why we strive to permeate with the warmth of Christ all the relationships that unite friends to each other: work, interests, social relations, economic and political relations ... everything.

Letters from friends Friendship is the human basis for apostolate. Everything, therefore, that helps to preserve friendship and make it stronger is also a requirement of the apostolate. Friendship flows in many channels and shows itself in many ways, taking various forms. Among these many forms, however, varying with the circumstances, there are some we could call universal because they suit any situation. One of these is the written word by which we may keep up a correspondence with people far away, staying in touch with what they are doing and with the

5 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940 6 From our Father, Letter 16 July 1933 7 Ibid.

218 The apostolic mission

circumstances in which they live: letter-writing. People who live apart usually keep in touch and stay united in this way. This is what we should do, raising it to the supernatural order and converting it into a means of apostolate, because it would not suit our way of acting to do things that appear strange. In this simple practice of writing letters, something that everyone does, we find an opportunity for apostolate. In fact, in itself it constitutes a specific form of apostolate which has been part of our life in the Work from the beginning: the letter-writing apostolate which our Father practised and taught the first members of the Work to carry out with the St Raphael people, their parents, their relatives and acquaintances. If writing letters is a way of doing apostolate, of maintaining friendships and strengthening them, it stands to reason that we should write. It does not matter whether or not we have literary skill, an enthusiasm for writing, or plenty of time. It is rather a question of whether or not we have, here and there, friends who need a few words of affection or encouragement. Who is not glad to get a letter that tells him he is remembered, offering him best wishes on his birthday or anniversary or telling him some interesting news? Writing is a question of friendship, of interest, and of apostolic drive. We must not let a friendship die when we leave town or our friend does. In fact, very often it is just this separation that strengthens the friendship because it highlights it. Sometimes it confirms the friendship, for he who keeps in touch shows that his friendship is stronger than distance and separation. At other times it really shows that there was more to it than just routine spending of time together at work or in living close to each other. There are further occasions when it helps to emphasise the disinterested nature of a relationship which continues without exchange of favours as its foundation and without any other reason for keeping in touch than mutual esteem. How easy it is to The letter-writing apostolate 219

renew a friendship when we have not allowed time or distance to break it! It is only natural and reasonable to renew it, since distance has created an expectancy that demands greater personal rapport, an even deeper friendship than before. On the other hand, if there had not been this expectancy, getting together again would seem artificial, imposed certainly by motives other than real friendship.

An apostolic intention Letters provide the only tangible relations with one who is absent and the only means to keep friendship from dying through lack of personal contact. The relationship, in fact, carries on, adapted to new circumstances. Hence letters must have characteristics similar to those of the original friendship. For one whose heart is filled with God letters are just a simple way of reflecting the divine life he wants to share with others. To do otherwise would even be artificial and a sign that the friendship is not Christian. If this were so, for a Christian it would not even be real friendship. Our letters should never be without an apostolic intention, a supernatural reference. Often the grace of God is waiting for the most trivial of circumstances such as a word, a gesture, or a few lines, in order to move a soul, free it from some danger, or make it go forward. Our Founder has left us a few lines that show just this happening in practice: As a heading to my letter I had written, as always, ‘May Jesus watch over you’. And he replies : ‘The “May Jesus watch over you” of your letter has already helped me out of more than one tight corner. May he also watch over all of you.’ 8 Our letters must be supernatural, but at the same time they must be

8 The Way, 312 220 The apostolic mission

simple. We speak and write in the same way as any other ordinary person, using expressions proper to our personal human upbringing. We try to speak with the gift of tongues, in the way best suiting the situation of the person to whom we are writing and his particular needs. A brief suggestion or a bit of practical advice can do more than many pages filled with arguments and reflections. What is most important is that the letters should spring from affection, that they should be sincere, intimate, personal, and genuine. See with what large letters I am writing to you with my own hand!,9 St Paul said to the Galatians in order to give practical proof of his affection for them. From the very beginning, Christian tradition has always known how to make use of the letter-writing apostolate. St John, already well on in years, writes to Gaius, a disciple who had remained faithful when many had fled, and puts in a few simple lines the love that is brimming in his heart: I had much to write to you, but I would rather not write with pen and ink; I hope to see you soon, and we will talk together face to face.10 Our Father has often told us how he carried on this letter-writing apostolate from the beginning of the Work. During the Civil War in Spain, when the St Raphael people were scattered over different fronts, our Father would write them a few lines full of affection whenever he could. He even travelled right up to the front line, by very uncomfortable means of transport, in order to bring them in person some words of human and supernatural encouragement. In 1938 he wrote to one of these young people: I heard you were ill and I am hurrying to write to you, now that I have just found out where you are.11 They were simple letters, full of fatherly love and concern. I am

9 Gal 6:11 10 3 Jn 13-14 11 From our Father The letter-writing apostolate 221

anxious to hear news of you from yourself. Write to me and then I will write back at more length.12 He was always anxious to re-establish the contact with other fellows that the war had broken. Let us have any addresses of our friends that you may know of.13 You praised the ‘letter-writing apostolate’ very highly when you wrote: ‘I just cannot manage to fill the pages with stuff likely to help the friend I am writing to. When I begin, I tell my guardian Angel that all I want is that my letter may do some good. And even if I only write nonsense, no one can deprive me – or my friend – of these moments spent praying for what I know he needs most’.14 There are many people with whom we can do this apostolate: relatives, friends, acquaintances. But above all we have a special obligation towards our parents and those with whom we have begun to do an apostolate that demands our communication and our help so that their interior life may not wither, but grow and give the fruit it is meant to give. The commandment to love our parents is a commandment of natural and of divine law, and we call it the most sweet precept. You have to treat them very well ... without losing your freedom. With a bit of “cunning”, you can make them very happy and bring them to love the Work, while at the same time you enjoy complete freedom to serve God.15 When our parents live a long way from us, almost the only way, but a very effective one, of drawing them closer to the Work is to write to them often and regularly. We must be shrewd enough to know how to let them share our joyful moments while passing over little matters that could worry them. We must try to keep them informed of what we are doing, and thus help make our vocation one they can love. They will see that our vocation makes us always remember them, that it

12 From our Father 13 From our Father 14 The Way, 976 15 From our Father, Crónica, July 1960, p.12 222 The apostolic mission

makes us happy and grateful. They should know that we love them. How can we be doing something pleasing to God, if we desert the souls of those who have loved us so much on earth and who have contributed 16 Our Father so much to our vocation, at times without realising it? would add, Let them see that we are making some return to them for all the care, concern and sacrifice they have spent on us.17

Continuity in apostolic work We cannot do without letters if we are to keep up a friendship because they are a bond that brings people closer together. That is why they are important in the apostolate. This contact through letters becomes more important, and may even become indispensable, in the work of St Raphael when for one reason or another the St Raphael people are separated from us for a time. Our Father has written: As I have often reminded you, my dearest daughters and sons, our work of St Raphael is a haven of generous effort and of peace, even in the midst of the greatest national and international conflicts.18 He would add: It is the seed-bed of Opus Dei. It is the ordinary means on which the grace of God counts to prepare vocations. To neglect it would be to tempt God, obliging him to grant extraordinary graces.19 Our Founder laid a lot of stress on this work which is fundamental in the apostolate of the Work. We should take great care in seeing that it is done exactly as God wants and that we spend the best of our time and energies on it. Our Father goes on to say: You will do proselytism in a special way with the St Raphael people who will be the favoured object of your care and your zeal, a care and

16 From our Father, Noticias January 1958, p.16 17 From our Father, Crónica, July 1960, p.12 18 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 19 Ibid.

The letter-writing apostolate 223

zeal that I ask God to increase in all of you from day to day. For in this way, with supernatural outlook, your apostolic zeal will grow in extension without losing intensity, employing as its instrument those very same souls to whom you give formation.20 This dedication to the work of St Raphael, varying according to circumstances, which our Father asks of all his children, demands effort, prayer, and personal sacrifice. It also requires continuity so as not to lose the fruit of a whole year’s work in a few days because of lack of care. I have often told you, my daughters and sons, our Founder goes on to remind us, that there are no vacations in the apostolate. The work of St Raphael goes on continuously all the year round. Of course, the work done with students during the vacations is different from that done during the academic year. But there is no break.21 There are always times in which it is more difficult to keep in contact with the St Raphael people, whether they be students or not. There is a time which can be very dangerous for young people: the summer months, which almost always mean separation from most of the companions who attend the means of formation with them. That is why we have to ensure by the ordinary means that the summer does not bring a complete break in their contact with us,22 so that the St Raphael people may keep up the strength of their interior life while they are away. Our Father used to insist: There is a great enemy to the perseverance of our youngsters, namely, the summer. Warn them about it before they leave: heaven is promised only to the one who perseveres to the end, qui ... perseveraverit usque in finem, hic salvus erit (Matt 10:22).23 The same words could be applied to a separation that is due to other reasons: a change of residence or place of work (whether of the St

20 Ibid. 21 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 22 Ibid. 23 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 224 The apostolic mission
##
Raphael fellow or of the member of the Work who is looking after him), journeys, etc. In all these cases the continuity of our relations with the young people must not be broken. You must help them to be faithful to the norms of piety they have begun to make part of their life, and to do some apostolate in the place where they spend the vacation. You have to help them to make good use of their time, by improving their cultural formation, studying some language, and resting, too. I have always told the youngsters that rest, which does not mean doing nothing but rather changing one’s activity, is important, and I have even wanted it to be a point in the examination of conscience in their monthly day of recollection.24 We must make certain that the relationship the St Raphael people have with the Work is not broken off during this period. The way of ensuring this will differ according to their personal circumstances and the reason for the separation. If they have gone to another city or to another country for good, the best thing may be to put them into contact with some centre or activity of the Work near the place where they now live. If the separation is caused by a member of the Work having to move, the way of keeping the people in touch will be different. Our Father tells us that because of the demands of their formation and the needs of the Work, the Numeraries may have to move from one city to another during their studies, even in many cases more than once, though we try not to move them. But this should not be a reason for breaking with the St Raphael people they were looking after. To avoid this, we have to ensure that they are really in the work of St Raphael and are close not only to their friend, but to a Centre and to several people in that Centre.25 When the separation is only temporary due to the vacations, leave

24 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 25 Ibid.

The letter-writing apostolate 225

of absence from their work, etc, you can organise trips which give you the chance to see them so that they do not lose touch with the Centre they were going to during the academic year. Or you can arrange to meet some of them in some place that is more or less at the same distance for all of you. When a number of them are spending their summer in places that are close together, each should be concerned to keep in touch with the others. In any case, it is always possible to write to them often, or to get the more mature among their friends to help you by writing to them.26 This is where the “letter-writing apostolate” our Father taught us to practise in the work of St Raphael becomes especially important. As he wrote in 1935: Do not forget that in each Centre you must keep up a correspondence with the people during the vacations, and insist that they write once a month.27 Our Father used to do this at that time himself. How much “letter-writing apostolate” have I done with my St Raphael people when I did not have them close by! At times, it would be three or four letters in a row before I got an answer. If I could not write to them at length, I would write them a couple of lines: something that would awaken them, encourage them, and also provide a reminder of their resolutions.28 Besides these personal, intimate letters, our Father used other means to keep alive this contact with people who were away. Many years ago he wrote: We have to send all of them a summary of the letters received, a news-sheet accompanied with a few personal lines for each one.29 These were periodic bulletins through which every one received the family warmth they needed to keep them united to the work of St Raphael. It helped them to keep alive their interior life and

26 Ibid. 27 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 28 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942 29 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935 226 The apostolic mission

their desire to do apostolate. Our Father’s example should encourage us never to neglect this letter-writing apostolate in the work of St Raphael. Its effect is not easily measured, but it is always important. It gives an impulse to the interior life of these young people, fosters their desire to do apostolate, and prepares them to receive a calling from God to the Work and to respond generously to that vocation. ‘Before their letter arrived I had been feeling low in spirits – for no particular reason – and I was immensely cheered as I read it and saw what the others are doing.’ And another: ‘Your letters and the news of my brothers helped me like a happy dream in the midst of the reality around us!’ ... And another: ‘It is so wonderful to receive those letters and to realise I am a friend of such friends!’ And another, and a thousand others: ‘I had a letter from “X” and was ashamed to think of my lack of spirit compared with his.’ Now do you not agree that the ‘letter-writing apostolate’ is 30 effective? The work of St Raphael deserves our greatest attention. No effort or sacrifice is too great when compared with the importance of maintaining its continuity. My daughters and sons, may all of you, from the youngest to those who are not so young any more, always have an active concern for our work of St Raphael. We must, I repeat, look at it with a special love. It must be the apple of our eye.31

30 The Way, 977 31 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942

## MAY ROADS {#cuaderno.5.22}

If we look at the world, at the People of God, (cf 1 Pet 2:10), at the start of this month of May, we will see devotion to Our Lady taking the form of many old and new customs practised with great love. It makes me very happy to see that this devotion is always alive, awakening in Christians a supernatural desire to act as domestici fidei, as members of God’s household (Eph 2:19). Seeing how so many Christians are expressing their affection for the Virgin Mary during these days in a thousand different ways, surely you also feel more a part of the Church, closer to those brothers and sisters of yours.1 There are many manifestations of devotion to Mary that brighten up the month of May. They are the fruit of the love that Christians feel for the Mother of God. By honouring the Virgin Mary we are honouring her Son, for “Mary has always been the path that leads to Christ. Every encounter with her can only result in an encounter with Christ himself. For what other reason do we continually turn to Mary than to seek for the Christ she holds in her arms, to seek in her, through her and with her, the Saviour to whom men feel the ever recurring need of turning. In the difficulties and dangers of life here below, they turn to her as to a haven of safety and an all surpassing source of life.”2

1 Christ is passing by, 139 2 Blessed Paul VI, Encyclical Mense Maio, 29 April 1965 228 The apostolic mission

We too are children of Mary. We love her with our whole soul. Together with so many other Christians we try to show a more tender affection for our Mother during this month of May. We try to renew this resolution every day, expressing it in the effort to fulfil the Norms very well, especially our marian Norms. Each of us on our own according to our personal situation, tries to offer Our Lady some small gift that expresses the real depth of our piety. How does a normal son or daughter treat his mother? In different ways, of course, but always affectionately and confidently, never coldly. In an intimate way, through small, common-place customs. A mother feels hurt if we omit them: a kiss or an embrace when leaving or coming home, a little gift, a few warm words.3

Marian pilgrimages Together with these personal devotions, the month of May offers us another opportunity to show our love for Mary through the customary pilgrimage. From very early times it has been a Christian custom to visit a sanctuary, a famous shrine, or an especially venerated image of Our Lady on a particular feastday. These are joyous occasions of prayer, sacrifice, and self-giving. In the Work we also feel the need to go on pilgrimage to Our Lady, but without having to make an external and public display of penance or devotion. It is simply a case of filial affection that seeks expression in the most straightforward and normal action suggested by love, without calling people’s attention: a visit to our Mother, in the city or out in the country, to speak with her and to tell her once more that we love her. Our Father has spoken to us about the birth of this marian Custom,

3 Christ is passing by, 142

May roads 229

which began during a visit to a shrine in Spain, the shrine of Our Lady of Sonsoles. It was not a pilgrimage in the normal sense: nothing noisy or elaborate, just three of us. I respect and love public demonstrations of devotion, but I must admit I prefer to offer Mary the same affection, and the same enthusiasm, in private visits or with very few people – a more intimate sort of thing.4 A pilgrimage is a sign of our love for Our Lady who has taken such good care of the Work and guided us from the beginning. Since then, during many visits to shrines of Our Lady, I have often reflected and meditated on the wonderful affection which so many Christians have for the Mother of Jesus. I have always seen it as a response of love, of filial love and thanksgiving to Our Lady, a sign of a child’s affection. For Mary is closely involved with the greatest sign of God’s love: the Word made flesh who became man like us and took upon himself our sins and weaknesses. Faithful to the divine purpose for which she was born, Mary continues to spend herself in the service of men, who are all called to be brothers of her son Jesus. The Mother of God is also truly the mother of men.5 Our pilgrimage preserves intact the features that have characterised this custom throughout the centuries. It is a visit to Our Lady filled with a spirit of prayer and mortification.

Praying the Rosary During the pilgrimage we pray the fifteen mysteries of the Rosary and the Litany of Our Lady. They channel our contemplative spirit. The joys, sorrows, and glories of Mary’s life weave a crown of praise that is constantly echoed by the angels and the blessed in heaven. The

4 Christ is passing by, 139 5 Ibid., 140 mysteries that we contemplate in the Rosary are the central points of that history of mercy and salvation that God Our Lord, by means of his incarnation, life, death, resurrection and glorification, wished to realise on earth. Hence, the Rosary is a living synthesis of the Catholic faith that we love so much. It offers nourishment for our piety and a fitting occasion for us to go deeper in a personal way into the human and divine life of the Son of God who has given himself to us and makes himself available to us through his Mother.

feel attracted to her, to be in her company as her loving children, we do not need to go into the theological theory, even though it is an inexhaustible mystery that she is Mother of God.

calls us his friends; his grace acts in us, freeing us from sin, enabling us to reflect in some way the features of Christ, even though we are still wretched dirt. We are not merely stranded people whom God has promised to save. His salvation is already at work in us. In our relationship to God, we are not blind people yearning for light and crying in anguished darkness. We are children who know that our Father loves us.

name goes straight to our heart. Our relationship with our own mother may show us how to deal with Mary, the Lady of the Sweet Name. We have to love God with the same heart with which we love our parents, our brothers and sisters, the other members of our family, our friends. And we must love Mary with that same heart, too.6

words, just like people in love.7 The pilgrimage is filled with intimate

6 Christ is passing by, 142 7 Ibid.

The apostolic mission

If we want to understand Mary’s role in the Christian’s life and to The Catholic faith sees Mary as a sign of God’s special love. God Mary tells us about this warmth and security. That is why her In praying the Rosary, the soul never gets tired of repeating its May roads 231

conversation with our Lady. Every Hail Mary, every greeting of Our Lady, is a new beat of a loving heart. But we must try to pray well, considering those prayers that the Archangel Gabriel addressed to Mary on behalf of God and the glowing words with which Elizabeth welcomed Our Lady, under the inspiration of the Holy Spirit. In this way our pilgrimage will be filled with God. It will really be an eloquent manifestation of our contemplative life which seeks everywhere and in every way to maintain a constant dialogue with God.

With a penitent and apostolic spirit We also try to live a spirit of penance on the pilgrimage. There are little mortifications that we offer gladly to Our Lady for the intentions of the Father, for the needs of the Church, and for the vocations of our friends. We will make the whole pilgrimage, or at least the last part of the journey, on foot. We will cheerfully accept the slight discomforts of the way or the inclement weather. We will not take the refreshments or snack that would be normal on a stroll or outing. These are little things that really show the spirit of penance that inspires us, the spirit recommended by Our Lady so insistently at Fatima and Lourdes and so praised by the Church.
