Together with a spirit of prayer and penance there is a third characteristic: apostolic zeal. Our vocation leads us to see souls in all the persons around us: souls that must be saved, souls that we must bring closer to God Our Lord. We cannot turn our backs on others, ignoring them, because we are caught up in our own little world. That was not how Jesus lived. The Gospel often speaks of his mercy, his ability to feel the sorrow and share the needs of others. He consoled the widow of Naim (cf Luke 7:11-17); he wept at the death of Lazarus (cf John 11:35); he felt compassion for the crowds that followed him with nothing to eat (cf Matt 15:32); he also had pity on sinners, on 232 The apostolic mission

those who go through life without knowing light or truth. And when he landed, Jesus saw a large crowd, and had compassion on them, because they were like sheep without a shepherd. And he began to teach them many things (Mark 6:34). When we are truly sons of Mary, we understand this attitude of Our Lord, and our heart expands and becomes tender. We feel the sufferings, doubts, loneliness and sorrow of all other men, our brothers. And we urgently want to help them and speak to them about God, so that they can treat him as their Father and understand the motherly care which Mary is offering them.8 On our way to visit the shrine of Sonsoles, which I mentioned earlier, we passed some wheat fields. The wheat shone as it waved in the breeze, and I remembered a part of the Gospel where Jesus said to his disciples: Do you not say, ‘There are yet four months and then comes the harvest’? I tell you: lift up your eyes and behold that the fields are already white for the harvest (John 4:35). And I realised again that our Lord wanted to put the same yearning into our hearts as he had in his own. And I left the road to pluck some ears of grain to keep as souvenirs.9 Often, recalling those fields of Castille close to Sonsoles, our Father spoke to us about those big ears of wheat filled with grain that bent the stalks with their weight, as if they were asking for the reapers to come. Our hearts then filled with a burning zeal for proselytism. One day, years ago, our Father commented: This morning I was reading some old verses written about the year 1200, when the romance languages were beginning to develop. I was reading the songs of the reapers. Those who gathered in the harvest from the fields were singing: “This indeed is the harvest of life, this indeed is the harvest

8 Christ is passing by, 146 9 Ibid.

May roads 233

of richness”. My children, when we think of the hunger for truth that there is in the world; of the nobility of so many hearts which lack light; of my weakness and yours, that of so many of us who have reason to be dazzled by Christ’s light; when we feel the need to sow the light of Christ for there to be a harvest of life, a harvest of richness; we will remember something that we have often meditated on that Christ walked in hunger along the roads of Palestine. At that time, St Matthew writes, Jesus was walking through the wheat fields on the Sabbath day. And his disciples, who were hungry, fell to plucking the ears of wheat and eating them (Matt 12:1). They too, like us now, thought of the need to spread the Good News as they walked through the wheat field, rubbing those rich ears in their hands and devouring the grain in their hunger. Messis quidem multa. The harvest, the multitude of men who were alive then and of those who were to come after, was great. Messis quidem multa, operarii autem pauci (Matt 9:37). The harvest is great but the labourers are few. Is that not what I often tell you, in a thousand different ways, when we sit down to consider the needs of the Work in this region or in that, when there are difficulties or the almost physical impossibility of going to a new country or beginning a new enterprise because, even though we be many, we still need more vocations? I tell you then that we must let time pass, that we have to go to God: rogate ergo Dominum messis, ut mittat operarios in messem suam (Matt 9:38), pray therefore the Lord of the harvest to send forth labourers into his harvest.10

10 From our Father, Meditation, 26 March 1964 234 The apostolic mission

An ally of proselytism One of the principal characteristics of the pilgrimage is that we take along our friends. We go with those whom we are trying to bring closer to God, so that they get to know our Mother better and fall more in love with her, and so that the Blessed Virgin take them into her care, under her mantle, and obtain a vocation for them. We have to yearn not to be alone. We have to encourage others to help in this divine task of bringing joy and peace to men’s hearts. As St Gregory the Great says: “Insofar as you progress, attract others to go along with you, desire to have companions on the road to the Lord (Homiliae in Evangeliae 6, 6)”.11 The pilgrimage is an excellent occasion to get fully inside the lives of the others, to open horizons for them, and to awaken in those souls that are ready the divine calling to sanctify their ordinary life. Many noble decisions can arise on a visit to Our Lady in the month of May. God, his Church and his Work need this to spread the light of Christ to all nations, to make God’s truth shine forth amid the darkness of uncertainty and ignorance that envelops so many souls. We have been sent to put this truth within reach of all with our word and with the example of our life. If we act in this way, we give those around us the example of a simple and normal life which is consistent, even though it has all the limitations and defects which are part and parcel of the human condition. When they see that we live the same life as they do, they will ask us: Why are you so happy? How do you manage to overcome selfishness and comfort-seeking? Who has taught you to understand others, to live well and to spend yourself in the service of others? Then we must disclose to them the divine secret of Christian existence. We must speak to them about God, Christ, the Holy Spirit, Mary. The time has come for us to use our poor words to communicate

11 Christ is passing by, 147

May roads 235

the depth of God’s love which grace has poured into our souls.12 We must be daring in order to invite on the pilgrimage difficult people, those stubborn friends of ours, or those who are not very pious. We must know how to open up for them the human and divine meaning of this Christian custom. Be daring, our Father insists. Count on the help of Mary, Queen of Apostles. Without ceasing to be a Mother, Our Lady is able to get each of her children to face their own responsibilities. Mary always does the immense favour of bringing to the cross, of placing face to face with the example of the Son of God, those who come close to her and contemplate her life. It is in this confrontation that Christian life is decided. And here Mary intercedes for us so that our behaviour may lead to a reconciliation of the younger brother, you and me, with the firstborn Son of the Father.13 We know from wonderful experiences, clear manifestations of the saving will of God, that when we honour his Mother, he never fails to give himself to souls and to shower his grace upon them. For many of our friends the pilgrimage has been not only the result or crowning of a process of improvement begun some time before, but also the first step on a path and the start of a journey that takes them to God: a real conversion. So we have to awaken those people who have fallen into the dangerous sleep Our Lord mentioned. We must remind them that life is not something to play with. It is a divine treasure which must bear fruit.14 Concern for proselytism will lead us to make the pilgrimage with those friends who could do more and do not. They could live all the consequences of their vocation as children of God, but refuse to do so through lack of generosity ... We must also show the way to those who
##
12 Christ is passing by, 148 13 Christ is passing by, 149 14 Ibid., 147 236 The apostolic mission

have good will and good desires, but do not know how to put them into practise. Christ urges us. Each one of us has to be not only an apostle, but an apostle of apostles, bringing others along, so that they in turn will encourage others to make Jesus Christ known to everyone.15 We will not lack Our Lady’s help. If we do all we can on our part, if we give ourselves passionately to the mission that her Son has entrusted to us, if we season apostolate and proselytism with the indispensable ingredients of prayer and mortification, God will give many souls the grace of a vocation to Opus Dei, and many others will begin to walk the roads of faith, hope and love. In his Gospel, St John narrates a wonderful scene: the wedding feast at Cana, where Our Blessed Lady showed her motherly affection and intercession for mankind. After asking her Son for a miracle of mercy, she advised the servants: Do whatever he tells you.16 This is what made that conversion of water into wine possible. That is what it is all about

– getting people to face Jesus and ask him: Domine, quid me vis 17 facere? (Acts 9:6), Lord, what do you want me to do? That is why we try to take a friend along on the pilgrimage, precisely so that this marian custom be apostolic in intent and in practise. If possible we will make several visits to our Mother so that we may put more souls under her special protection. For we have seen and experienced it so often, we know well that many conversions, many decisions to give oneself to the service of God have been preceded by an encounter with Mary. Our Lady has encouraged us to look for God, to desire to change, to lead a new life. So the Do whatever he tells you has turned into real self-giving, into a Christian vocation, which from then on enlightens all our personal life.18

15 Ibid. 16 Jn 2:5 17 Christ is passing by, 149 18 Ibid.

May roads 237

The Lord would not want us to miss the opportunity the month of May offers us to grow in his love through an affectionate relationship with his Mother. Let us try each day to show her in little things and in tender attention that we her children love her, and that our holiness and apostolate are becoming a reality, since we are making a constant effort to assist with the salvation that Christ has brought to the world. Sancta Maria, spes nostra, ancilla Domini, sedes Sapientiae, ora pro nobis! Holy Mary, our hope, handmaid of the Lord, seat of 19 Wisdom, pray for us!

19 Ibid.

## LIVING FAITH {#cuaderno.5.23}

Returning to Jerusalem after a night spent in Bethany, the Apostles noticed the fig tree cursed by Jesus the previous afternoon. Master, look! The fig tree which you cursed has withered.1 Our Father comments: The first Twelve, who had seen Christ work so many miracles, were completely astonished once again. Their faith was not yet a burning faith.2 The Gospel narrative is full of passages where Our Lord asks faith of those who would come close to him.3 Whether he is performing miracles and cures or engaging the Pharisees in controversy, Jesus drives home this requirement for the life and mission of the Apostles. Once they asked him, What must we do to be doing the works of God? Jesus answered them: this is the work of God, that you believe in him whom he has sent.4 Faith is the first principle upon which every supernatural work is based. St Augustine says: “Ground all of your works in faith, for the just man lives by faith and faith acts through love. May your works be based on faith; believing in God will make you faithful.”5 Intellectual adherence to the truths which God has revealed to us is not the whole

1 Mk 11:21 2 Friends of God, 203 3 Cf Mt 6:30; 9:22; 14:31; 15:28; Mk 5:34; 10:52; Lk 7:50; 8:25; 17:19; 18:42 4 Jn 6:28-29 5 St Augustine, Enarrationes in Psalmos, 32, 4

Living faith 239

of faith. We are to believe sincerely in God, in his omnipotence, in his saving will as manifested in the supreme act of the Incarnation and in the Passion and Death of Christ, in the coming of the Holy Spirit, and in the mission of the Church. But precisely because it is all this, faith is full of practical consequences. It is reflected in our conduct. It informs our whole life down to the last detail. We see things in the light of faith and act accordingly. This is the faith Our Lord asks of us: a living faith, full of supernatural fruit. The seed sown on good soil, this is he who hears the word and understands it; he indeed bears fruit, and yields, in one case a hundredfold, in another sixty, and in another thirty.6 After Our Lord’s Ascension into heaven the Apostles themselves, guided and strengthened by the Holy Spirit, will show us the secret of their activity and effectiveness by word and example. In dealing with the incredulity of the pagans, the blindness of the Jews and sometimes, the frailty of the first communities, it is this faith that gives them the sure strength to overcome contradictions, obstacles, misunderstanding, and deadly persecution. This is the faith which makes Peter and John cry out when threatened by the Sanhedrin: we cannot but speak of what we have seen and heard.7 And the reason is What does it profit, my brethren, if a man says he has faith but has not works? Can his faith 8 save him? “Men can become apostates in two ways. They may separate themselves from their Creator either in regard to faith or in regard to works. Just as one who leaves the faith is an apostate, so whoever returns to the evil deeds which he had forsaken, will without doubt be considered by God an apostate even if he seems to keep the faith. For one without

6 Mt 9:22 7 Acts 4:20 8 Jas 2:14 240 The apostolic mission

the other can have no value. Faith is no good without works, and similarly works without faith are useless, unless they are performed in order to obtain faith, as in the case of Cornelius, who before coming to believe deserved to be heard because of his good works.”9 We too, in order to be faithful to the vocation we have received, must lead a life of faith. First comes faith in God: “Confide wholly in God, entrust yourself to him, cast all your care upon his providence and he will sustain you so that you will be able to say with confidence: The Lord is concerned for me10.”11Next is faith in the Work which has come to fulfil his will: We are not souls who have joined with other souls in order to do a good thing. That is a lot – and yet it is little. We are apostles who fulfil an imperative command of Christ.12 And finally comes faith in our personal vocation because as our Father reminds us, before he created the world, God was already thinking about me, about us, about each one of us, with a love of predilection. This is because he is a Father, and because he is our Brother, and because he is the Spirit who dwells in my spirit, making it capable of performing human works and divine ones as well.13 We manifest this faith in the firmness of our struggle to reach sanctity and to carry out the apostolate.

Superiority complex Vocation and mission are inseparably united. Along with our vocation God has given us our own apostolic spirit and method which our Father has “carved out” for us and which, by means of our formation, continues to be shown to us just as God has wanted it to be. We have to

9 St Gregory the Great, In Ezechielem homiliae, 1, 9, 6 10 Ps 40:17 11 St Bernard, In vigilia Nativitatis sermo 5, 5 12 From our Father, Instruction, 19 March 1934 13 From our Father, Meditation Dying and Reigning, 29 October 1967, in While he spoke to us on the way, p. 301

Living faith 241

accept this spirit and this apostolic method with faith because faith is what will enable us to see them as divine means capable of giving supernatural fruit. Having those means to hand, together with a divine promise of effectiveness, we are made to feel that alongside our personal weakness there is a superhuman, supernatural strength, infinitely superior to all the powers of this earth. You must have a superiority complex, our Father tells us, because God has given you intelligence, the means and a vocation. You must always react as St Paul says: Omnia possum in eo qui me confortat! (Phil 4:13) I can do all things in him who strengthens me. You must put Christ into the hearts of others. Not you alone! For you have a vocation to the Work and you have the supernatural means, so you can always say, Omnia possum in eo qui me confortat. With grace I can do everything. This superiority complex makes us supernaturally daring in apostolate; we know that the world is good because it has come from God’s hands. We know, too, that all honest professions can and must be sanctified. If you are supernatural, the persons you are dealing with have the requirements to receive the vocation to Opus Dei. And so, my children, none of you has the right to say: I cannot do apostolate, I cannot do proselytism.14 Neither the magnitude of the mission to which we are called, nor the consideration of our small personal worth, nor the obstacles which may arise along the way are sufficient reason to stop or delay our apostolic work, as it is not based simply on human considerations. Discouragement is possible because the difficulties are sometimes great. To overcome them prudence is necessary, together with a resolutely daring attitude. This is no merely human prudence, which would often consider it rash to advance. It is supernatural prudence,

14 From our Father 242 The apostolic mission

informed by faith, which indicates the way to us, and reminds us of our vocation. It makes us realise that we are instruments in the hands of God, that we are doing his apostolate, and that God takes our defects into account and supplies us with sure means, pointing out the precise way for us to launch out with daring. I have often meditated on Peter’s response: In verbo autem tuo laxabo rete (Luke 5:5). It conveys a sense of complete confidence in Jesus Christ. Because you say so, because you want it, I will do this and anything else you ask me to do. I will do it confidently and fearlessly. I will speak, I will strive hard in whatever is necessary. With Our Lord there is no possibility of fear, nor of human respect. He comes above all else, always and in every circumstance.15 With this certainty that faith in Our Lord gives us, faith in his name and in his word, this knowledge that heaven is bent on the fulfilment16 of the Work of God, we will approach our apostolate with an attitude of dominion. The reason is that God wants it and that the Lord of all things makes us sharers in his own dominion by giving us our vocation: it is our life of faith that calls for this share for us. You are my son, today I have begotten you. Ask of me, and I will make the nations your heritage.17

Growing in spite of the difficulties Faith joins us to Christ the Redeemer and to his power over all creatures. For in Christ Jesus you are all sons of God through faith, St Paul reminds us, for as many of you as were baptised into Christ have put on Christ.18 Faith must be operative; it must move us to try and act

15 From our Father 16 From our Father, Instruction, 19 March 1934 17 Ps 2:7-8 18 Gal 3:26-27

Living faith 243

uprightly, certain that for all who are led by the Spirit of God are sons of God.19 With this spirit of divine filiation which our Father has taught us to practise, we will not be frightened by difficulties; we will feel protected by our Father God even when things are toughest. Should there be obstacles, misunderstandings, pitfalls, which Satan wills and the Lord permits, we must have faith and works, faith and sacrifice, faith and humility. My son, whoever has faith knows how to judge earthly things well. He is objective in considering his vocation. He knows that life here below is, in a phrase of Saint Teresa, a bad night in a bad inn. He doesn’t forget that this is tempus purgatorii ad solvenda iustitiae divinae debita, a time of purgatory to settle the debt due to divine justice.20 At other times the difficulties may arise from our lack of faith, as when we rate too highly the particular circumstances of our environment, or when we give too much importance to considerations arising from human prudence, or when we lack a right intention. “There is nothing, no matter how easy, that our lukewarmness does not present to us as difficult and burdensome. Nor is there anything so difficult and burdensome that it is not made perfectly easy and bearable by our fervour and determination. Tell me, please, what could be more difficult than to suffer the danger of death daily? Yet, the Apostle Paul called this a thing of little importance when he said: For this momentary affliction is preparing for us an eternal weight of glory.21 For if it is true that this could be burdensome for nature, then hope for the future would make it light. This is the reason the Apostle himself gave, saying: We look not to the

19 Rom 8:14 20 From our Father, Meditation, 12 October 1947, in Crónica, July 1963, pp.57-58 21 2 Cor 4:17 244 The apostolic mission

things that are seen but to the things that are unseen22.”23 When we analyse the means and the possibilities for our apostolic work we must count on the supernatural ones above all. My sons, the Work has advanced through prayer and mortification. In the beginning we could not rely on any human means. All we had was youthfulness, joy, determination, and a perfect certainty, in so far as human frailty permitted, that we were doing God’s will. And all that, anointed with a great deal of prayer and penance.24 This superiority complex, based on faith, which bears the fruit of daring and certainty, is also shown in our determination to hold firmly to the mandate we have received from God by being faithful to that spirit and apostolic method which have been pointed out to us. We can count on everything that we need in order to be effective. But we will not be effective unless we use these means, precisely these and no others. Our Lord would withdraw his grace, even should we be trying to accomplish something good, because we would no longer be doing Opus Dei on earth; we would not be responding to our calling. God has a special right over us, his children: the right of our correspondence to his love, in spite of our personal faults. This conviction imposes a responsibility on us which we cannot evade, but which at the same time gives us a complete assurance: we are instruments in the hands of God and he counts on us each day. For this reason we will strive to do Opus Dei each day.25 That is the only way we will be effective; that is the only way we will come to be alter Christus, ipse Christus, and consequently co-redeemers with him. If the results are sometimes slow in coming, our attitude has to be one of faith and humility. Faith keeps us from questioning the efficacy

22 2 Cor 4:18 23 St John Chrysostom, De compunctione 1, 5 24 From our Father, Crónica, November 1955, p.4 25 From our Father, Crónica, 1968, p.1065

Living faith 245

of these means, knowing that in the Lord your labour is not in vain,26 St Paul tells us. And after relating the difficulties which kept him from preaching at Troas, he continued: But thanks be to God, who in Christ always leads us in triumph, and through us spreads the fragrance of the knowledge of him everywhere.27 We need humility, too, in order to examine whether we have made good use of all the means, correcting whatever we have done badly and not giving in to the temptation to use other means which suit our wishes better, or to adjust the apostolic method of the Work, thinking that what we have at hand is fine but that the given situation or environment needs other measures, certain concessions. Do not let yourselves be fooled, our Father forewarns us, by false apostolic tactics, for you will encounter people who are blinded even by the very ideal of winning souls. On the pretext of seeking the lost sheep they will end up falling into the quicksands of the error they seek to combat, deceived by compromise, concession, or imprudent accommodation.28

Daring We have to get into people’s lives without hesitating, not like the timid person who feels obliged to excuse himself. We have to enter as into something that pertains to us, for Our Lord has given it to us by sending us out to work so that we might bear fruit. Wherever we happen to be we have to proclaim the truth, not holding back on account of the environment. We must be convinced that with the authenticity of our lives and of our words, and the grace of God which will not be lacking, we will succeed in transforming souls.

26 1 Cor 15:58 27 2 Cor 2:14 28 From our Father, Letter 16 July 1933 246 The apostolic mission

This attitude requires self-sacrifice and renunciation of personal aims. Some people neither attack the faith nor defend it. They have slipped into a comfortable and self-centred scepticism which, under the guise of respect for the opinions of others, takes refuge in indecision and irresponsibility. Their attitude is well reflected in the verses which somebody wrote in jest (if he wrote them in earnest, we would have to conclude that he understood the Gospel as poorly as he did literary style): In this hostile world / there is nobody you can trust. / It is every man for himself, I for myself / you for yourself / and let him save himself who can.29 Whenever it is necessary we must also break through the other person’s screen of selfishness by fearlessly entering into his life, just as others have done in our life. “This is why our Father tells us: Be daring! For in order to lead souls to God we have to understand and forgive them, get close to them, overlook things. But we also have to tell them the truth and so decrease their pride, their worldliness, their obscurity. To succeed in this, it is especially necessary for us to be daring, for if it is hard to leave one’s shell, one’s ivory tower, how much harder it is to tell the truth, veritatem facientes in caritate (Eph 4:15), ignoring the consideration that in human ears telling the truth often creates hatred: Veritas parit odium (truth makes way for hatred), as the pagans used to say. For us, children of God in the Work, the truth cannot but produce supernatural fruit.”30 It is not for us to cut out, change or yield in anything that belongs to the spirit and organisation of the Work of God.31 Whoever was not ready to pass on the spirit of our Father in all its integrity and in the way he has shown us, would not fit in Opus Dei, he would only get in

29 Ibid. 30 Don Alvaro, note 140 to the Instruction, 8 December 1941 31 From our Father, Instruction, 19 March 1934

Living faith 247

the way. If for any reason, no matter how noble the intention, we should try to adapt to situations or movements foreign to what Our Lord wants us to do, and foreign to the spirit and apostolic method proper to the Work, we would not be effective and there would be no vocations. Should we present an imperfect image of what the Work is to those with whom we are dealing, their logical response would be to prefer instead whatever it was we had tried to accommodate or compare the Work to. Moreover, if vocations did come, they would be false vocations, attracted to a life that is not ours. On the contrary, our life of faith, our authenticity, is what will attract the souls God has placed at our side. St Paul wrote: When I came to you, brethren, I did not come proclaiming to you the testimony of God in lofty words or wisdom. For I decided to know nothing among you except Jesus Christ and him crucified.32

In God’s hands Omnia possum in eo qui me confortat (Phil 4:13). With him there is no possibility of failure. Hence the superiority complex; hence we can take on tasks with a spirit of victory, because God grants us his strength. An inferiority complex? Why? I see no reason for it. How can you have an inferiority complex in Opus Dei? You have to have a superiority complex! But Father, wouldn’t that be a sign of pride? No, my children! It is a consequence of humility, of a humility that makes me say: Lord, you are he who is. I am nothing. You have all the perfections: power, strength, love, glory, wisdom, dominion, honour. If I get close to you like a child in the strong arms of his father or in his mother’s wonderful lap, I will feel the warmth of your divinity, I

32 1 Cor 2:1-2 248 The apostolic mission

will feel the light of your wisdom, I will feel your strength running through my veins.33 The superiority complex our Founder asks of us in our apostolic activities corresponds to a profound personal humility. It comes from knowing that our effectiveness derives from God and not from ourselves. It comes from renouncing human wisdom in order to preach only what we have received from God. It comes from overcoming our selfseeking in order to pursue the interests of Christ. It comes from being steadfast in all that is not ours, because it is God’s, and from giving way in all that is ours. It is the opposite of the attitude of those whose false humility and hypocritical mea-culpism does not keep them from presenting the Holy face of the Church as full of wrinkles or of making the Gospel fit the tastes of their audience so as to win their approval more easily. The touchstone of true humility is the uprightness of the intention which motivates us not to serve or rely on ourselves but on God. I have a superiority complex because I am in God’s hands, and he is my Father, et adorabunt eum omnes reges terrae; omnes gentes servient ei (Ps 71:9). Even Satan serves him. Even creatures condemned for all eternity do service to God. I have a superiority complex because potestas eius, potestas aeterna, quae non auferetur: et regnum eius, quod non corrumpetur (Dan 7:14). Also I realise, Lord, that you can do everything. I rectify my intention. As a ship at sea corrects its course by looking at a star, so I correct my intention by looking at Mary. And I will be certain of always reaching port. I will mark the reefs and with holy shamelessness I will let them be seen, those things that are at times little snares and at other times flagrant ignorance or hateful excuses; or sometimes,

33 From our Father, Meditation Dying and Reigning, 29 October 1967, in While he spoke to us on the way, p. 300

Living faith 249

manifestations of the impotence of men who cannot stand the fruitfulness which you give to the others.34 The Apostles, full of daring and confidence, scattered throughout the world to preach the Gospel, doing so with the certainty that it would bring them no human success. “Their preaching was new and unexpected. The preaching of Moses and the Prophets was about the earth and the good things of the earth; but that of the Apostles was about the Kingdom of heaven and all that pertains to it ... They did not shrink away from their mission, nor did they waver as did the men of old. Do you say that nothing difficult was asked of them? Don’t you hear them speak of prisons, of being led to execution, of internal strife, of the universal hatred that they would provoke? All this Our Lord had foretold would shortly happen to them. Yes, others he had sent as heralds and messengers of infinite treasures, but to them he prophesied only insufferable evils.”35 Our apostolic ambition must likewise be accompanied by such upright intention that we are ready as Christ was in his Passion and Death to renounce personal success, and the applause, esteem, and approval of men. In this way, through our faith in God, expressed in deeds of faithfulness to the spirit and apostolic method proper to the Work, we will experience the full strength of God in our life. We will undertake our apostolate fully confident of victory; nothing will be able to hold us back. For this is the love of God, that we keep his commandments; and his commandments are not burdensome. For whatever is born of God overcomes the world; and this is the victory that overcomes the world, our faith.36

34 From our Father, Meditation Dying and Reigning, 29 October 1967, in While he spoke to us on the way, p. 304 35 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae, 32, 4 36 1 Jn 5:3-4 ___________________________________________________________________ Apud Collegii Romani Sanctae Crucis – 2015
